NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
I. Executive Summary
1. Overview
1.1. Summary of Regulatory Action
Ionis Pharmaceuticals (Applicant) submitted this new drug application (NDA) for olezarsen 
sodium (tradename Tryngolza), seeking approval of this APOC-III-directed antisense 
oligonucleotide to reduce triglycerides in adults with familial chylomicronemia syndrome (FCS), 
a rare genetic disorder. Patients with FCS have very high concentrations of triglycerides (TG) 
and are at increased risk for acute pancreatitis, a potentially fatal complication of this disease. 
The NDA was reviewed by a multidisciplinary review team. Each discipline recommends 
approval for adult patients with FCS for the reduction of triglycerides and the signatory authority 
for this application concurs with those recommendations.
Substantial evidence of effectiveness for olezarsen in patients with FCS was established using 
data from one adequate and well-controlled trial with confirmatory evidence (CE). A single trial 
in patients 18 years of age and older with FCS showed a large and clinically significant reduction 
in triglycerides that was statistically significant compared to placebo along with a numerical 
decrease in pancreatitis events; all patients were on a background of low-fat diet  and optimized 
triglyceride-lowering therapy. Confirmatory evidence providing strong mechanistic support 
includes the well-established etiology of the disease, the mechanism of action of the therapy, and 
trial results in a related condition in patients with moderate to severe hypertriglyceridemia. 
The available safety data show that olezarsen is safe for its intended use. Common adverse 
reactions included injection site reactions, decreased platelet count, and arthralgia. Laboratory 
test abnormalities included decreases in platelet count and increases in glucose, liver enzymes, 
and low-density lipoprotein cholesterol (LDL-C). These laboratory changes were notable but did 
not lead to clinical adverse events. Elevations in liver enzymes were more frequent in patients 
with moderate hypertriglyceridemia as compared to those patients with FCS. All the identified 
safety risks for olezarsen can be adequately mitigated through labeling and further evaluated 
during routine and enhanced pharmacovigilance.
The NDA includes appropriate preapproval nonclinical studies, and no additional nonclinical 
studies or clinical pharmacology studies will be conducted as postmarketing requirements. We 
are requesting a postmarketing requirement to complete the ongoing trials in patients with severe 
hypertriglyceridemia to evaluate further the potential for drug induced liver injury with 
olezarsen. Enhanced pharmacovigilance will be requested for cases of liver toxicity.
As described in the Benefit/Risk Framework below, we conclude that the reduction in 
triglycerides, a surrogate for the reduction in risk for acute pancreatitis, observed with use of 
olezarsen outweighs the risks when olezarsen is used as recommended in the approved labeling.
Reference ID: 5499859

NDA 218614

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
1.2. Conclusions on Substantial Evidence of 
Effectiveness
Substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) was established with one adequate and well-
controlled clinical investigation and confirmatory evidence.
Familial chylomicronemia syndrome (FCS) is a rare genetic disorder. Individuals have very high 
concentrations of triglycerides (TG) and are at increased risk for acute pancreatitis, a potentially 
life-threatening consequence of this condition. Reduction of serum TG levels in patients with 
severe hypertriglyceridemia (TG ≥500 mg/dL) is considered a surrogate for reduction of the risk 
of acute pancreatitis. Olezarsen is a new molecular entity and would be first-in-class for the 
treatment of FCS, for which there are no FDA-approved therapies.
Primary evidence of olezarsen’s efficacy on TG reduction in adult patients with FCS was 
demonstrated by a single trial, ISIS 678354-CS3. In this 53-week, double-blind, placebo-
controlled, randomized trial of adult subjects with FCS, olezarsen 80 mg as an add-on to 
background TG-lowering therapy decreased TG at Month 6 (average of Weeks 23, 25, and 27) 
by 44.5% (95% confidence interval [CI]: -72, -17; p=0.0015 compared to placebo)1. The 
treatment effect with olezarsen was seen in adult subjects with fasting TG levels ≥880 mg/dL 
despite low-fat diet (≤20 grams fat per day) and optimized lipid lowering therapies and was 
similar across important subgroups. Over the 12-month treatment period, the numerical 
incidence of pancreatitis in subjects treated with olezarsen 80 mg was lower compared with 
placebo (one subject reported 1 event of adjudicated acute pancreatitis (AP) in the olezarsen 80 
mg group compared with 11 events experienced by seven subjects in the placebo group). The 
results of this trial are not typical for the clinical trajectory of FCS and support the efficacy of 
olezarsen in this population. 
The approval decision for olezarsen also relies on confirmatory evidence from a related 
indication from Trial ISIS 678354-CS8. Trial CS8 demonstrated efficacy on TG lowering for this 
same dose, 80 mg administered Q4W as a subcutaneous (SC) injection for 1 year, in adults with 
hypertriglyceridemia (HTG) and atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (established or at 
increased risk for), and/or with severe HTG (sHTG). The results from Trial CS8 are similar to 
those observed in Trial CS3. In Trial CS8, the difference between the olezarsen 80 mg group and 
the placebo group in percent change in fasting TG from baseline to Month 6 (average of Weeks 
25 and 27) was -56.5% (95% CI: -67.4%, -45.6%; p<0.0001)2. No subjects in any treatment 
group had an adjudicated AP event during the treatment period in this trial. At Month 6, placebo-
adjusted mean percent change in fasting non-high-density lipoprotein cholesterol (non-HDL-C) 
levels from baseline for olezarsen 80 mg was -24.5% (95% CI: -37.6, -11.5) and for 
apolipoprotein B (ApoB) was -19.3% (95% CI: -29.8, -8.8)3. This randomized, controlled trial 
1 See Table 11: Primary Analysis: Mean Percent Change in Baseline in Fasting Triglycerides (mg/dL) at Month 6 
(Full Analysis Set), Trial C3 in Section 6.2.2.6.
2 See FDA results from Table 31: Primary Analysis Percent Change in Baseline in Fasting Triglycerides (mg/dL) at 
Month 6 (Full Analysis Set) in Section 6.2.3.6.
3 See Table 33: Secondary Endpoint: Percent Change in Baseline in Fasting Non-high-density-lipoprotein 
Cholesterol (mg/dL) at Months 6 and 12 (Full Analysis Set) and Table 35: Secondary Endpoint: Percent Change in 
Baseline in Fasting Total Apolipoprotein B (mg/dL) at Months 6 and 12 (Full Analysis Set)–Section 6.2.3.6.
Reference ID: 5499859

NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
was highly persuasive, having demonstrated a large treatment effect on TG, a highly statistically 
significant result, and reassuring changes on secondary lipid endpoints.
Typically, two adequate and well-controlled trials are required to achieve the statutory definition 
of substantial evidence. However, in the case of FCS, given the rarity of the condition, the 
seriousness of the disease, the unmet medical need to treat this disease, the persuasiveness of the 
results of Trial ISIS 678354-CS3, and the robustness of the confirmatory evidence in subjects 
with moderate or severe HTG, the Division of Diabetes Lipid Disorders and Obesity concludes 
that the Applicant has provided the SEE required by law [see 21 CFR 314.126(a)(b)] to support 
approval.
Although this application relies primarily on a single, blinded, placebo-controlled trial, the 
results constitute SEE in this serious, rare condition with a well-characterized natural history. 
The application supports approval of olezarsen, administered SC 80 mg monthly, as an adjunct to 
low-fat diet for the treatment of adults with FCS.

2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework
Dimension
Evidence and Uncertainties
Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of 
condition
•
Familial chylomicronemia syndrome (FCS) is a rare 
autosomal recessive condition leading to a deficiency in 
lipoprotein lipase (LPL) enzyme activity from inactivation of 
the LPL gene or genes encoding its co-factors (such as 
apolipoprotein CII).
•
Prevalence of FCS is 1 to 10 in 1,000,000 individuals.
•
Characterized by severely elevated chylomicrons and 
plasma triglycerides.
•
The most serious complication is acute pancreatitis, which 
can be life-threatening.
•
Other clinical manifestations include eruptive cutaneous 
xanthomas, lipemia retinalis, episodic abdominal pain, and 
hepatosplenomegaly.
•
FCS is a rare genetic disorder of lipid metabolism 
characterized by excessive chylomicronemia and severe 
hypertriglyceridemia. 
•
The most serious complication is acute pancreatitis. Other 
severe and debilitating symptoms include abdominal pain, 
fatigue, and depression.
Current 
treatment 
options
•
Restrictive low-fat diet (<20 grams daily) is the mainstay of 
therapy that can lower triglycerides, but adherence is 
difficult.
•
Other triglyceride-lowering agents, such fibrates, niacin, or 
omega-3 fatty acids, are generally not effective in FCS.
•
No approved drug therapies
•
Current management of the disease is severe dietary 
restriction. There is no effective drug therapy, and there 
exists a high unmet need for effective and safe treatment.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Dimension
Evidence and Uncertainties
Conclusions and Reasons
Benefit
•
The efficacy of olezarsen in patients with FCS was 
evaluated in a single phase 3 trial (CS3).
•
Randomized (1:1 ratio to Cohort A [50 mg] or Cohort B [80 
mg]; each cohort was further randomized 2:1 to groups 
receiving either the olezarsen dose for the cohort or a 
matching volume of placebo, respectively), double blind, 
placebo control, 53-week trial of 66 subjects with FCS, 
enriched for history of acute pancreatitis or symptoms 
suggestive of pancreatitis (80%), evaluating olezarsen 50 
or 80 mg administered subcutaneously once every 4 weeks 
versus placebo.
–
Primary endpoint of mean percent change in fasting TG 
levels from baseline to Month 6 for olezarsen 80 mg 
compared with placebo: 33% decrease in TG versus 
11% increase for pooled placebo; placebo-adjusted 
mean percent decrease of 44.5% (p=0.0015; 95% CI: -
72.0, -17.0).
–
Treatment difference persisted over time: placebo 
adjusted percent change in TG levels from baseline 
was -61.4% (95% CI:  -101.9,  -20.8) at Month 12 in the 
olezarsen 80 mg treated group.
–
At Month 6, placebo-adjusted mean percent decreases 
in fasting non-HDL-C levels from baseline for  
olezarsen 80 mg was 25.7% (95% CI: -44.3, -7.1)
–
During the Treatment Period, 1 subject had 1 event of 
adjudicated acute pancreatitis in each of the olezarsen 
treatment groups (total n = 2) compared with 11 events 
by 7 subjects in the placebo group.
–
Approximately 88% or 38/43 subjects in the pooled 
olezarsen treatment group completed the one-year 
trial. Three (14%) subjects in the olezarsen 80 mg 
group compared to 1 (4%) subject in the placebo group 
discontinued study drug. 
•
Olezarsen has demonstrated benefit to patients with FCS 
characterized by lower TG levels which are expected to 
reduce the risk of pancreatitis.  
•
Olezarsen 80 mg was tolerated by the majority of subjects.
•
The triglyceride results were compelling, the reduction in 
acute pancreatitis events was reassuring, and the 
discontinuation rate was acceptable.

Conclusions and Reasons
•
Confirmatory mechanistic evidence from Study CS8, 
randomized (3:1 allocation within 50 mg and 80 mg

olezarsen 80 mg or olezarsen 50 mg once every 4 weeks in 
154 subjects with hypertriglyceridemia (HTG) and

trial. Seven subjects (12%) in the olezarsen 80 mg 
group and 3 subjects (8%) in the placebo group 
discontinued study drug.

NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
10
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Risk and risk 
management
•
In CS3, 43 subjects with FCS were exposed to olezarsen. 
Twenty-two subjects were treated with olezarsen 80 mg 
every 4 weeks with a median exposure of 52 weeks (mean, 
46 weeks).
•
In CS8, 57 subjects were treated with olezarsen 80 mg 
every 4 weeks with a median exposure of 52 weeks (mean, 
48 weeks).
•
Supportive data include CS13, an open-label extension of 
CS3 in 54 subjects with FCS treated with olezarsen 80 mg 
every 4 weeks with a median exposure of 33 weeks (mean, 
35 weeks).
•
Supportive data include CS7, an open-label study in 24 
subjects with FCS previously treated with volanesorsen, 
switched to olezarsen 80 mg every 4 weeks. The median 
duration of exposure was 52 weeks (mean 47 weeks).
•
No data in pediatric patients.
•
Hypersensitivity reactions: In CS3, 2 (9%) subjects in the 
olezarsen 80 mg group discontinued because of 
hypersensitivity or flu-like reactions compared to none in 
placebo. There were no serious hypersensitivity reactions 
in CS3 or CS8 although 3 serious reactions were seen in 
the open-label extension trials. The onset of symptoms 
usually occur within minutes or same day of the injection. 
•
Decreases in Platelet Count: Olezarsen treatment causes 
reductions in platelet counts. Platelet counts remain on 
average 5 to 15% below baseline. The incidence of 
bleeding events was low and similar across treatment 
groups. No olezarsen-treated subject in the FCS trial CS3 
paused, reduced, or discontinued olezarsen dosing due to 
a bleeding AE. No olezarsen-treated subjects, to date, have 
reported a platelet count below 50x109/L.
•
The most concerning risk is serious hypersensitivity 
reactions, including symptoms of bronchospasm, diffuse 
erythema, chills, and myalgias. This risk is clinically 
monitorable by the healthcare provider. Olezarsen should 
be discontinued if serious hypersensitivity reactions occur.
•
The clinical impact of the drug-induced LDL-C and apoB 
increases in the FCS population is uncertain, but likely 
minimal in this population, because despite increases in 
LDL-C, the value remains low (i.e., <70 mg/dL) for most 
study subjects. LDL-C is monitorable and can be mitigated 
with lipid-modifying therapy.  
•
Small increases in liver enzymes occurred but the 
incidence of hepatic events was low and similar across 
treatment groups. This is described in Section 6.1 of the 
prescribing information (PI). A postmarketing requirement 
will assess further this potential liver signal in patients with 
severe hypertriglyceridemia along with enhanced 
pharmacovigilance for liver toxicity in the postmarket setting 
in patients with FCS. 
•
The 80 mg dose of olezarsen, administered 
subcutaneously every month, was reasonably well-
tolerated throughout these trials. Approximately 90% of 
patients with FCS in trial CS3 completed the one year trial.
•
All risks associated with olezarsen can be adequately 
addressed through labeling and no specific monitoring, 
beyond that necessary for the treatment and management 
of FCS, is recommended. Olezarsen is contraindicated in 
patients who have experienced serious hypersensitivity 
reactions with olezarsens. This finding is described in 
sections 4 and 5 of the PI. Common adverse reactions 
included injection site reactions, decreased platelet count, 
and arthralgia. Laboratory test abnormalities included 
decreases in platelet count and increases in glucose, liver 
enzymes, and low-density lipoprotein cholesterol (LDL-C). 
These laboratory changes were notable but did not lead to 
clinical adverse events and resolved with discontinuation of 
the drug.

•
Increases in Liver Enzymes: The incidence of hepatic 
events was low and similar across treatment groups. 
Olezarsen was associated with mean increases in ALT, 
AST, GGT within the first 3 months which stabilized, and 
remained within normal range. Liver enzymes returned 
towards baseline with discontinuation of olezarsen. In some 
subjects, increases in liver enzymes lead to discontinuation 
or interruption of study drug. There were no cases of Hy’s 
Law, an indicator of drug-induced liver injury.
•
Dysglycemia: In trial CS3, average glucose and HbA1c 
values increased slightly more in the olezarsen treatment 
groups than placebo. Using an algorithmic FMQ for 
hyperglycemia consisting of adverse events, new 
antidiabetic medication, and laboratory values, a higher 
proportion of olezarsen-treated subjects without a medical 
history of diabetes at baseline (52%) versus placebo-
treated subjects (35%) fulfilled at least one of the FMQ 
criterion, suggesting a safety signal for worsening glycemic 
control.
•
Injection site reactions (ISRs): ISRs, characterized primarily 
by erythema and discoloration, occur with olezarsen 
treatment. In CS3, ISRs occurred in 3 (14%) subjects in 
both the olezarsen 80 mg and 50 mg groups vs. 2 (9%) 
subjects in placebo group. None of the ISRs were serious, 
no subjects in FCS trials reduced study drug, withdrew from 
the trial, or required treatment of the ISR. 
•
Changes in LDL-C and Apo B: Increases in LDL-C and 
apoB of approximately 50% from baseline were noted in 
the FCS population treated with olezarsen compared to a 
<1% increase in those treated with placebo. In contrast, in 
trial CS8 of subjects with moderate to high levels of 
triglycerides, LDL-C and apoB changes were smaller and 
were decreased in the olezarsen groups as compared to 
placebo. 
•
Immunogenicity: Treatment with olezarsen results in 
development of treatment-emergent anti-drug antibodies in 
approximately 40% of treated subjects. No relationship 
between adverse events, including hypersensitivity events, 
and ADA status was seen in CS3 and CS8.

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
The benefit of TG lowering, with the expectation of a reduction in risk for acute pancreatitis, in 
patients with FCS outweighs the potential risks in this population.
FCS is a rare genetic disorder of lipid metabolism characterized by excessive chylomicronemia 
and severe hypertriglyceridemia. The most serious complication is acute pancreatitis. Common, 
and sometimes debilitating, symptoms include episodic abdominal pain, fatigue, and depression. 
Other clinical manifestations include eruptive cutaneous xanthomas, lipemia retinalis, and 
hepatosplenomegaly. Current management of the disease is severe dietary restriction. There is no 
effective drug therapy, and there exists a high unmet need.
Olezarsen 80 mg once monthly effectively lowers TG levels in patients with FCS. TG lowering 
is considered a surrogate for reduction in the risk of acute pancreatitis. Numerically lower 
incidence of pancreatitis in patients assigned to FCS is supportive of the expected clinical 
benefit.
Trial CS3 was a 53-week, double-blind, placebo-controlled, randomized trial of adult subjects 
with FCS with fasting TG levels ≥880 mg/dL despite very low-fat (≤20 grams per day) diet and 
optimized lipid-lowering therapies. Olezarsen 80 mg administered every 4 weeks (as an add-on 
to background TG-lowering therapy) decreased TG at Month 6 by 44.5% compared to placebo. 
Over the 12-month treatment period, the numerical incidence of pancreatitis in subjects 
randomized to olezarsen 80 mg (one event experienced by one subject) was lower compared with 
the incidence in patients assigned to placebo (11 events experienced by seven subjects). 
Treatment discontinuation at one year was acceptable (14% in the olezarsen group versus 4% in 
the placebo group).
Even considering uncertainty around the magnitude of the benefit on acute pancreatitis based on 
reliance on a surrogate endpoint, the anticipated benefit clearly outweighs the risk in the FCS 
population.
The safety of olezarsen in patients with FCS was established by the clinical program. The most 
concerning risk is serious hypersensitivity reactions, including symptoms of bronchospasm, 
diffuse erythema, chills, and myalgias. This risk is clinically monitorable and may be adequately 
communicated in the Contraindications and Warnings and Precautions section of the prescribing 
information. The risk for drug-induced liver injury was primarily seen in patients with moderate 
hypertriglyceridemia. The risk in patients with FCS is very low but an additional evaluation of 
this potential risk will be assessed further in postmarket requirements.
The clinical impact of the drug-induced LDL-C and apoB increases in the FCS population is 
uncertain but does not outweigh the benefit. Although LDL-C increased by approximately 50% 
and ApoB by approximately 12% compared to placebo, baseline values were very low (mean 
baseline LDL-C was 17 mg/dL in placebo and to 27 mg/dL in the 80 mg arms, while mean 
baseline total ApoB was 59.7 mg/dL and 58.4 mg/dL, respectively), thus the impact of these 
percentage increases is likely minimal. The absolute increase in these parameters was relatively 
low magnitude and remained within the normal range for most subjects. LDL-C is monitorable, 
and elevations can be mitigated with lipid-modifying therapy.  Notably, LDL-C and ApoB 
decreased with olezarsen 80 mg versus placebo in the moderate and severe HTG populations in 
CS8 compared to placebo.
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 31 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
14
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Other adverse events, including decreases in platelet count, increases in liver enzymes, and 
increase in glucose are monitorable and may be communicated in the Adverse Reactions section 
of labeling.
Although the safety database is small, strengths include the placebo-controlled design, use of two 
doses to assess dose-response, the 1-year treatment duration, with additional longer-term 
exposure in the OLE, the low study drop-out rate, and small amount of missing data.
Modeling and simulation were used to bridge the Q4W regimen evaluated in clinical trials to the 
once monthly regimen proposed for the marketed product. There are not expected to be clinically 
meaningful differences between the to-be-marketed one monthly dosage and the Q4W regimen 
used in trials.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
July 2020 and August 2022, advice letters following review of phase 3 protocols, statistical 
analysis plan (SAP), integrated summary of safety, and a pre-NDA meeting in December 2023. 
Olezarsen is a new molecular entity and is not currently marketed in the United States. Olezarsen 
would be first-in-class for the treatment of FCS, for which there are no FDA-approved therapies. 
Olezarsen received fast track designation in January 2023, breakthrough therapy designation in 
February 2024, and priority review for this NDA submission. Refer to Section 12 for a detailed 
summary of the regulatory history.
Trial ISIS 678354-CS3, the pivotal trial in this submission, was conducted to evaluate the 
efficacy and safety of olezarsen as compared to placebo in 66 adults with genetically confirmed 
FCS. The trial was designed as a double-blind, placebo-controlled, 53-week trial. Eligible study 
subjects were randomized to either olezarsen 50 mg, olezarsen 80 mg, or placebo administered 
SC Q4W. The primary efficacy endpoint was percentage change in fasting TG from baseline to 
Month 6 (average of Week 23, 25, and 27 assessments). Other evaluations included assessment 
of the effect of olezarsen on non-HDL-C concentrations and adjudicated acute pancreatitis (AP) 
event rates. Safety and tolerability assessments included adverse events (AEs), clinical 
laboratory tests, electrocardiogram (ECG), use of concomitant medications, and independently 
adjudicated event rate of major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE) for olezarsen compared 
with placebo.
The safety and confirmatory evidence of effectiveness of olezarsen in patients with HTG was 
provided with data from phase 2b Trial ISIS 678354-CS8, and other trials in the olezarsen 
clinical development program. Trial CS8 was a multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-
controlled trial evaluating olezarsen 50 mg or olezarsen 80 mg administered Q4W as an SC  
injection for 1 year in 154 subjects with HTG and atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease 
(established or at increased risk for), and/or with sHTG. The primary efficacy endpoint was the 
percent change in fasting TG from baseline at Month 6, which was defined as the average of 
Weeks 25 and 27, compared with the placebo group. Safety assessments were similar to those of 
Trial CS3.
The review team identified three review issues relevant to the evaluation of benefit (Section 6.3)
•
Confirmation of efficacy of TG reduction of the 80 mg dose in Trial CS3
•
 
•
Confirmation of the reduction in pancreatitis events in Trial CS3
At the start of the review process, several potential risks were identified for olezarsen based on 
prior knowledge of both olezarsen and volanesorsen. These risks (or potential risks) were 
multidisciplinary and included concerns such as hypersensitivity reactions, thrombocytopenia, 
hepatic-related or renal-related events, dysglycemia, and the bridging of the vial and autoinjector 
(commercial product), as the 90% confidence interval (CI) for maximum plasma concentration 
(Cmax) for the 80 mg dose is outside the standard upper CI limit of  80 to 125%.

3.1.1.4. Adequacy of the Confirmatory Evidence in Trial CS8 
in the Severe and Moderate Hypertriglyceridemic 
Populations 
The following lines of evidence constitute adequate confirmatory evidence of treatment benefit: 
(1) well-understood underlying disease pathophysiology; (2) targeted mechanism of action of 
olezarsen; (3) evidence of drug effect on a pharmacologically relevant biomarker; and (4) 
evidence of exposure-response on the biomarker. 
The review team established that there is adequate confirmatory evidence to generate substantial 
evidence of effectiveness (SEE) with Trial CS8, a phase 2 trial in severe and moderate HTG 
populations. For a detailed discussion, refer to Section 6.3.4. 
3.1.1.5. Confirmation of Other Lipoproteins and Parameters 
in Trial CS3 in the FCS Population
The lipid parameters LDL-C, non-HDL-C, total ApoB and ApoC-III levels were confirmed by 
the statistical team. These changes in the lipid profile (LDL-C, non-HDL-C, and total ApoB) are 
expected based on the lipid metabolism pathway and do not alter the favorable benefit-risk 
profile of olezarsen 80 mg of reducing TG and the risk of AP. The reduction of ApoC-III 
confirmed the mechanism of action of olezarsen.
3.1.2. Key Safety Review Issues
3.1.2.1. Hypersensitivity Reactions
Hypersensitivity reactions, including symptoms of bronchospasm, diffuse erythema, chills, and 
myalgias, requiring medical treatment have occurred in patients treated with olezarsen.
3.1.2.2. Platelet Reductions and Bleeding Events
Olezarsen can cause reductions in platelet count, including reductions below the lower limit of 
normal. No olezarsen-treated patient with FCS had severe thrombocytopenia, defined as a 
platelet count <50,000/mm3. There were no major bleeding events associated with a low platelet 
count. Overall, the proportion of patients experiencing a bleeding adverse event was similar 
across treatment groups.
3.1.2.3. Increases in Liver Enzymes
Increases from baseline in liver enzymes within the normal range were observed with olezarsen 
treatment in the FCS population. The incidence of hepatic events was low and similar across 
treatment groups. In some subjects, increases in liver enzymes lead to discontinuation or 
interruption of study drug. There were no cases of Hy’s Law, an indicator of drug-induced liver 
injury.
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 36 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
19
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
3.1.2.4. Dysglycemia
Small increases in average values in fasting glucose and HbA1c over time were observed with 
olezarsen treatment in the FCS population. Using an algorithmic FDA Medical Dictionary for 
Regulatory Activities Query (FMQ) for hyperglycemia consisting of adverse events, new 
antidiabetic medication, and laboratory values, a higher proportion of olezarsen-treated subjects 
without a medical history of diabetes at baseline (52%) versus placebo-treated subjects (35%) 
fulfilled at least one of the FMQ criterion, suggesting a safety signal for worsening glucose 
metabolism.
3.1.2.5. Injection Site Reactions
Injection site reactions (ISRs), characterized primarily by erythema and discoloration, occur with 
olezarsen treatment. In CS3, ISRs occurred in 3 (14%) subjects in both the olezarsen 80 mg and 
50 mg groups vs. 2 (9%) subjects in placebo group. None of the ISRs were serious, no subjects 
in FCS trials reduced study drug, withdrew from the trial, or required treatment of the injection 
site reaction (ISR).
3.1.2.6. Increase in LDL-C
Increases in LDL-C and total apolipoprotein B (apoB) were observed in the FCS population 
treated with olezarsen compared to those treated with placebo. Despite increases in LDL-C, the 
value remained low (i.e., <70 mg/dL) for most patients.
3.1.2.7. Imbalance in Cardiovascular Events
In the olezarsen clinical development program there was a higher number of deaths, serious 
cardiac events, and adjudicated MACE with olezarsen treatment compared to placebo treatment. 
Most of the serious cardiac events and MACE occurred in a non-FCS population with underlying 
cardiovascular comorbidities. The number of MACE and duration of exposure is too small to 
provide a definitive causality assessment for olezarsen. At this time there is no clear signal of 
cardiovascular harm in the FCS population.
3.1.2.8. Immunogenicity
Treatment with olezarsen results in development of treatment-emergent antidrug antibodies 
(ADA) in approximately 40% of treated subjects. No relationship between adverse events, 
including hypersensitivity events, and ADA status was seen in CS3 and CS8.
3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
The primary evidence of efficacy and safety for olezarsen for the proposed indication of an 
adjunct to diet to reduce TG in adults with FCS is provided by a single adequate and well-
controlled trial conducted in the United States, Canada, the United Kingdom, and Europe (Trial 
CS3; ISIS 678354-CS3). CS3 is a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled phase 3 trial. 
Refer to Sections 6.2.2 and 7.6.2 for additional details. In addition to Trial CS3, a trial conducted 
Reference ID: 5499859

NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
20
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
in the United States and Canada (Trial CS8; ISIS 678354-CS8) provided confirmatory evidence 
of olezarsen effectiveness to substantiate the results of CS3 and provided supportive safety data. 
CS8 is a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled phase 2b trial. Refer to Sections 6.2.3 and 
7.6.3 for additional details. Recommendations from FDA during protocol development were, for 
the most part, incorporated into the trial design for CS3 and CS8.
Additional supportive information was provided by the open-label extension (OLE) of the CS3 
pivotal trial (ISIS 678354-CS13) and the OLE of the CS7 trial (ISIS 678354-CS7); the latter trial 
in subjects previously administered volanesorsen. These four trials provide the basis of the 
benefit-risk assessment for olezarsen in patients diagnosed with FCS. In general, these trials 
included similar subject populations (FCS in CS3, CS13, and CS7; moderate/severe HTG in 
CS8), dosage regimens for olezarsen, as well as primary endpoint definitions and timing of 
assessments in CS3 and CS8. The Applicant asserts that all of the trials were conducted 
according to the International Council for Harmonisation tripartite guideline on Good Clinical 
Practice. Refer to Section 7.5 for additional details.
Additional supportive safety and efficacy information from Trial CS2 (ISIS 678354-CS2), which 
enrolled subjects with HTG with or at high risk for cardiovascular disease (CVD), was also 
submitted. This trial used smaller doses than the proposed marketed dosage of 80 mg monthly. 
The Applicant provided additional information to support the applicability of the data from the 
non-U.S. sites to the U.S. population and provided full clinical study reports and data sets for all 
five trials. Although olezarsen is not yet commercially available in any country, volanesorsen 
shares the identical oligonucleotide sequence as olezarsen and is approved outside of the United 
States. Given their similarities, the safety for volanesorsen was reviewed during the previous 
FDA review for volanesorsen (NDA 210645) as well as the global marketed experience of 
volanesorsen.

5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, 
and Clinical Pharmacology
5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential 
Effectiveness
Olezarsen (ISIS 678354) is an apoC-III mRNA-directed ASO proposed for an indication as an 
adjunct to diet to reduce TG in adults with FCS. Olezarsen is comprised of the active ASO 
component (identical to volanesorsen, which is marketed outside the United States) that is 
covalently attached to triantennary N-acetyl galactosamine (GalNAc3) via a 
 
Reference ID: 5499859
(b) (4)

===== PAGE 43 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
26
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
linker to form an ASO-GalNAc conjugate. The ASO portion binds to apoC-III mRNA and the 
GalNAc3 portion targets the hepatocyte-specific asialoglycoprotein receptor, which is expressed 
abundantly on the hepatocyte cell surface. After internalization, the GalNAc3 and linker are 
cleaved to release the free ASO. The ASO portion of olezarsen binds to the 3’ untranslated 
region of apoC-III mRNA and results in ribonuclease H1-mediated degradation, preventing the 
production of ApoC-III protein. 
FCS is a rare, serious, autosomal recessive inherited disease of lipid metabolism characterized by 
severely impaired LPL activity and extremely high levels of plasma TG carried primarily by 
chylomicrons (resulting in HTG and hyperchylomicronemia). Olezarsen targets apoC-III mRNA 
and reduces expression of ApoC-III protein, a key regulator of plasma TG levels (Figure 1), 
leading to increased TG LPL activity, hepatic uptake of TG-rich lipoproteins, and hepatic lipase 
activity. The increased clearance of plasma TG (as chylomicrons and very low-density 
lipoprotein particles) would be expected to significantly reduce plasma TG and chylomicron 
levels in individuals with FCS. 
Figure 1. A Schematic of ApoC-III Inhibition of Lipoprotein Transport
Source: Figure modified from (Ward et al. 2022)
Abbreviations: ANGPLT3, angiotensin-like protein 3; apoB, apolipoprotein B; apoC-III, apolipoprotein C-III; CM, chylomicron; CMR, 
CM remnant; IDL, intermediate density lipoprotein; LDL, low-density lipoprotein; LPL, lipoprotein lipase; VLDL, very low-density 
lipoprotein
The pharmacological activity of olezarsen was characterized in vitro in primary human 
hepatocytes (HepatoPac®) and primary hepatocytes of transgenic mice expressing the human 
apoC-III gene. Olezarsen produced a concentration-dependent reduction of apoC-III mRNA with 
an IC50 of 0.18 µM and a maximal efficacy of 82% in human hepatocytes, and reduced apoC-III 
mRNA levels in transgenic mouse hepatocytes with an IC50 of 0.012 µM and maximal efficacy 
of 89%. 
Studies with olezarsen or a mouse-specific surrogate were conducted in several animal models. 
In wild-type mice, a mouse-specific apoC-III surrogate ASO was used (ISIS 838707, a murine 
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 44 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
27
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
GalNac3-conjugated version), due to the divergence in sequence between mouse and human 
apoC-III mRNA. In wild-type mice, using the surrogate, liver apoC-III mRNA expression and 
plasma TG levels were reduced by up to 88% and 76%, respectively, compared to control mice 
following 6-week treatment with olezarsen. Studies were also conducted in human apoC-III 
transgenic mice. In the transgenic mice, a single dose of olezarsen up to 10 mg/kg resulted in 
dose-dependent reductions in liver apoC-III mRNA by up to 77%, and reduced plasma ApoC-III 
protein and plasma TG levels by up to 87%. Olezarsen exhibits full complementarity to the 
apoC-III mRNA transcript of humans and cynomolgus monkeys. In monkeys administered 
olezarsen once weekly for up to 39 weeks, there were dose-dependent reductions in liver apoC-
III mRNA of up to 90% and reductions in plasma ApoC-III protein of up to 77%. Minimal 
effects on plasma TG levels were seen in monkeys given olezarsen, which was likely because 
chow-fed monkeys have very low plasma TG levels at baseline. Overall, these animal models 
demonstrated dose and time-dependent reductions in hepatic apoC-III mRNA, plasma ApoC-III 
protein, and (in rodents) plasma TG levels (see Section 13 for further details). The observed 
effects were consistent with the expected pharmacological properties of the compound. Results 
from these animal models provide reasonable proof-of-concept that supported the initiation of 
human trials as assessed using a clinically translatable and monitorable pharmacological 
endpoint, plasma TG.
Of note, a robust series of nonclinical pharmacology studies were previously conducted with 
volanesorsen, the unconjugated active antisense oligonucleotide component of olezarsen, which 
lacks the GalNac moiety but is otherwise structurally identical. Volanesorsen has not received 
marketing approval in the United States, but the nonclinical studies with the active component 
are considered relevant and were cross-referenced by the Applicant to the current NDA. 
Olezarsen and volanesorsen demonstrate a similar pharmacologic profile (sequence identical and 
specific knockdown of apoC-III mRNA in monkeys and humans) and differ primarily in their 
pharmacokinetic and tissue distribution profiles. Olezarsen is specifically distributed to the liver 
and therefore a more rapid liver uptake and lower nominal doses of olezarsen were theoretically 
achievable compared to volanesorsen, which lacks GalNAc.
There was no evidence of off-target activity against other mRNA or downstream protein targets. 
Olezarsen is not an inhibitor or an inducer of major drug-metabolizing cytochrome P450 
enzymes nor is it a substrate or inhibitor of various drug transporters. The established 
pharmacologic class for olezarsen should be consistent with prior members of the same drug 
class, i.e., “Olezarsen is an APOC-III-directed ASO indicated as an adjunct to diet to reduce 
triglycerides in adults with familial chylomicronemia syndrome (FCS)”.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Characteristic
Drug Information
Elimination
Mass balance results
A mass balance study was not conducted due to the risk of prolonged residence 
time in liver. Urinary excretion of total full-length olezarsen within the first 24 
hours represented a small fraction of the administered dose (< 1% of 
administered dose) in healthy volunteers. The low renal clearance over the first 
24 hours supports the hypothesis that the initial plasma clearance is the result 
of rapid tissue uptake, not excretion. The ultimate elimination of unconjugated 
olezarsen is likely a combination of slow nuclease metabolism within tissues 
and subsequent excretion of shortened metabolites (as well as any remaining 
full-length oligonucleotide) into urine.
Clearance 
Population PK model estimated apparent clearance was 13.5 L/h.
Half-life 
The terminal elimination half-life is approximately 30 days.
Metabolic pathway(s)
The ASO-moiety of olezarsen (volanesorsen) is metabolized by endo- and 
exonucleases in tissues to shorter oligonucleotide metabolites. Oligonucleotides 
like olezarsen are not substrates for the classic CYP metabolic pathways.
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight
While there was a clear trend of declining exposure with increasing body weight 
for Cmax,ss and AUCtau,ss, no such apparent trend was identified for 
Ctrough,ss. Considering steady-state apoC-III and TG reductions (percent 
change-from-baseline) were similar across different body weight groups, the 
impact of body weight on olezarsen PK was not considered clinically 
meaningful, and a fixed dose of 80 mg monthly is appropriate, regardless of 
body weight.
Age
Age (adults aged < 65 years [N = 223], 65 to 74 years [N = 41], and ≥ 75 years 
[N = 8]) was not identified as a statistically significant covariate in the population 
PK analysis.
Renal impairment
No dosage adjustment is needed for patients with mild or moderate impairment 
of renal function. Olezarsen was not studied in patients with severe renal 
impairment.
Hepatic impairment
No dosage adjustment is needed for patients with mild hepatic impairment. 
Olezarsen was not studied in patients with moderate or severe hepatic 
impairment.
Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator)
Inhibition/induction of 
metabolism
Olezarsen did not show any direct or time-dependent inhibition of any of the
major CYP enzymes (CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, 
CYP2D6, CYP2E1, and CYP3A4) in vitro at clinically relevant concentrations. 
Olezarsen did not induce CYP1A2, 2B6 or 3A4 at clinically relevant 
concentrations.
Inhibition/induction of 
transporter systems
Olezarsen was not a substrate or an inhibitor of OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, 
OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K, BSEP, BCRP, or P-gp.
Immunogenicity
Bioanalysis
The ADA assay’s ability to detect anti-olezarsen antibodies in the presence of 
circulating olezarsen (drug tolerance) was determined to be 39.1 ng/mL of 
antibody in the presence of up to 4000 ng/mL of olezarsen which is found to be 
acceptable. OPQR reviewed the Applicant’s ADA assay and confirmed that it is 
suitable to detect ADAs against olezarsen.
Incidence
With duration of treatment up to 53 weeks, treatment-emergent ADAs against 
olezarsen were detected in 38.1% (8/21) of patients in the 50 mg olezarsen 
group, 45.5% (10/22) of patients in the 80 mg olezarsen group in Study ISIS 
678354-CS3. ADAs to olezarsen were not characterized for neutralizing activity.

6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential 
Effectiveness
6.1.1. Proposed Olezarsen Dosage Regimen
The Applicant’s proposed dosage of Tryngolza for the proposed indication is 80 mg 
administered by SC injection once monthly (Q1M). 
Missed Dose
Administer Tryngolza as soon as possible after a missed dose. Resume dosing at monthly 
intervals from the date of the most recently administered dose.
6.1.2. Dose Selection Rationale for Clinical Trials
The 50 mg and 80 mg doses of olezarsen Q4W were selected for both the pivotal Trial ISIS 
678354-CS3 and the confirmatory efficacy Trial ISIS 678354-CS8 based on the pre-clinical data 
and the pharmacokinetic (PK), pharmacodynamic (PD), and safety analyses from the Phase 1 
study in healthy volunteers with HTG (ISIS 678354-CS1) and the results of the Phase 2 study in 
patients with atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (ASCVD) and HTG (ISIS 678354-CS2). 
A pooled analysis of safety for the Phase 1 and Phase 2 clinical studies did not demonstrate any 
clear difference in safety or tolerability between the different doses tested (Phase 1: 10 to 120 mg 
single doses, 15 or 30 mg per week for 6 weeks, and 60 mg Q4W for 13 weeks; Phase 2: 10 mg 
or 50 mg Q4W, 15 mg Q2W, and 10 mg Q1W, all for at least 6 months and up to 1 year).
Considering that patients with FCS have extremely high TG levels (often >2000 mg/dL), and 
that the targeted TG range for these patients was <880 mg/dL, a maximal TG reduction over 
70% was considered optimal for olezarsen to prevent the risk of AP. Population PK/PD model-
based simulations for a population of patients with FCS predicted a 70% reduction in TG levels 
and an 86% reduction in ApoC-III levels with a dosing regimen of 80 mg Q4W, which was 
selected as the highest dose tested in this study. The 50 mg Q4W dose of olezarsen was expected 
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 48 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
31
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
to produce a 64% TG lowering and a 79% ApoC-III lowering from baseline and was included in 
the study to evaluate dose-response.
Patients with FCS require chronic treatment, and therefore a Q4W dosing regimen may provide 
advantages over weekly dosing in terms of convenience and compliance for patients.
6.1.3. Exposure-Response Relationship for Efficacy 
and Safety
Evaluation of the Proposed Dosage Regimen
The dosing regimen is supported by the favorable benefit-risk profiles in patients with FCS (Trial 
ISIS 678354-CS3) and efficacy and safety profiles in patients with HTG and ASCVD/high-risk 
for ASCVD or with sHTG (Trial ISIS 678354-CS8, see Section 6.2). In ISIS 678354-CS3, the 
olezarsen 80 mg group had a greater placebo-adjusted mean percent decrease in fasting TG from 
baseline compared with the 50 mg group at Month 6 (44.5% for the 80 mg group vs. 21.5% for 
the 50 mg group) and at Month 12 (61.4% vs. 43.2%, see Section 6.2.2.6 for details). For the 
primary endpoint of mean percent decrease in fasting TG levels from baseline at Month 6, the 
decrease for the 80 mg group compared with the placebo group was statistically significant (p= 
0.0015), but the same comparison for the 50 mg group and the placebo group did not reach 
statistical significance (p=0.1153). Consistent with the mechanism of action of olezarsen, the 80 
mg group had a greater placebo-adjusted mean percent decrease in ApoC-III levels from baseline 
compared with the olezarsen 50 mg group at Month 6 (73.69% vs. 65.48%), and at Month 12 
(81.28% vs. 77.06%). Consistent with the results observed in ISIS 678354-CS3, a greater mean 
percent decrease from baseline in TG levels and ApoC-III levels in the 80 mg group compared 
with the 50 mg group was also observed in ISIS 678354-CS8. The adjudicated AP event rates 
were the same for the 80 mg group (one [4.5%] patient experienced one event) and 50 mg group 
(one [4.8%] patient experienced one event).
In addition, population PK and exposure-response modeling and simulation were conducted to 
support the proposed dosing regimen. According to the exposure-ApoC-III and exposure-TG 
modeling and simulation, 50 mg and 80 mg olezarsen administered Q4W are estimated to 
achieve, at steady-state exposure and the end of the dosing interval, a median percent reduction 
from baseline in ApoC-III of 64.7% versus 73.5% (for 50 mg versus 80 mg), and a median 
percent reduction from baseline in TG of 23.4 versus 29.4%, in patients with FCS. Figure 2 
shows a trend towards a marginally higher percentage change from baseline (CFB) in ApoC-III 
and TG for the 80 mg compared to the 50 mg doses, with overlapping 95% CIs for the percent 
CFB in TG concentrations.

Dosing Regimen Comparison of Once Monthly and Once Every 4 Weeks
To bridge from the Q4W dosing regimen used in the clinical program to the once-monthly 
regimen proposed for commercialization, simulations were performed using the final population 
PK/PD model and applying the covariate distribution from Study ISIS 678354-CS3 in patients 
with FCS. The simulated olezarsen PK and PD profiles following the administration of olezarsen 
80 mg Q4W for 13 doses or once monthly for 12 doses overlapped on Day 1 and at steady-state 
with only marginal difference in median (and 80% prediction interval) olezarsen concentrations 
and ApoC-III or TG concentrations (Figure 3 and Figure 31).
The comparison of Q4W with a once-monthly dosing regimen showed no meaningful differences 
in the simulated PK exposure (Cmax,ss, Ctrough,ss, and AUCtau,ss) (Figure 4) and ApoC-III or TG 
profiles when compared for different dose levels (30, 50, 80, and 120 mg) (Figure 5). 
Overall, there do not appear to be any clinically relevant differences in PK, ApoC-III, or TG 
between the dosing regimen of Q4W and Q1M.

The primary endpoint of BE between the autoinjector and the vial presentations based on AUC0-
336h was achieved but the geometric mean ratio 90% CI for Cmax was slightly above the standard

Cmax, during which plasma concentration versus time profiles showed a small difference, and the 
rest of the concentration-time profiles were nearly superimposable; (2) the percent changes in 
ApoC-III and TG levels from baseline on any of the sampling days were similar between the two 
treatments (autoinjector and vial). These data demonstrate that the small difference in Cmax at the 
80 mg dose level between the two treatments had no clinically meaningful effect on PD; and (3) 
there was no clinical meaningful difference in safety profiles and ISRs between the two 
presentations for olezarsen. Of note, as detailed in Section 17, the autoinjector was introduced 
into the open-label clinical trials, CS7 and CS13, and the exposure to the autoinjector in these 
trials was small.

6.2.2. Trial ISIS 678354-CS3
6.2.2.1. Design, Trial CS3
The pivotal Trial ISIS 678354-CS3 (Trial CS3) was a randomized, double blind, placebo-
controlled trial in patients with FCS with and without a prior history of pancreatitis. The primary 
objective of this trial was to demonstrate the efficacy of olezarsen (two different doses) on the 
percent change in fasting TG over a 53-week period compared to placebo. Subjects were 
randomized in a 1:1 ratio to either cohort A (50 mg) or Cohort B (80 mg). Each cohort was 
further randomized 2:1 to receive olezarsen or placebo. Randomization was stratified by prior 
history of pancreatitis (within 10 years prior to screening) and previous treatment with 
volanesorsen. The study design is shown below (Figure 6).
Figure 6. Study Design, Trial CS3
Source: Complete Study Report, Trial CS3.
The primary efficacy endpoint was change in fasting TG from baseline to Month 6 (defined as 
the average of Weeks 23, 25, and 27). The primary endpoint family included analysis of the 
percent change in fasting TG from baseline at Month 6 first for the olezarsen 80 mg group, and, 
if the comparison with placebo was statistically significant for the olezarsen 80 mg group, then 
Reference ID: 5499859

37
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
superiority over placebo of the percent change in fasting TG from baseline at Month 6 for the 
olezarsen 50 mg group was to be determined. 
The effect on AP events was also evaluated in this trial. Out of a total of 28 secondary endpoints, 
a comparison of adjudicated AP between the olezarsen treatment arms and placebo was listed in 
endpoints #13-16, and #25-26 (see below). 
Other FCS-related symptoms were assessed as exploratory endpoints.

6.2.2.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trial CS3
Main Inclusion Criteria
1. A diagnosis of FCS (type 1 hyperlipoproteinemia) by documentation of confirmed 
homozygote, compound heterozygote, or double heterozygote for loss-of-function mutations 
in type 1-causing genes, such as LPL, glycosylphosphatidylinositol-anchored high-density 
lipoprotein-binding protein 1 (GPIHBP1), apolipoprotein A5 (ApoA5), apolipoprotein C-II 
(ApoC2), glycerol-3 phosphate dehydrogenase 1 (GPD1), or lipase maturation factor 1 
(LMF1).
2. Fasting TG ≥880 mg/dL at screening. If the fasting TG was <880 mg/dL, up to two 
additional tests may have been performed, with any single test used to qualify
3. Age ≥18 years at the time of informed consent
4. History of pancreatitis (defined as a recorded diagnosis of AP or hospitalization or ER visit 
for severe abdominal pain consistent with AP and for which no alternate diagnosis was 
made) within 10 years prior to screening. Patients without a recorded history of pancreatitis, 
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 57 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
40
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
or no recorded history within 10 years prior to screening, were also eligible but their 
enrollment was capped at 35% (i.e., ≤21) of the 60 planned patients.
5. Willing to follow a diet comprising ≤20 g of fat per day during the study
6. The following concomitant medications were allowed if on a stable dose for at least 4 weeks 
prior to screening:
Statins, omega-3 fatty acids, prescription and over-the-counter (OTC), fibrates, or other lipid-
lowering medications
Antidiabetic medications
Antihypertensive medications
Oral anticoagulants
Tamoxifen, estrogens or progestins
Main Exclusion Criteria
1. Clinically significant abnormalities in medical history (e.g., previous acute coronary 
syndrome within 6 months of screening, major surgery within 3 months of screening) or 
physical examination
2. AP within 4 weeks prior to screening
3. Screening laboratory results as follows, or any other clinically significant abnormalities in 
screening laboratory values that would have rendered a patient unsuitable for inclusion:
Platelet count <100,000/mm3 at Screening or Qualification. If the platelet count was 
<100,000/mm3, up to two additional tests may have been performed to qualify at both 
screening and qualification
Alanine aminotransferase (ALT) or aspartate aminotransferase (AST) >3.0x upper limit of 
normal (ULN)
Total bilirubin > ULN unless due to Gilbert’s syndrome
Estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) <45 mL/min/1.73 m2 (as determined by the 
Chronic Kidney Disease Epidemiology formula for creatinine clearance)
Urine protein/creatinine ratio (UPCR) ≥500 mg/g, or urine albumin/creatinine (UACR) ratio 
≥300 mg/g
4. Uncontrolled arterial hypertension (blood pressure [BP] >160/100 mm Hg)
5. History of bleeding diathesis or coagulopathy or clinically significant abnormality in 
coagulation parameters at screening
6. Malignancy within 5 years, except for basal or squamous cell carcinoma of the skin or 
carcinoma in situ of the cervix that had been successfully treated
7. Active infection with HIV, hepatitis C or hepatitis B diagnosed by initial serological testing 
and confirmed with RNA testing, or prior treatment for hepatitis C.
8. Treatment with another investigational drug (non-oligonucleotide), biological agent, or 
device within 1 month of screening, or five half-lives of investigational agent, whichever was 
longer
The above eligibility criteria are acceptable.

6.2.2.3. Patient Screening and Disposition, Trial CS3
A total of 144 subjects were screened for the trial. Of those screened, approximately 54% failed 
screening due mostly to failure to meet randomization criteria (69), other (6), physician decision 
(2), and withdrawal by patient (1). The reason “Other” for screen failure was further investigated 
(Listing 16.2.1.2). In most cases (5 out of 6) it was due to subject not able to meet according to 
visit schedule.

A total of 66 subjects were randomized to receive olezarsen (olezarsen 80 mg, n=22; olezarsen 
50 mg, n=21) or placebo (n=23). The number of patients in each treatment group with prior 
history of pancreatitis within 10 years of Screening and with previous treatment with 
volanesorsen were similar, due to stratified randomization.
Approximately 88% or 38/43 subjects in the pooled olezarsen treatment group completed the 
study. Table 8 below summarizes the disposition of subjects by treatment arm. 
Overall, five subjects (5/43 or 11.6%) discontinued pooled olezarsen treatment (three subjects in 
the olezarsen 80 mg group, two subjects in the olezarsen 50 mg group) compared to one subject 
in the placebo group. 
Overall, three subjects (3/43 or 7.0%) in the pooled olezarsen group discontinued study drug due 
to an adverse event (AE); no patients in the placebo group discontinued treatment due to an AE. 
One of the discontinuations due to an AE was a sudden death in the olezarsen 50 mg group 
which is further examined in the safety review. 
Across the treatment groups, the percentage of subjects that completed the trial was balanced and 
allowed for a meaningful assessment of efficacy and safety.

Baseline lipid and lipoprotein levels were generally consistent across treatment arms. Mean

50 mg, and placebo treatment groups, respectively.
6.2.2.5. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial CS3
Analysis Population
The FAS was defined as all patients who were randomly assigned to treatment and received any 
amount of olezarsen or placebo. The primary efficacy analysis was performed using FAS. The 
safety set was also defined as all patients who were randomized to treatment and received any 
amount of olezarsen or placebo. The safety analysis was performed using the safety set.
Primary Efficacy Analysis
The primary analysis time point was at Month 6. The value of the primary endpoint was defined 
as the average of Week 23, 25, and 27 fasting assessments. The Applicant’s prespecified primary 
analysis of the primary endpoint, the mean percent change in fasting TG from baseline to the 
primary analysis time point, was performed using an analysis of covariance (ANCOVA) model 
with missing values imputed using the retrieved dropout method (patients who discontinue 
treatment before Week 23 but still have TG assessments at Weeks 23, 25, or 27). The model 
included treatment group (olezarsen 80 mg, olezarsen 50 mg, pooled placebo) and the two 
randomization stratification factors (prior history of pancreatitis within 10 years prior to 
screening (yes vs. no) and previous treatment with volanesorsen (yes vs. no) as the fixed effects 
and natural log-transformed baseline fasting TG as a covariate. To account for potential 
heterogeneity in variance, the 95% CIs were calculated using the robust variance estimator based 
on the Bell and McCaffrey method. The Applicant stated that any intercurrent events occurring 
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 62 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
45
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
before Week 23 (e.g., treatment discontinuation, use of additional medication, changes in 
background or concomitant treatments) were implemented using the treatment policy strategy 
where the patients’ assessments post intercurrent events were collected and used in the analysis. 
The Applicant conducted a post hoc test for normality based on the observed data using a 
Shapiro-Wilk Normality Test. The FDA results shown are different from the Applicant’s results, 
but the conclusions concur.
Missing Data
There was about 7.5% missing data at Month 6 (one patient in olezarsen 50 mg, three patients in 
olezarsen 80 mg, and one patient in the placebo group). There was not a sufficient number of 
retrieved dropouts, thus the Applicant used a washout multiple imputation approach (Jump to 
Reference, J2R) to impute missing TG data. The Applicant stated that patients treated with 
olezarsen who had missing TG values after the last dosing date multiply imputed based on 
baseline fasting TG, the two stratification factors of their own treatment group, and the 
imputation model for the placebo group. Patients in the placebo group had missing TG values 
multiply imputed based on baseline fasting TG, fasting TG values at postbaseline visits and the 
two stratification factors from the placebo group. 
There was some concern over how the missing data were imputed. We were unable to replicate 
the results. An information request was sent to the Applicant seeking clarification on whether 
their imputation procedure had taken into account all uncertainties of the estimated imputation 
model based on placebo data. It seems that the Applicant selected the first 100 imputed datasets 
with the imputed values falling into feasible range of the imputed endpoints among 500 
imputations. This procedure may underestimate the uncertainty of the imputed values. In 
response to the information request , the Applicant performed alternative imputation by directly 
restricting the imputed values to be within the feasible range instead of discarding out of range 
imputations. FDA conducted a washout imputation in the primary analysis to impute missing 
data without truncating any imputed values. FDA results are shown in Section 6.2.2.6. The 
results are different from the Applicants, but the conclusion remained the same. FDA’s analyses 
used only placebo completer data for the placebo-washout imputation whereas the Applicant’s 
imputation used all observed placebo data, which also contributed to the difference in results.  
The Applicant conducted some sensitivity analyses in the FAS population to evaluate the impact 
of missing data on the analysis conclusion for the primary endpoint. A pattern mixture model 
with ANCOVA was conducted, as well as a nonparametric analysis with Wilcoxon rank-sum 
test, a two-dimensional tipping-point analysis, and an ANCOVA model using log-transformed 
data. The results of the nonparametric analysis with Wilcoxon rank-sum will be shown in this 
review. 
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 63 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
46
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Secondary Endpoints
There are many secondary endpoints (see Section 6.2.2.1 and Section III). This review will 
present results of 
:
•
Percent change in fasting TG from baseline at Month 12 
•
Percent change in fasting ApoC-III from baseline at Month 6 and Month 12 
•
Percent change in fasting ApoB-48 from baseline at Month 6 and Month 12 
•
Percent change in fasting non-HDL-C from baseline at Month 6 and Month 12 
•
Adjudicated AP event rate.
•
Adjudicated AP event rate in patients with a prior history of pancreatitis within 10 years prior 
to screening 
•
Adjudicated AP event rate between pooled olezarsen and pooled placebo in the subset of 
patients with two or more adjudicated AP events in 5 years prior to enrollment (FAS).
There are some endpoints of interest to the FDA that were not included in the main clinical study 
report by the Applicant that are also shown in this review:
•
Percent change in baseline in LDL-C ultracentrifugation (mg/dL)
•
Percent change in baseline in fasting total ApoB (mg/dL) 
•
Percent change in baseline in fasting high density lipoprotein cholesterol (mg/dL).
Note: Month 12 value was defined as the average of Weeks 51 and 53 values of an endpoint.
The continuous secondary endpoints were analyzed similarly to the primary endpoint with the 
same missing data imputation implemented. FDA conducted a washout imputation to impute 
missing data without truncating any imputed values. 
The pancreatitis endpoints were analyzed using a negative binomial regression model with 
treatment group and previous treatment with volanesorsen (yes/no) as the factors, and number of 
adjudicated AP events in 5 years prior to the enrollment, i.e., events occurred within 1,826 days 
(calculated by 365.25 times 5 and rounded to integer) before the first dose date and up to the first 
dose date and time, as a covariate. The logarithm of time in year that each patient was observed 
from Week 1 to 53 was used as an offset variable.
Multiplicity
A step-down procedure to account for multiplicity across treatment comparisons and key 
endpoints was used. For the primary endpoint family, the two treatment arms (olezarsen 80 mg 
and olezarsen 50 mg) were compared against pooled placebo using the hierarchical testing 
procedure, in which the olezarsen 80 mg treatment group was first compared to the pooled 
placebo at the 2-sided alpha level of 0.05. If the comparison was statistically significant 
(p<0.05), then the olezarsen 50 mg treatment group was compared against the pooled placebo at 
the alpha level of 0.05. If the comparison of the olezarsen 80 mg treatment group was not 
Reference ID: 5499859
(b) (4)
(b) (4)

===== PAGE 64 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
47
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
statistically significant, then the comparison of the olezarsen 50 mg treatment group against 
pooled placebo and all secondary endpoints were considered exploratory.
Testing of the secondary endpoint family was performed only if the comparison of the primary 
endpoint was statistically significant (p<0.05) for both the olezarsen 80 mg and 50 mg treatment 
groups. If the comparison of the first secondary endpoint was statistically significant (p<0.05), 
then the second secondary endpoint was tested. However, if the comparison of the first 
secondary endpoint was not statistically significant, then all endpoints of lower rank were 
considered exploratory. This process was repeated with each subsequent secondary endpoint.
6.2.2.6. Results of Analyses, Trial CS3

significance was not achieved for the olezarsen 50 mg group compared with the pooled placebo

Acute Pancreatitis
In Study CS3, an independent blinded committee prospectively adjudicated all AEs suggestive of 
AP for which the subject visited the emergency room or was hospitalized. Of note, the 
committee also adjudicated retrospective AP events which occurred within 5 years of 
randomization using the same definitions; see Section III for more discussion of retrospective AP 
adjudication. 
To prospectively adjudicated AP, the committee used the revised Atlanta Diagnostic Criteria 
(Banks et al. 2013) which required two of the following for the diagnosis of documented AP:
•
Abdominal pain consistent with AP
•
Serum amylase or lipase levels that were at least 3x ULN
•
Computed tomography (CT)/magnetic resonance imaging findings consistent with AP 
However, since clinicians do not always obtain a complete workup for AP, and to ensure that AP 
events were not missed, the committee defined additional categories for the diagnosis of AP: 
•
Probable pancreatitis- required all of the following: abdominal pain consistent with AP; 
typical supportive treatment for pancreatitis (such as the patient being NPO, receiving 
intravenous [IV] fluids, and on analgesics); amylase and lipase had to be elevated but did not 
have to be above 3x ULN; radiology investigations were not performed or not diagnostic; 
discharge diagnosis was AP with no alternative diagnosis. 
•
Possible pancreatitis- required all of the following: a previous medical diagnosis of AP; 
abdominal pain strongly consistent with AP; use of typical supportive measures for AP; 
serum amylase/lipase were normal or not determined or missing; radiology investigations 
were not performed or not diagnostic; discharge diagnosis was AP with no alternative 
diagnosis.
•
Unable to adjudicate- insufficient source documentation.
•
No diagnosis of AP- sufficient evidence provided to conclude there was no diagnosis of AP.
For data analysis, events that were decided as documented, probable, or possible were considered 
to be positively adjudicated AP. 
The adjudication workflow for AP was such that a triggering event was randomly assigned to 
two adjudicators to be adjudicated independently; if there was consensus between the reviewers, 
the decision was complete. If there was disagreement, or if additional information was required, 
the event was assigned to a meeting for final adjudication. At the meeting of three reviewers, the 
chair would render the final decision.
In Study CS3, there were 31 prospective events adjudicated by the committee. Of the 31 events, 
13 were determined to be confirmed AP events. Of the 13 events, 2 occurred in the olezarsen 
treatment arms (one each in the olezarsen 80 mg group and 50 mg group) and 11 events occurred 
in the placebo arm; the mean rate ratio (pooled olezarsen groups vs. placebo) was 0.12 (95% CI, 
0.02 to 0.66, nominal p-value 0.0144) (Table 20). All subjects who had a positively confirmed 
AP event had a history of two or more previous episodes before the trial.

Abbreviations: CI, confidence interval; N, total number of subjects
The Applicant prespecified a number of secondary efficacy analyses related to adjudicated AP. 
All the endpoints were of nominal significance. Note that the treatment period was “Week 1 to 
Week 53”. The Applicant created a second interval “Week 13-Week 53” to reflect the near 
steady-state (at approximately three half-lives) of olezarsen. The “Week 13-Week 53” interval is 
considered exploratory by the FDA. 
Table 21 displays the results for the adjudicated AP event rates between pooled olezarsen and 
pooled placebo in the subset FAS population with a prior history of pancreatitis within 10 years 
prior to screening for Week 1 to 53 and Week 13 to 53. There were two events that occurred in 
the pooled olezarsen group and 11 events in the pooled placebo group from Week 1 to 53. There 
was a 90% decrease in the AP event rate for the pooled olezarsen group compared to the pooled 
placebo group from Week 1 to 53, the estimated reference range (RR) was 0.10 with 95% CI

A post hoc analysis of the proportion of subjects in the single treatment arm olezarsen 80 mg 
who had an event of adjudicated AP from Week 1 to 53 was conducted in the FAS (Table 23), in 
the subset of patients with a prior history of pancreatitis within 10 years prior to screening (Table 
24), and in the subset of patients with two or more events of AP in 5 years prior to enrollment 
versus placebo (Table 25).
For the olezarsen 80 mg group, a nominally significant decrease in the proportion of patients 
experiencing an event of AP from Week 1 to 53, compared with the placebo group, was observed 
for all patients (nominal p-value = 0.0470) and for patients with a prior history of pancreatitis 
within 10 years prior to screening (nominal p-value = 0.0133), while the decrease in proportion 
for patients with two or more events of adjudicated pancreatitis in 5 years prior to enrollment did 
not reach nominal significance (nominal p-value = 0.1189).

Table 26 shows the demographics of subjects with prospectively confirmed adjudicated AP. The 
subjects with confirmed prospective AP in Study CS3 were relatively young, with a mean age of 
44.8 years, but with an age range that was broad from 32-69 years. There were more females 
than males who had adjudicated pancreatitis in this study. All the subjects had a history of a 
previous AP event within the last 5 years with a range from 2 to 16 prior events. The baseline TG 
ranged from 1355 to 6116 mg/dL.

Regarding the two subjects who had adjudicated pancreatitis on the olezarsen treatment arms, 
Subject ID#
 (olezarsen 80 mg), the subject had a large reduction in TG from 6116 to 
1806 mg/dL, but for the subject receiving olezarsen 50 mg, the TG slightly increased from 
baseline 1651 mg/dL to 1876 mg/dL, the last TG before the AP event. Therefore, the degree of 
TG reduction did not necessarily correspond to AP risk reduction. 
6.2.3. Trial ISIS-678354-CS8
6.2.3.1. Design, Trial CS8
The confirmatory phase 2 Trial ISIS 678354-CS8 (Trial CS8) was a randomized, double blind, 
placebo-controlled trial in patients with sHTG and moderate HTG with increased risk for or 
established CVD. The primary objective of this trial was to demonstrate the efficacy of olezarsen 
(two different doses) on the percent change in fasting TG over a 53-week period compared to 
placebo.

Figure 10. Study Design, Trial CS8

The primary efficacy endpoint was the change from baseline to Month 6 (defined as the average 
of Weeks 25 and 27). The primary endpoint family included analysis of the percent change in 
fasting TG from baseline at Month 6 first for the olezarsen 80 mg group, and, if the comparison 
with placebo was statistically significant for the olezarsen 80 mg group, then analysis of the 
percent change in fasting TG from baseline at Month 6 for the olezarsen 50 mg group was to be 
determined.

in FAS was listed as secondary endpoint #47. Acute pancreatitis was adjudicated by a blinded 
independent committee according to the Atlanta Classification and as outlined in the Pancreatitis 
Charter. Additional exploratory/safety endpoint for MACE was also explored.

6.2.3.2. Eligibility Criteria, Study Trial CS8
Main Inclusion Criteria
1. Aged ≥18 years at the time of informed consent
2. Was included in at least one of the following groups (a or b):
Hypertriglyceridemia with fasting TG ≥150 mg/dL and <500 mg/dL with either
Clinical diagnosis of ASCVD defined as documented coronary artery disease, 
cerebrovascular disease, or peripheral artery disease
At increased risk of developing ASCVD defined as the following:
1. Type 2 diabetes mellitus,
2. Two (2) or more of the following cardiovascular (CV) risk-enhancing factors
a.
Males ≥55 years of age or females ≥65 years of age
b. Females with a history of preeclampsia, or premature menopause
c.
Family history of premature ASCVD
d. Current tobacco use (5 cigarettes/day or more for at least 1 year)
e.
Metabolic syndrome
f.
Hypertension requiring antihypertensive treatment
g. Chronic kidney disease stage 3 (eGFR 30-59 mL/min/m2)
h. LDL-C ≥160 mg/dL
i.
LDL-C ≥100 mg/dL on therapy to lower LDL-C
j.
Elevated high-sensitivity C-reactive protein (hsCRP) (≥2.0 mg/L)
k. Elevated lipoprotein (≥50 mg/dL)
l.
Ankle-brachial index <0.9 in asymptomatic patients
m. Coronary artery calcium score ≥100
Severe HTG with fasting TG ≥500 mg/dL
3. Patients were to be on standard of care lipid-lowering medications per local guidelines. 
Lipid-lowering medications were to be optimized and stabilized for at least 4 weeks prior to 
screening to minimize changes in these medications during the study. 
4. Patients were to be willing to comply with diet and lifestyle recommendations
5. Satisfied the following:
Females could not be pregnant or lactating, and had to be either:
Surgically sterile (e.g., tubal occlusion, hysterectomy, bilateral salpingectomy, bilateral 
oophorectomy)
Postmenopausal (defined as 12 months of spontaneous amenorrhea in females >55 years 
of age or, in females ≤55 years, 12 months of spontaneous amenorrhea without an 
alternative medical cause and follicle-stimulating hormone levels in the 
postmenopausal range for the laboratory involved)
Abstinent*
If engaged in sexual relations of childbearing potential, had to agree to use a highly 
effective contraceptive method from the time of signing the informed consent form  
until at least 30 weeks after the last dose of study drug (olezarsen or placebo)
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 82 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
65
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Males had to be surgically sterile, abstinent*, or if engaged in sexual relations with a female 
of childbearing potential, were to utilize a highly effective contraceptive method from the 
time of signing the informed consent form until at least 30 weeks after the last dose of 
study drug (olezarsen or placebo)
* Abstinence was only acceptable as true abstinence (i.e., when this was in line with the 
preferred and usual lifestyle of the patient). Periodic abstinence (e.g., calendar, ovulation, 
symptothermal, post-ovulation methods), declaration of abstinence for the duration of a trial, 
and withdrawal were not acceptable methods of contraception.
Key Exclusion Criteria
1. Diabetes with any of the following
Newly diagnosed within 12 weeks of screening
Glycosylated hemoglobin (HbA1c) ≥9.5% at screening
Change in basal insulin regimen >20% within 3 months prior to screening
For patients with type 1 diabetes: episode of diabetic ketoacidosis, or three or more episodes 
of severe hypoglycemia within 6 months prior to screening
2. Acute coronary syndrome or stroke/transient ischemic attack within 6 months prior to 
screening
3. Major surgery, peripheral revascularization, or non-urgent percutaneous coronary 
intervention within 3 months prior to screening, or upcoming planned major surgery or major 
procedure (e.g., arterial revascularization) during the course of the study
4. Active pancreatitis within 4 weeks prior to screening
5. Screening laboratory results as follows, or any other clinically significant abnormalities in 
screening laboratory values that would have rendered a patient unsuitable for inclusion:
ALT or AST >3.0 × ULN
Total bilirubin >1.5 × ULN unless due to Gilbert’s syndrome
eGFR <30 mL/min/1.73 m2 (as determined by the Chronic Kidney Disease Epidemiology 
formula for creatinine clearance;
UPCR ≥500 mg/g
6. Uncontrolled arterial hypertension (BP >180/100 mmHg) despite antihypertensive therapy
7. Uncontrolled hypothyroidism, such as thyroid-stimulating hormone > 1.5 × ULN and free 
thyroxine < lower limit of normal, clinical evidence of hypothyroidism, or thyroid hormone 
therapy that had not been stable for ≥4 weeks prior to screening
8. Active infection with human immunodeficiency virus, hepatitis C or hepatitis B (e.g., HBsAg 
positive) diagnosed by initial serological testing and confirmed with RNA testing, or prior 
treatment for hepatitis C. Patients at screening who tested positive by serology (for example, 
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 83 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
66
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
positive for Hepatitis C antibody), but negative by RNA may have been allowed in 
consultation with the Sponsor Medical Monitor or designee
9. Malignancy within 5 years of screening, except for non-melanoma skin cancers, cervical in 
situ carcinoma, breast ductal carcinoma in situ, or Stage 1 prostate carcinoma that had been 
successfully treated.
10. Hypersensitivity to the active substance or to any of the excipients (olezarsen or placebo)
11. Concomitant medication/procedure restrictions
Systemic corticosteroids or anabolic steroids within 6 weeks prior to screening and during 
the study unless approved by the Sponsor Medical Monitor or designee
Use of bile acid resins such as colestipol, cholestyramine, or colesevelam within 4 weeks 
prior to screening or planned during the study
Plasma apheresis within 4 weeks prior to screening or planned during the study
Change in medications known to exacerbate HTG such as certain beta blockers, thiazides, 
isotretinoin, oral antidiabetic medications, tamoxifen, estrogens, or progestins within 4 
weeks prior to screening
Change in atypical antipsychotic medications (e.g., olanzapine and clozapine) within 3 
months prior to screening
The eligibility criteria for Trial CS8 were acceptable.
