6.2.3.5. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial CS8
Analysis Population
The FAS was defined as all patients who were randomly assigned to treatment and received any 
amount of olezarsen or placebo. The primary efficacy analysis was performed using the FAS. 
The safety set was also defined as all patients who were randomized to treatment and received 
any amount of olezarsen or placebo. The safety analysis was performed using the safety set.
Primary Efficacy Analysis
The primary analysis time point was at Month 6. The value of the primary endpoint was defined 
as the average of Weeks 25 and 27 fasting assessments. The Applicant’s prespecified primary 
analysis of the primary endpoint, the mean percent change in fasting TG from baseline to the 
primary analysis time point, was performed using an ANCOVA model that assumes unequal 
variance with missing values imputed using the retrieved dropout method (patients who 
discontinue treatment before Week 25 but still have TG assessments at Weeks 25 or 27). For 
Month 12 the retrieved dropout method includes patients who discontinue treatment before Week 
51 but still have TG assessments at Weeks 51 or 53. The model included treatment group 
(olezarsen 80 mg, olezarsen 50 mg, pooled placebo) as the fixed effect and natural log-
transformed baseline fasting TG as a covariate. 
Missing Data
There was about 7.1% missing data at Month 6 (five patients in olezarsen 50 mg, five patients in 
olezarsen 80 mg, and one patient in the placebo group). If there was not a sufficient number of 
retrieved dropouts, the missing TG data was imputed using a wash-out multiple imputation 
approach (J2R). The Applicant stated that patients treated with olezarsen who had missing TG 
values after the last “observed value” multiply imputed based on baseline fasting TG and the 
imputation model for the placebo group. Patients in the placebo group had missing TG values 
multiply imputed based on baseline fasting TG and fasting TG values at postbaseline visits from 
the placebo group. 
The Applicant selected the first 100 imputed datasets with the imputed values falling into 
feasible range of the imputed endpoints among 1000 imputations. This procedure may 
underestimate the uncertainty of the imputed values. FDA conducted a washout imputation in the 
primary analysis to impute missing data without truncating any imputed values. FDA results are 
shown in Section 6.2.3.6. The FDA results shown are different from the Applicant’s results, but 
the outcomes concur.
The Applicant conducted some sensitivity analyses in the FAS population to evaluate the impact 
of missing data on the analysis conclusion for the primary endpoint. The primary analysis was 
repeated using the completers set, which is all observed data (no missing data imputation). A 
nonparametric analysis with Wilcoxon rank-sum test, a 2-dimensional tipping-point analysis, on 
the FAS population, a pattern mixture models with ANCOVA was conducted, and an ANCOVA 
model using log-transformed data in the FAS population. 
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 88 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
71
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Secondary Endpoints
There are many secondary endpoints (see Section III). This review will show the secondary 
endpoints, percent change in fasting TG from baseline at Month 12, the percent change in fasting 
non-HDL-C, HDL-C, LDL-C, total Apo from baseline at Month 6 and 12 and adjudicated AP 
event rate during the treatment period. The Month 12 value was defined as the average of Weeks 
51 and 53 values of the endpoint. The Applicant’s prespecified continuous secondary endpoints 
were analyzed using an ANCOVA model that assumes unequal variance with missing values 
imputed using the retrieved dropout method. The model included treatment group (olezarsen 80 
mg, olezarsen 50 mg, pooled placebo) as the fixed effect and natural log-transformed baseline 
fasting TG as a covariate for mean percent change in fasting TG from baseline to Month 12. For 
the percent change in fasting non-HDL-C, HDL-C, LDL-C, and total Apo from baseline to 
Month 6 and 12 were analyzed using an ANCOVA model that assumes unequal variance with 
missing values imputed using the retrieved dropout method. The model included treatment group 
(olezarsen 80 mg, olezarsen 50 mg, pooled placebo) baseline stratification factor (qualification 
fasting TG levels of <500 mg/dL vs. ≥500 mg/dL) as the fixed effect and natural log-transformed 
baseline fasting associated endpoint as a covariate. 
Adjudicated AP event rate: The pancreatitis endpoint was analyzed using a negative binomial 
regression model or Poisson regression model with treatment group and natural log transformed 
baseline fasting TG as the factors, and number of adjudicated AP events in 5 years prior to the 
enrollment as a covariate. The logarithm of time in year that each patient was observed from 
Week 1 to 53 was used as an offset variable.
Multiplicity
A step-down procedure to account for multiplicity across treatment comparisons and key 
endpoints was used. All tests will be conducted at a two-sided alpha level of 0.05. For the 
primary endpoint family, the two treatment arms (olezarsen 80 mg and olezarsen 50 mg) were 
compared against pooled placebo using the hierarchical testing procedure, in which the olezarsen 
80 mg treatment group was first compared to the pooled placebo at the 2-sided alpha level of 
0.05. If the comparison was statistically significant (p<0.05), then the olezarsen 50 mg treatment 
group was compared against the pooled placebo at the alpha level of 0.05. If the comparison of 
the olezarsen 80 mg treatment group was not statistically significant, then the comparison of the 
olezarsen 50 mg treatment group against pooled placebo and all secondary endpoints were 
considered exploratory.
Testing of the secondary endpoint family was performed only if the comparison of the primary 
endpoint was statistically significant (p<0.05) for both the olezarsen 80 mg and 50 mg treatment 
groups. 
If the comparison of the first secondary endpoint was statistically significant (p<0.05), then the 
second secondary endpoint was tested. However, if the comparison of the first secondary 
endpoint was not statistically significant, then all endpoints of lower rank were considered 
exploratory. This process was repeated with each subsequent secondary endpoint.

6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
Issue/Background
The pivotal phase 3 trial in the FCS population, Trial CS3, assessed two primary co-endpoints, 
the mean percent change in fasting TG from baseline at Month 6 (average of Weeks 23, 25, and 
27) for olezarsen 80 mg compared with placebo and the mean percent change in fasting TG from 
baseline at Month 6 for olezarsen 50 mg compared with placebo. 
The primary TG endpoint was statistically significant for olezarsen 80 mg, but not for the 
olezarsen 50 mg dose. TG as an efficacy endpoint has been the basis of approval for other TG-
lowering drugs in patients with severe hypertriglyceridemia (TG ≥500 mg/dL). TG has also been 
historically accepted by the Division as a surrogate for the reduction in the risk of AP. In 
addition to TG reduction, other lipid parameters were reviewed to determine whether the overall 
benefit risk profile remained favorable.
Trial CS3 also assessed key secondary endpoints of a reduction in prospectively adjudicated AP; 
it is the first outcome trial submitted to the Agency to measure adjudicated AP in an HTG 
population. The nominal finding of a reduction in the rate of AP is consistent with the 
pathophysiology that links elevated levels of TG with AP and the reduction of TG with a 
subsequent reduction in AP. However, the limitations of the trial are its small size and relatively 
short duration to determine AP outcomes. 
The olezarsen FCS program was based on one adequate and well-controlled trial supported by 
confirmatory evidence generated by Trial CS8, a phase 2 trial in severe and moderate HTG 
populations. 
6.3.1. Efficacy of Triglyceride Reduction of 80 mg 
Olezarsen in Trial CS3 in the FCS Population 
Issue
Does the olezarsen 80 mg dose show efficacy in reducing TG levels in subjects with FCS in Trial 
CS3? 
Background
Familial Chylomicronemia Syndrome 
Familial chylomicronemia syndrome is a rare inherited disease of lipid metabolism that causes 
sHTG and hyperchylomicronemia and is estimated to affect 1 to 2 persons per 1,000,000 in the 
United States (Warden et al. 2020; Tripathi et al. 2021). FCS is caused by reduced or absent LPL 
activity which leads to a reduced ability to clear TG leading to their accumulation in plasma. 
Because patients with FCS lack LPL activity, they are dependent on less efficient hepatic 
pathways for TG clearance. Genetic testing can confirm the diagnosis of FCS; however, FCS is 
often diagnosed using clinical signs and symptoms due to barriers to obtain genetic testing 
(Moulin et al. 2018). 
The major complication of FCS is acute and recurrent episodes of pancreatitis. Approximately 
85% of patients with FCS report at least one episode of AP, and 40% have multiple recurrences 
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 93 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
76
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
during their lifetime (Davidson et al. 2018). In addition, patients with FCS frequently experience 
eruptive xanthomas, lipemia retinalis, nausea, vomiting, and recurrent abdominal pain (Baass et 
al. 2020). 
Apolipoprotein C-III as a Target for Drug Development in Hypertriglyceridemia
ApoC-III is a small protein that inhibits the activity of LPL, the enzyme responsible for the break 
down and clearance of TG from the blood. ApoC-III also inhibits the uptake of TG-rich particles 
by the liver. Therefore, ApoC-III is a target for drug development aimed at lowering TG levels. 
Olezarsen is a GalNAc3-conjugated antisense oligonucleotide that reduces ApoC-III production 
by binding to apoC-III mRNA and inducing its degradation (Alexander et al. 2019). 
TG Reduction Used in Other Drug Approvals in Hypertriglyceridemic Populations 
TG has historically been accepted by the Division as the basis of approval for TG-lowering drugs 
in patients with sHTG (TG ≥500 mg/dL) and mixed dyslipidemia (TG ≥150-200 mg/dL). 
Moreover, elevated TG levels (>1,000 mg/dL) increase the risk of pancreatitis. The mechanism 
for hypertriglyceridemic pancreatitis is postulated to involve hydrolysis of TG by pancreatic 
lipase and release of free fatty acids that induce free radical damage, inflammation, and damage 
to the pancreas. Another hypothesis postulates that hyperviscosity due to elevated levels of 
chylomicrons in pancreatic capillaries leads to ischemia and pancreatitis (Tsuang et al. 2009). 
Since elevated TG levels are hypothesized to cause pancreatitis by these mechanisms, TG 
reduction has been assumed to decrease the risk for AP, although there have been no previous 
outcomes trials for AP in HTG populations. 
Assessment
In Trial CS3, TG reduction (primary endpoint) was assessed as the difference in mean percent 
change from baseline to Month 6 in fasting TG between olezarsen 80 mg and pooled placebo in 
subjects with FCS in the FAS. 
When deciding between using the median or the mean to describe changes in TG, the choice may 
depend on the distribution of the data, the clinical question of interest, and the consideration of 
influence of extreme large values. The mean is often the common choice as the difference in 
means estimate the average casual effect. The mean could be sensitive to extreme values (which 
may or may not be outliers). If there are outliers or if the distribution is highly skewed and the 
sample size is not large, inference on the mean relying on normal approximation might be 
suboptimal. If TG levels are highly variable with outliers, the median provides a better 
representation of the typical changes in TG. Previous studies indicate the typical distribution of 
TG is skewed. Recent TG lowering drugs (such as Vascepa and Epanova) and statins with TG 
lowering indications (Crestor, Lipitor) have used median percent TG changes as the primary 
endpoint as TG tends to have a skewed distribution (at least in non-FCS populations). The 
Applicant conducted a post hoc test for normality based on the observed data using a Shapiro-
Wilk Normality Test. This test indicated that there was not enough evidence to conclude that the 
percent change in TG did not follow normal distribution. Thus, the Applicant used the difference 
in mean to describe the percent changes in TG. 
A sensitivity analysis using a non-parametric Wilcoxon rank sum test was conducted to explore 
the impact of distributional assumption on the analysis conclusion.  The median percent change 
in TG from baseline to Month 6 endpoint was -33.6% for olezarsen 80 mg, and +2.8% for 
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 94 =====
NDA 218614

placebo. The median of the treatment difference between olezarsen 80mg and placebo was 
-40.8% (95% CI -65.5, -16.1). 
Another prespecified sensitivity analysis for the primary endpoint used a log transformation of 
TG data (Table 37). Testing was conducted to investigate whether the primary analysis was 
sensitive to violation of the normality assumption. These sensitivity analyses were consistently in 
favor of olezarsen 80 mg over placebo.

robustness of the results. Furthermore, the primary endpoint analysis was repeated in the FAS 
with no missing TG value at baseline and the primary analysis time point. This analysis also 
supported the conclusion from the primary analysis. 
Additionally, the secondary endpoint of percent change in TG from baseline to Month 12 
between olezarsen 80 mg and pooled placebo in subjects in the FAS showed persistence of TG 
reduction at Month 12. At Month 12, the placebo-subtracted mean percent decrease in TG for 
olezarsen 80 mg was approximately -61.4% (95% CI -101.9% to -20.8%).

6.3.3. Acceptability of the Secondary Endpoint of 
Acute Pancreatitis Risk Reduction
Issue
Is the secondary endpoint of reduction in the rate of AP acceptable and can it be described in the 
olezarsen labeling?  
Background
As stated previously, patients with FCS have a significantly elevated risk of AP due to extremely 
high TG levels. Thirty to 40% of FCS patients will experience at least one episode of AP, and 
many will have recurrent, severe episodes that are potentially life-threatening. Estimates of the 
lifetime incidence of pancreatitis in this population range from approximately 50 to 80% (Stroes 
et al. 2017). Acute pancreatitis can lead to complications such as pancreatic necrosis, infection, 
and multiorgan failure.
Assessment
Trial CS3 investigated whether treatment with two different doses of olezarsen (80 mg and 50 
mg) affected the rate of AP as compared to placebo in several secondary endpoints. 
Because of the failure of efficacy (TG reduction) of olezarsen 50 mg dose (co-primary endpoint), 
all following endpoints, including AP rates, did not meet statistical significance but were 
considered to be of nominal significance. However, the reduction in the pancreatitis rate can be 
considered clinically significant. Note that the secondary endpoints summarized below regarding 
pancreatitis rate reduction considered the pooled treatment arms for both olezarsen 80 mg and 
olezarsen 50 mg.
Reference ID: 5499859
(b) (4)

NDA 218614

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Background
Requirements for Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
Substantial evidence of effectiveness, the regulatory requirement for approval, generally consists 
of evidence from at least two adequate and well-controlled trials. In certain circumstances, a 
single adequate and well-controlled trial demonstrating efficacy may be sufficient to generate 
SEE if there is adequate confirmatory evidence. Such circumstances are described in the FDA 
draft guidance for industry Demonstrating Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness With One 
Adequate and Well-Controlled Clinical Investigation and Confirmatory Evidence (September 
2023). 
For example, clinical trial data from diseases with similarities in their underlying 
pathophysiology is appropriate for use as confirmatory evidence. Additionally, the degree of 
similarity between the efficacy endpoints in similar diseases can further support the use of these 
data as confirmatory evidence.  
Other criteria for allowing use of confirmatory evidence includes the persuasiveness of the single 
investigation; the robustness of the confirmatory evidence; the seriousness of the disease, 
particularly when there is an unmet medical need; the size of the patient population; and whether 
it is ethical and practicable to conduct more than one adequate and well-controlled clinical 
investigation. 
These criteria and considerations are all relevant to the olezarsen development program for FCS, 
a very rare and serious disease with an unmet need. Because the olezarsen FCS program has only 
one adequate and well-controlled trial, we sought to assess whether there is adequate 
confirmatory evidence to generate SEE with Trial CS8, a phase 2 trial in severe and moderate 
HTG populations. 
Causes and Classification of Hypertriglyceridemia
Plasma TG levels represent the TG content within various lipoprotein particles, mainly 
chylomicrons and VLDL. A fasting TG level below 150 mg/dL is generally considered within 
the normal range. Non-fasting TG levels of 175 mg/dL fall around the 75th percentile of the 
normal range, while levels of approximately 400 mg/dL are in the 97th percentile. In the general 
population, plasma TG levels are skewed to the right with a long tail toward higher values, 
varying based on population diversity. Due to this skew, a logarithmic transformation is used to 
define statistical normal ranges for TG levels, which can range from 30 mg/dL to 10,000 mg/dL. 
TG levels also differ between sexes, being higher in men than women, and they increase with 
age and the development of conditions like central adiposity, metabolic syndrome, and diabetes 
(Subramanian 2000).
FCS, sHTG and moderate HTG are all disorders characterized by elevated TG levels, but are 
differentiated by the degree of TG elevation, with moderate HTG populations generally 
characterized by TG between 150 mg/dL to 499 mg/dL, sHTG populations characterized by TG 
between 500 mg/dL to 1,000 mg/dL and FCS characterized by TG ≥1,000 mg/dL. TG levels can 
overlap between categories; for example, some patients with sHTG can have TG over 1,000 
mg/dL and patients with FCS with TGs in the range of sHTG. 
HTG can be caused by various factors. Mild to moderate HTG is a complex and genetically 
diverse condition, typically polygenic, resulting from the combined effects of common and rare 
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 99 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
82
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
variants in over 30 genes, as measured by genetic risk scores. All genetic forms can be worsened 
by environmental factors. Since these conditions arise from the interaction between multiple 
susceptibility genes and lifestyle factors such as poor diet, obesity, or diabetes, individuals with 
moderate to severe HTG should be treated as a single group, regardless of accompanying 
lipoprotein abnormalities. Due to the complexity of these disorders, routine genetic testing is not 
recommended (Subramanian 2000).
FCS is a rare genetic disorder characterized by extremely high TG levels, often above 2,000 
mg/dL. The elevated TG in FCS are due to the accumulation of chylomicrons, large particles that 
carry TG from the intestines into the bloodstream. FCS results from mutations in genes involved 
in the breakdown of chylomicrons, most commonly the LPL gene, which impairs the body’s 
ability to clear TG.
Both FCS and sHTG result from impaired TG metabolism. Both FCS and sHTG are associated 
with an increased risk of AP. Extremely high TG levels (>1,000 mg/dL) in both conditions can 
trigger this life-threatening inflammation of the pancreas. However, the risk is particularly high 
in FCS patients, who may experience recurrent pancreatitis even with modest elevations in TG 
levels.
Assessment 
In Trial CS8, the primary efficacy endpoint of mean percent change in TG from baseline at 
Month 6 compared to placebo was met for both 80 mg olezarsen and 50 mg olezarsen treatment 
groups. Specifically, the placebo-adjusted mean percent change from baseline in TG was -56.5% 
for olezarsen 80 mg group (95% CI, -67.4, -45.6) and -51.7% (95% CI, -62.5, -41.0) for the 
olezarsen 50 mg group; p-value <0.0001 for both olezarsen treatment groups. Results of all 
sensitivity analyses on the mean percent change from baseline in fasting TG at Month 6 were 
consistent with the results for the primary efficacy endpoint.
In the 80 mg olezarsen treatment group, the mean baseline TG was 315 mg/dL (SD of 222 
mg/dL) which decreased to 102 mg/dL (SD of 65 mg/dL) at Month 6. In the 50 mg olezarsen 
treatment group, the mean baseline TG was 292 mg/dL (SD of 183 mg/dL) which decreased to 
115 mg/dL (SD of 76 mg/dL) at Month 6. In the placebo group, the mean baseline TG was 301 
mg/dL (SD of 202 mg/dL) which decreased to 258 mg/dL (SD of 141 mg/dL) at Month 6. 
The TG reduction with olezarsen treatment in Trial CS8 in the sHTG and moderate HTG 
population was consistent with the TG reduction in Trial CS3 in the FCS population. Not only 
were the results of Trial CS8 consistent, but the results were robust with a p-value of <0.0001. 
The TG reduction was also clinically meaningful with TG reductions that were below a threshold 
of 150 mg/dL, considered to be within normal range. 
A sensitivity analysis using a non-parametric Wilcoxon rank sum test was conducted to explore 
the impact of distributional assumption on the analysis conclusion. The median percent change in 
TG from baseline to Month 6 endpoint was -68.6% for olezarsen 80 mg, and -15.0% for placebo. 
The median of the treatment difference between olezarsen 80 mg and placebo was -52.8% (95% 
CI -63.0, -42.6). 
Another prespecified sensitivity analysis for the primary endpoint used a log transformation of 
TG data (Table 39). Testing was conducted to investigate whether the primary analysis was

In Trial CS8, there were no events of AP.  
Conclusion
Yes, Trial CS8, a phase 2 trial conducted in subjects with severe and moderate HTG, is 
acceptable to be used as confirmatory evidence for effectiveness of TG lowering in the FCS 
population. 
FCS and severe and moderate HTG are related through their shared impact on TG metabolism 
and their elevated risk for pancreatitis. However, while FCS is a rare genetic disorder caused by 
a single-gene defect, severe and moderate HTG are often a result of both genetic predisposition 
and environmental influences. Both conditions require careful management to prevent 
complications, especially pancreatitis.
6.3.5. Confirmation of Other Lipoproteins and 
Parameters in Trial CS3 in the FCS Population
Issue
Are the changes in LDL-C, total ApoB, non-HDL-C and ApoC-III in Trial CS3 confirmed by the 
statistical team? 
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 101 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
84
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Background
Understanding the changes in the overall lipid and lipoprotein profile is important because it can 
provide an assessment of the risk for CVD. Because Trial CS3 was of 53 weeks duration, and 
was not powered to evaluate cardiovascular risk reduction, the confirmation of lipid and 
lipoprotein changes was conducted to identify the changes that occurred during the trial. The 
lipid profile can guide healthcare providers to prescribe dietary changes, exercise, or medications 
(e.g., statins) to improve cholesterol levels as needed for the individual patient.  
Elevated TG is a risk factor for AP. Additionally, elevated TG and LDL-C are both risk factors 
for CVD. TG are mainly transported by VLDL. As VLDL particles release TG into tissues (via 
lipoprotein lipase), they become smaller, denser particles called LDL, which are richer in 
cholesterol. Therefore, when TG are removed from the bloodstream, the number of LDL 
particles can increase, raising LDL-C levels (Feingold 2022). 
ApoC-III plays several important roles in lipid metabolism, particularly in the regulation of TG 
levels. Its main functions include inhibition of lipoprotein lipase, an enzyme responsible for 
breaking down TG contained within VLDL-C and chylomicrons. By inhibiting LPL, ApoC-III 
slows the breakdown and clearance of TG from the bloodstream. ApoC-III also impairs the 
liver’s ability to remove TG rich lipoproteins from circulation by inhibiting their uptake by 
hepatic receptors. For these reasons, ApoC-III has is a target for therapies aimed at reducing TG, 
such as olezarsen.  
Assessment
Refer to Section 6.2.2.6 to review changes in LDL-C, non-HDL-C, total ApoB, and ApoC-III. 
Refer also to Section 16 for additional lipid analyses (waterfall plots). In Trial CS3, mean LDL-
C increased, mean non-HDL-C decreased, and there were slight increases in mean total ApoB 
with olezarsen 80 mg as compared to placebo. ApoC-III levels were also decreased with 
olezarsen treatment versus placebo. 
The observed increases in LDL-C are consistent with the effects of other TG-lowering 
medications in patients with sHTG. The mean LDL-C values remained below 100 mg/dL for 
olezarsen 80 mg/dL. On average, non-HDL-C levels decreased with olezarsen treatment, 
although total ApoB increased slightly compared to placebo. The net effect on atherosclerotic 
risk is unknown. One (1.8%) and three (5.2%) patients in the olezarsen 80 mg and 50 mg groups, 
respectively, experienced MACE treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) during the study, 
while no patients in the placebo group experienced such an event. MACE events are discussed 
further in Section 7. 
Non-HDL-C includes all forms of atherogenic cholesterol such as LDL-C, VLDL-C, and 
intermediate-density lipoprotein. Non-HDL-C may be a more accurate predictor of CVD risk 
than LDL-C alone, especially in people with moderately high TG or metabolic disorders. In the 
FCS population, non-HDL-C levels decreased with olezarsen 80 mg to a greater degree than 
placebo. The clinical significance of the change in non-HDL-C in the FCS population is 
unknown.
Total ApoB is a measure of the total number of atherogenic lipoproteins in the blood. ApoB is a 
protein found on the surface of certain lipoproteins, including LDL-C, VLDL-C, intermediate-
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 102 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
85
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
density lipoprotein and lipoprotein(a). The increase in total ApoB may reflect the increase in 
LDL-C seen with olezarsen treatment. 
ApoC-III slows down the clearance of TG from the blood and inhibits the action of LPL, leading 
to HTG. ApoC-III levels were decreased with olezarsen treatment confirming the mechanism of 
action of olezarsen.  
Conclusion
Yes, the lipid parameters LDL-C, non-HDL-C, total ApoB and ApoC-III levels were confirmed 
by the statistical team. These changes in the lipid profile (LDL-C, non-HDL-C, and total ApoB) 
are expected based on the lipid metabolism pathway and do not alter the favorable benefit-risk 
profile of olezarsen 80 mg of reducing TG and the risk of AP. The reduction of ApoC-III 
confirmed the mechanism of action of olezarsen. The results of these lipid and lipoprotein 
parameters will be shown in the olezarsen label. 
7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on 
Nonclinical Data
The nonclinical evaluation of the safety of olezarsen included repeat-dose general toxicology 
studies of up to 26 and 39-weeks in duration with 13 and 26-week recovery periods in mice and 
monkeys, respectively, an evaluation of effects on male and female fertility and early embryonic 
development in mice, a battery of genotoxicity studies in vitro (bacterial reverse mutation assay 
and chromosomal aberration assay) and in vivo (micronucleus assay in mouse), and an impurity 
qualification toxicity study. Carcinogenicity, embryofetal development, and pre-/postnatal 
development toxicity studies were not scientifically justified for olezarsen because studies 
conducted with the unconjugated active ASO (volanesorsen) provided acceptable assessments of 
carcinogenic, embryo-fetal development, and pre-/postnatal risk. 
Potentially clinically relevant observations from repeat-dose toxicity studies with olezarsen (also 
observed with volanesorsen, the unconjugated ASO) included thrombocytopenia, effects on the 
liver, injection site reactions, and hypersensitivity/inflammatory responses, which are 
summarized below.
Thrombocytopenia
Reductions in platelet counts were noted in mice and monkeys following SC administration of 
olezarsen, which was anticipated based on similar findings with volanesorsen. In a 13-week 
mouse toxicity study that included a 13-week recovery period, a 25% reduction in platelet count 
was reported in females administered 24 mg/kg/wk olezarsen (~6x maximum recommended 
human dose based on body surface area [BSA]) that was reversible. However, reductions in 
platelet counts were not observed in the 26-week mouse study at doses up to 20 mg/kg/wk (~5x 
MRHDBSA).  
In a 39-week monkey toxicity study that included a 26-week recovery period, olezarsen induced 
a marked reduction in platelet count (>98% decrease) in a single male animal at the 12 mg/kg/wk 
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 103 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
dose (12x MRHDBSA) on Day 65, resulting in petechia and oral mucosal bleeding. The platelet 
count recovered following treatment with methylprednisolone but was again reduced (~95%) at 
the scheduled termination of this animal at Week 13.  
These findings were considered treatment-related and adverse considering prior observations of 
thrombocytopenia with volanesorsen in both mice and monkeys at clinically relevant doses.  
Thrombocytopenia and bleeding events were monitored during clinical development (Section 
7.2)
Hepatic Findings
Drug-related histopathology findings were reported in the liver of both mice and monkeys 
following SC administration of olezarsen. In the 26-week mouse toxicity study with olezarsen, 
dose-related increases in the incidence and severity of hepatocellular hypertrophy, cytoplasmic 
alteration, karyomegaly, increased mitotic figures, and vacuolated/granular macrophages were 
noted. These microscopic findings correlated with increased absolute (by up to ~60%) and 
relative liver weights at ≥6 mg/kg/wk (~1.5x MRHDBSA) and increases in liver enzymes 
(approximately 3-fold AST, 7-fold ALT and 2-fold alkaline phosphatase) at 20 mg/kg/wk dose 
(~5x MRHDBSA) in both sexes compared to control. These effects were reversible. 
Hepatic-related events were also observed in a 39-week monkey toxicity study with olezarsen. 
Liver findings included increases (over baseline) in ALT (4-fold increase), AST (1.6-fold 
increase) and glutamate dehydrogenase (~5-fold) at ≥6 mg/kg/wk (~6x MRHDBSA). The 
increases in liver enzymes correlated with microscopic observations of basophilic granules in 
hepatocytes and Kupffer cells (along with Kupffer cell hypertrophy). At ≥12 mg/kg/wk (~12x 
MRHDBSA), increases (up to 2-fold) in absolute and relative liver weights occurred. The effects 
on liver enzymes and liver weight were reversible. 
From a nonclinical perspective, the liver is considered a target organ but with uncertain 
translatability to humans. These observations in animals are known to be associated with ASO 
uptake and accumulation in the liver, leading to hepatocellular vacuolation, hepatocellular 
single-cell necrosis, and Kupffer cell hypertrophy resulting from the presence of basophilic 
granules in the liver of both mouse and monkey (Frazier 2015). Vacuolated or granular 
macrophages in the liver are also associated with cellular activation and cytokine production by 
the proinflammatory properties of ASOs (Engelhardt et al. 2015). These effects were associated 
with liver weight increases and with increases in liver enzymes. The effects on the liver were 
generally reversible as the incidence/severity was lessened following a recovery period. Humans 
are thought to be less sensitive to these effects, but liver safety was carefully monitored during 
clinical development (Section 7.2).
Injection Site Reactions 
Injection site reactions were observed in the 26-week mouse and 39-week monkey toxicity 
studies. In mice, vacuolated/granular macrophages were observed in the skin and injection sites 
at all dose levels (≥2 mg/kg/wk, ~0.5x MRHDBSA). Mononuclear cell infiltration and basophilic 
granules in histiocytes were also found in both sexes at the injection site. In monkeys, minimal to 
marked mononuclear cell infiltration or slight acute inflammation was observed at the injection 
sites at ≥6 mg/kg/wk (~6x MRHDBSA). These microscopic changes are consistent with the pro-
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 104 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
87
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
inflammatory effects seen in animals with ASOs. Injection-site reactions were monitored during 
clinical development (Section 7.2).
Hypersensitivity/Inflammatory Reactions
Changes in biomarkers indicative of hypersensitivity reactions were observed in the mouse and 
monkey toxicity studies. 
In the 26-week mouse study, an increase in the potent inflammatory cytokine, monocyte 
chemoattractant protein-1, which suggests an inflammatory response. In addition, IgM decreased 
in males at ≥ mid dose (up to 40% lower) and in females at ≥ low dose (up to 58%). This effect 
was reversible in the 13-week recovery group. In the 39-week monkey study, complement 
activation was observed. Dose-dependent increases in complement Bb were seen at all dosages. 
Up to 3-fold (females) and up to 21-fold (males) at 12 and 30 mg/kg/wk and up to 3.6-fold 
(males) and 5.5-fold (females) at ≥2 mg/kg/wk. Increases in complement Bb was accompanied 
by decreases in complement C3 at all dosages in males and at 12 and 30 mg/kg/wk in females. 
Effects indicative of generalized inflammation occurred primarily at dosages >12 mg/kg and 
included increases in C-reactive peptide, monocyte chemoattractant protein-1 (MCP-1), and/or 
gamma interferon (IFNƴ) levels.
Phosphorothioate oligonucleotides are reported to cause the release cytokines in rodents and 
nonhuman primates secondary to complement activation (Henry et al. 2002), but humans are 
generally considered less sensitive than these animal models. Monkeys are generally more 
sensitive to 2ʹ-O-methoxyethyl (2’ MOE) ASO-mediated complement activation than humans 
(Shen et al. 2014). Monitoring for hypersensitivity reactions was conducted during clinical 
development. 
Mutagenicity/Carcinogenicity
Olezarsen was not mutagenic in vitro or in vivo. Carcinogenicity studies were not conducted 
with olezarsen, but lifetime carcinogenicity studies were conducted for the active unconjugated 
ASO, volanesorsen, in both mice and rats. These carcinogenicity studies identified increased 
incidences of neoplasms in both mice and rats. Increases in hepatocellular adenomas and 
carcinomas, hemangiomas and hemangiosarcomas, histiocytic sarcomas, and pituitary gland 
adenomas were observed in mice, while malignant fibrous histiocytomas at the injection site 
were observed in rats. These findings are likely related to the increased sensitivity of rodents to 
the proinflammatory effects of phosphorothioate oligonucleotides and/or the method of 
administration and have also been reported in carcinogenicity studies with other ASOs. 
Therefore, the clinical relevance of these tumors is uncertain but may be limited.
Reproductive Safety
No significant risk for pregnancy or embryo-fetal development was detected in studies with 
volanesorsen in mice and rats. Refer to Section 8.4 of this review for labeling recommendations. 
Conclusion
Overall, administration of olezarsen in animals at clinically relevant exposures was well-
tolerated. Observations identified with olezarsen in the 26-week mouse and 39-week monkey 
studies of potential clinical relevance were thrombocytopenia, effects in liver, injection site 
Reference ID: 5499859

reactions, and hypersensitivity/inflammatory responses. Most of the effects are consistent with 
those reported for other ASOs, and indeed some of these effects were also noted clinically (see 
Section 7.7). The clinical significance of the changes in complement activation observed in 
cynomolgus monkeys is unclear, but monkeys are generally thought to have increased sensitivity 
compared to humans. The safety margins and multiples for these observations from these pivotal 
studies are summarized below in Table 40.

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on 
Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
The potential safety concerns for olezarsen were initially based on the clinical experiences with 
volanesorsen and information known about anti-sense oligonucleotides in general. The safety 
profile of volanesorsen and/or antisense oligonucleotides is characterized by hypersensitivity 
reactions/flu-like reactions (Section 7.7.1), injection site reactions (Section 7.7.5), 
thrombocytopenia and bleeding events (Section 7.7.2), hepatic-related events (Section 7.7.3), 
dysglycemia (Section 7.7.4), changes in LDL-C and ApoB (Section 7.7.6), and immunogenicity 
(Section 7.7.8). These topics informed the key safety review issues. For further discussion of 
these issues, refer to the relevant sections hyperlinked above.
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 106 =====

===== PAGE 107 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
90
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
unless otherwise noted. These include safety tables and figures for patient disposition, exposure 
data, AEs, and serious adverse events (SAEs).
Phase 1 and 2 trials were screened for safety signals that may not have been identified in the 
phase 3 program.
The safety population in all clinical trials was defined as randomized subjects who received at 
least one dose of study drug. Subjects were analyzed in the treatment group according to the 
actual treatment they received. 
The primary data for review of olezarsen safety was from CS3, the pivotal, 53-week, placebo-
controlled trial in adults with FCS. Supportive safety data was provided in CS8, the 53-week 
placebo-controlled trial in adults with moderately elevated TG at risk for ASCVD and adults 
with high TG. Two open-label trials in adults with FCS, CS13 and CS7, provide additional 
supportive safety data. All eligible subjects completing treatment in CS3 could elect to enroll in 
an OLE study, CS13, and Trial CS7 provides safety information from adults with FCS that had 
been previously treated with volanesorsen.
The Applicant provided several pooled analyses using different combinations of the above trials 
including a pool of the phase 3 placebo-controlled trials, CS3 and CS8, a pool of studies of 
individuals with FCS – CS3, CS7, CS13, and a pool of all phase 2/3 trials – CS2, CS3, CS8, 
CS7, and CS13. Although the phase 3 trial designs share a placebo-controlled period and dosing 
regimen of olezarsen, both trials had key differences that may impact the types and frequency of 
events, including different patient populations and treatment allocation (1:1 vs. 2:1). Open-label 
trials have inherent biases given treatment assignment is known which may impact both trial 
participants and individuals involved in the study conduct. Therefore, pooling an open-label trial 
with a placebo-controlled blinded trial may be limited in its ability to interpret a drug’s true 
effect. For this safety review, longitudinal data on subjects participating in CS3 and CS13 was 
evaluated such as platelet count changes over time. However, in general, in this clinical 
reviewer’s opinion, pooling did not provide additional information that cannot be gleaned from 
review of the individual trial safety data. Therefore, this review does not present pooled safety 
data.
In Trials CS3 and CS8, a TEAE was defined as an event present prior to receiving the first dose 
of study drug and subsequently worsens, or it was not present prior to receiving the first dose of 
study drug but subsequently appeared.
SAEs were defined as any AE that resulted in death, was life-threatening, required inpatient 
hospitalization or prolonged a current hospitalization, cause persistent or significant 
disability/incapacity, or was a congenital anomaly/birth defect in the offspring of a subject who 
received study drug. In addition, any event that required intervention to prevent one of these 
outcomes could be considered a serious adverse event (SAE) (i.e., allergic bronchospasm 
requiring intensive treatment in an emergency department or home), but this was at the judgment 
of the investigator. 
Following discussion between the clinical reviewers and clinical data scientist teams, the 
following were implemented in the safety analyses, first because adjudicated pancreatitis events 
were considered clinical endpoints of efficacy, these events were excluded from the safety tables 
and figures. Second, the Applicant translated verbatim terms to Medical Dictionary for 
Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) preferred terms for the events reported in the trial. The 
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 108 =====
NDA 218614

91
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
translations were reviewed, and several verbatim terms noted events occurring during or shortly 
after administration of study drug which were recoded with the preferred term ‘injection related 
reaction’. For example, a verbatim term ‘IP INJECTION REACTION DIARREA (sp) AFTER 
15 MINUTES’ was coded by the Applicant with the preferred term of ‘Diarrhoea’. This event 
was considered more consistent with a hypersensitivity reaction and was recoded as ‘injection 
related reaction’ by the review team. 
7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
The safety database for this application is small, due in part to the rarity of the FCS population. 
The Applicant was strongly encouraged, before phase 3 was initiated, to bolster the safety 
database by increasing the size of studies investigating olezarsen in other patient populations, 
especially since the Applicant was contemplating development of olezarsen for the more 
common severe hypertriglyceridemia population (i.e., non-FCS, TG ≥500 mg/dL) at the time. In 
response, the Applicant conducted a small (n=154), 53-week, placebo-controlled trial (CS8) in 
patients with moderate HTG with or at risk for ASCVD and patients with sHTG.
Limitations of this safety database include differences in the CS3 and CS8 populations in regard 
to comorbidities, concomitant medications, and treatment objectives (i.e., the majority of 
subjects in CS8 had moderate HTG with risk for ASCVD and therefore the primary objective is 
to reduce the frequency of MACE versus pancreatitis). These factors may limit the 
generalizability of the safety findings in Trial CS8 to the FCS population. Another gap in the 
safety database is the absence of safety data available to evaluate the effect of olezarsen in 
pediatric patients with FCS, a condition that presents during childhood. The Applicant has an 
agreed Pediatric Investigation Plan with the European Medicine Agency that is deferred 
 
 to determine pediatric dosing (EMA 2023). Therefore, it is possible, especially given 
the size of the database, that additional safety signals may emerge or be more clearly 
characterized with additional exposure in patients with FCS.
However, the strengths of the safety database include the placebo-controlled design, use of two 
doses to assess dose-response, and the 1-year treatment duration in this rare disease population, 
with additional longer-term exposure in the OLE. In addition, there were very few study drop-
outs and the amount of missing data is small. Trial CS8, despite its limitations provides 
additional supportive safety information from a placebo-controlled trial in an HTG population. 
Overall, the safety database is adequate to assess the safety of olezarsen for the proposed 
indication, dosage regimen, and patient population.
At the time of the various data-cutoffs, a total of 202 subjects were exposed to olezarsen in the 
phase 2/3 program, including 87 subjects with FCS. Of these 87 subjects, 54 had at least 12 
months exposure to olezarsen (35 subjects at 80 mg and 19 subjects at 50 mg or 80 mg). Table 
41 below provides the number of subjects exposed to either olezarsen or placebo in the phase 2/3 
program. Treatment exposure for Trials CS3 and CS8 follow the table.

7.6.2.2. Deaths, Trial CS3
There was one death in the olezarsen 50 mg group in Trial CS3. A summary is included here. 
•
Subject #CS3-
; CS3 – Sudden death (unknown cause), olezarsen 50 mg Q4W – 
74-year-old female with a medical history of pancreatitis and hypertension. The patient was 
receiving the concomitant medications calcifediol, calcium carbonate, cholecalciferol, 
denosumab, gemfibrozil, hydrochlorothiazide, losartan, manidipine, and omeprazole. She had 
been previously treated with volanesorsen in Trial CS7 OLE study for 1.5 years. She 
discontinued volanesorsen due to a nonserious treatment-emergent adverse event (TEAE) of 
polymyalgia rheumatica and had been off volanesorsen for 2.5 years. The subject had 
received two doses of olezarsen 50 mg prior to her death. The subject died suddenly while 
she was making dinner on Study Day 42 (12 days post last dose of olezarsen). There were no 
symptoms in the minutes or hours prior to the event. No TEAEs, clinically meaningful 
laboratory results, or electrocardiogram (ECG) abnormalities were reported for the patient 
prior to the death. The cause of death remains unknown. No autopsy details or death 
certificate were available.
Following review of the narratives and case report forms, it is difficult to discern olezarsen’s 
contribution, if any, to this death given the limited information available.
7.6.2.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial 
CS3
Narratives, as well as relevant datasets, and case report forms were reviewed for these events. 
These events are further described, where applicable, in the key safety review topics (i.e., serious 
hepatic-related event of cirrhosis is discussed in Section 7.7.3)
The number and percentage of subjects with a treatment-emergent SAE were lower in the 
olezarsen-treatment groups. A review of the SAEs did not yield a safety signal for olezarsen due 
to the low number of events and alternative causative etiologies (e.g., SAE of intestinal 
perforation secondary to a foreign body ingestion; SAE of bacterial gastroenteritis confounded 
by recent travel history; SAE of intentional overdose confounded by concurrent medical history 
of post-traumatic stress disorder treated with antidepressant medication).

Treatment-emergent adverse events are defined as any AE events starting or getting worse on or after the first dose of the study drug.
Serious adverse events defined as any untoward medical occurrence that at any dose results in death, is life-threatening, requires hospitalization or prolongation of existing

7.6.2.4. Adverse Events Leading to Treatment 
Discontinuation, Trial CS3
Results of the FDA analysis of AEs leading to treatment discontinuation (DAE) for Trial CS3 are 
shown in Table 46. Overall, there were very few subjects (one subject olezarsen 50 mg – ‘sudden 
death’ described in Section 7.6.2.2; two subjects olezarsen 80 mg) that discontinued due to an 
AE. The two subjects (CS3-
; CS3-
) treated with 80 mg olezarsen 
discontinued treatment due to a hypersensitivity or flu-like reaction. This key safety review issue 
is further described in Section 7.7.1. FMQ analysis was done but did not yield additional 
information.

7.6.2.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial CS3
Results of the FDA analysis of TEAEs by preferred term and by FMQ that occurred in ≥5% of 
subjects in any treatment arm for CS3 are shown in Table 47 and Table 48. A higher proportion 
of subjects in the placebo group compared to the olezarsen treatment groups reported a TEAE. 
FMQs that had a larger risk difference in the 80 mg olezarsen treatment groups versus placebo 
were related to reductions in platelet count, injection site reactions, nasopharyngitis, anemia, 
Reference ID: 5499859
(b) (6)
(b) (6)

===== PAGE 115 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
98
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
vomiting, dyspnea, arthralgia, urinary tract infections. Discussion of the key safety review topics 
regarding reduction in platelet count, injection site reactions, are discussed in Sections 7.7. 
It was noted that the preferred term ‘anxiety’ occurred in three (14.3%) subjects and the FMQ 
anxiety occurred in four (19%) subjects treated with olezarsen 50 mg and none in either the 
olezarsen 80 mg or placebo treatment groups. Review of the anxiety AEs at subject level did not 
suggest a relatedness to study drug. There was also no imbalance for anxiety or other psychiatric 
FMQs for olezarsen 80 mg. Note that the FMQ ‘Study agent abuse potential’ is populated by the 
preferred term, ‘somnolence’ in the 80 mg olezarsen group, which on its own does not suggest a 
safety signal for potential abuse.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
7.6.2.6. Laboratory Findings, Trial CS3
Results of the FDA analysis of laboratory value abnormalities for Trial CS3 are shown in Table 
49. There was an imbalance unfavorable to olezarsen in the proportion of subjects with 
elevations in fasting plasma glucose Level 1 ≥100 mg/dL to 125 mg/dL and Level 2 ≥126 
mg/dL, as well as low glucose levels <54 mg/dL and <40 mg/dL. This risk is further discussed in 
Section 7.7.4.

Hematology
Thrombocytopenia leading to bleeding events is a potential antisense oligonucleotide class effect; therefore, analyses evaluated 
coagulation parameters and platelet levels. Additional hematopoietic parameters were also evaluated. A greater proportion of 
olezarsen-treated subjects compared to placebo-treated subjects met lower platelet thresholds. This risk is further discussed in Section 
7.7.2. Another difference of note was a higher proportion of olezarsen-treated subjects had an HbA1c ≥5.7% compared to placebo-
treated subjects. Dysglycemia is discussed in Section 7.7.4.

7.6.3. Safety Results, Trial ISIS 678354-CS8
7.6.3.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events 
Summary, Trial CS8
In Trial CS8, there was a higher proportion of olezarsen-treated subjects experiencing an SAE or 
discontinuation of study drug due to an AE compared to the placebo-treated subjects (Table 56). 
Subjects treated with olezarsen 50 mg reported a higher percentage of study drug 
discontinuations or modifications due to AEs than the olezarsen 80 mg and placebo treatment 
groups. However, overall TEAEs were higher in the placebo treatment group (76.9%) versus the 
olezarsen treatment groups (olezarsen 50 mg, 74.1%; olezarsen 80 mg, 66.7%).

7.6.3.2. Deaths, Trial CS8
There was one death in a subject randomized to olezarsen 50 mg in Trial CS8; a brief summary 
is included here:
•
Subject #CS8-
; CS8 – Death (unknown cause); olezarsen 50 mg Q4W - 76-year-
old male in the olezarsen 50 mg group, experienced a severe serious TEAE leading to death 
(unknown cause) on Study Day 206, approximately 5 weeks after the last dose of olezarsen 
(Study Day 169). The patient had ongoing coronary artery disease, a cardiac pacemaker 
insertion, hypertension, hyperlipidemia requiring statin treatment, Type 2 diabetes, and was a 
former smoker. The patient experienced no liver-related or renal-related laboratory 
abnormalities with olezarsen treatment during the study. The MACE adjudication committee 
were unable to determine if the event was a myocardial infarction or heart failure.
Note that another death occurred in Trial CS2, which enrolled a similar population with 
moderate HTG, with established CVD or at high risk for CVD and is described below:
•
Subject #CS2-
; CS2 – Cardiac arrest, olezarsen 15 mg every 2 weeks – 63-year-old 
male randomized to olezarsen 15 mg every 2 weeks, experienced a fatal cardiac arrest on 
Study Day 90, 5 days after the last dose of olezarsen. He had received seven doses of 
olezarsen before his death. No autopsy was performed. Relevant medical history included 
type 2 diabetes, acute myocardial infarction, coronary artery bypass graft, coronary 
revascularization, heart failure, hypertension, and valvular heart disease. At screening, his 
treated blood pressure was 123/65, and body mass index was 36.6 kg/m2 (Class 2 obesity). 
Concomitant medications included rosuvastatin 40 mg, Vascepa, fenofibrate, Lasix, 
carvedilol, aspirin, losartan, insulin, canagliflozin, and clopidogrel. 
It is difficult to discern olezarsen’s contribution, if any, to these deaths especially given the study 
subjects’ age, significant cardiovascular history, and underlying comorbidities.
7.6.3.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial 
CS8
In Trial CS8, SAEs occurred in a higher proportion of olezarsen-treated subjects. The biggest 
treatment difference between olezarsen and placebo-treatment groups were SAEs occurring 
within the cardiac disorder system organ class (SOC). This risk is discussed further in Section 
7.7.7. Four subjects in the olezarsen 80 mg group compared to one subject in the placebo group 
reported an oncological AE. There is temporal plausibility given the time to diagnosis for some 
of these cases, however, causality cannot be easily determined given the small number of cases 
and underlying comorbidities – remote smoking history and type 2 diabetes that confound the 
causality assessment. 
Reference ID: 5499859

7.6.3.4. Adverse Events Leading to Treatment 
Discontinuation, Trial CS8
FDA analysis of AEs and FMQ leading to treatment discontinuation was done. The analysis by 
SOC and preferred term is shown in Table 58. FMQ analysis is not shown as it did not yield 
additional relevant information. Eight (13.8%) subjects treated with olezarsen 50 mg and three 
(5.3%) subjects treated with olezarsen 80 mg discontinued due to an AE compared to no 
placebo-treated subjects. Half (n=4) of the subjects in the olezarsen 50 mg group discontinued 
treatment due to events related to the injection site, and one of the three subjects treated with 
olezarsen 80 mg who discontinued due to an AE did so because of an injection-related reaction 
(verbatim term ‘post-dose flushing - face, neck, chest, lower body’). Further discussion of 
injection site reactions and hypersensitivity events are found in Section 7.7.5 and Section 7.7.1.

7.6.3.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial CS8
Overall, there was a smaller proportion of olezarsen-treated subjects compared to placebo-treated 
subjects with any TEAE. TEAEs occurring at ≥5% frequency are shown in Table 59 below. 
There were imbalances not favoring both olezarsen treatment groups compared to the placebo 
group for the following preferred terms that occurred in ≥5% of subjects: ‘alanine 
aminotransferase increased’, ‘diabetes mellitus’, ‘injection site erythema’, ‘COVID-19’, and 
‘liver function test increased’. Related topics such as injection site reactions, hepatic-related 
events, and dysglycemia events are further discussed in Section 7.7.4. Please note that there were 
imbalances not favoring the placebo treatment group compared to the olezarsen treatment groups 
such as ‘type 2 diabetes’, ‘sinusitis’, ‘fall’, ‘hypertension’, and ‘urinary tract infection’.

7.6.3.6. Laboratory Findings, Trial CS8
Chemistry
Review of the chemistry laboratory parameters demonstrated an imbalance in the number of 
subjects with an elevated fasting glucose (Level 1 ≥100 to 125 mg/dL). Further discussion of 
dysglycemia is in Section 7.7.4. No other clinically meaningful differences were observed. See 
Section 17 for details.

Hematology
Differences observed in platelet count, lymphocytes, and neutrophils between olezarsen and

values compared to placebo-treated subjects. Additional discussion regarding platelets can be 
found in Section 7.7.2 and changes in other hematological parameters in Section 17.

7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
7.7.1. Hypersensitivity Reactions
Issue
Serious hypersensitivity reactions have occurred with olezarsen treatment, it is unclear whether 
there is a relationship to the development of anti-olezarsen antibodies or previous volanesorsen 
treatment.
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 141 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
124
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Background
Hypersensitivity reactions is a known and labeled adverse drug reaction for several FDA-
approved ASOs. As of 2023, there have been 10 ASOs approved for a variety of medical 
diseases. Hypersensitivity reactions are listed in the Warnings & Precautions section of labeling 
for four of these drugs – defibrotide, inotersen, eteplirsen, and golodirsen. The types of reactions 
observed include rash, urticaria, skin exfoliation, chills, flushing, dysphagia, tachycardia, and 
bronchospasm. Serious hypersensitivity reactions were noted to occur in the clinical 
development program for volanesorsen, including an anaphylactic-like reaction in a study subject 
without FCS within 5 minutes of dosing which required emergency treatment (EMDAC 2018).  
Assessment
In this review, hypersensitivity events were evaluated in categories defined as flu-like reactions 
and hypersensitivity reactions using a variety of company, FDA, and MedDRA queries. 
Flu-like reactions (FLR) were defined as the following:
•
FLR 1 – influenza-like illness, pyrexia, feeling hot, body temperature increased, chills, 
myalgia, or arthralgia starting on day of injection or next day.
•
FLR 2 – (A) influenza-like illness or (B) pyrexia, feeling hot, or body temperature increased, 
plus two of the following: chills, myalgia, or arthralgia starting on day of injection or next 
day.
Hypersensitivity reactions were defined using the following:
•
Hypersensitivity Standardised MedDRA Query (SMQ) (narrow),
•
Hypersensitivity FMQ (narrow, broad, algorithmic4) and
•
Anaphylactic FMQ (narrow).
An AE of special interest was defined as a requirement of any use of medications as pretreatment 
to avoid a hypersensitivity reaction or recurrence of a previous hypersensitivity reaction. 
Table 66 is an overview of the phase 2/3 trials which combines the hypersensitivity events and 
flu-like reactions, followed by a breakdown of the types of each event in Trials CS3 and CS8. 
Serious events or events that led to study drug discontinuation from these trials are discussed.

In the ongoing open-label trials, CS7 and CS13, there were additional hypersensitivity and FLR 
events observed (hypersensitivity SMQ [narrow] CS7 [n=3; 12.5%] and CS13 [n=7; 13%]; FLR 
CS7 [n=4, 16.7%] and CS13 [n=2; 3.7%]). 
Among Trials CS3, CS8, CS13, and CS13, there were seven subjects who discontinued due to 
hypersensitivity or FLR. Three of these discontinuations were considered serious TEAEs, 
including one serious ‘anaphylactic reaction’. The narratives of these subjects were reviewed and 
are summarized in tabular form in Section 17. Summaries of the serious hypersensitivity events 
are included below. 
Serious Hypersensitivity Events
•
Subject 
, Trial CS7 – ‘Hypersensitivity Reaction’: 62-year-old female with FCS. 
She had been previously treated with volanesorsen for 5 years. Her last dose of volanesorsen 
was approximately 9 months before starting olezarsen 80 mg Q4W in the open-label trial, 
CS7. Within 10 hours of administration of her 2nd dose of olezarsen, she reported an itchy 
Reference ID: 5499859
(b) (6)

===== PAGE 144 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
127
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
rash on her back. Two days later she reported flu-like symptoms (runny nose, headache, sore 
throat, and fatigue), progressive whole-body rash, wheezing, stiff neck, swelling of the eyes 
and face, and dyspnea. She did not seek any additional medical attention and reported 
treatment with loratadine 10 mg and fexofenadine hydrochloride 180 mg. Approximately 10 
days after the start of the event, the swelling resolved and treatment was permanently 
discontinued. Her ADA titers remained negative. 
•
Subject 
, Trial CS13 – ‘Hypersensitivity Reaction’: 56-year-old male with FCS, 
previously treated with volanesorsen for 4 months and reported an immunization reaction – 
with injection site discoloration, injection site erythema with occurrence of positive ADA 
(peak titer 6000). No further volanesorsen treatment was received.
This subject’s first dose of olezarsen occurred in the OLE study (randomized to placebo in parent 
study CS3). ADA titers at the beginning of the OLE were 800. Approximately two minutes after 
the administration of the second dose of olezarsen, the subject exhibited an intense redness of his 
face and trunk, which was accompanied by an intense headache. He did not present with dyspnea 
or fainting at this time. The subject’s vital parameters were immediately checked and were as 
follows: BP of 107/70 mmHg, heart rate (HR) of 72 bpm, and no dyspnea on room air with 88% 
oxygen saturation (SpO2). The patient did not go to the emergency room or hospital but was 
treated at the study site with IV saline and 4 mg betamethasone intramuscularly. Monitoring of 
the patient continued. Resolution of the skin rash was noted after approximately 10 minutes post 
onset of symptoms. The investigator noted the appearance of a wheal reaction with redness in 
correspondence to the patient’s abdomen area (periumbilical region and under the right rib) 
which was associated with intense itching. Approximately 20 minutes after symptoms began, the 
patient’s SpO2 (98%) and BP (127/85 mmHg) had returned to normal. Treatment with 
chlorpheniramine maleate was administered intravenously to treat the itching. An hour after the 
onset of hypersensitivity, the patient experienced bronchospasm and shaking chills. Treatment 
included a single dose of methylprednisolone with a combination of beclomethasone 
dipropionate, ipratropium bromide and salbutamol which resulted in an improvement of 
symptoms. An ECG showed no abnormalities. Laboratory values of hsCRP, C3, C4, absolute 
eosinophil count and tryptase before and 1.5 hours after the event were normal. ADA following 
second dose increased to 6400. The subject’s condition improved, and he was discharged from 
the study site on the same day in good clinical condition. 
•
Subject 
, Trial CS13 – ‘Anaphylactic reaction’: 26-year-old male with FCS, 
previously treated with volanesorsen in Trial CS7, an open-label extension study. In CS7-
OLE, he received 24 doses of volanesorsen 300 mg weekly and had a positive ADA titer in 
 (last assessment) of 400. He enrolled in Trial CS3 (baseline ADA positive 
titer 400) and was treated with olezarsen 80 mg for 1 year and rolled into the open-label 
study, CS13 (ADA titer at baseline 25,600). Forty-five minutes after each of the first two 
injections of olezarsen in the OLE trial, he experienced chills, headache, and diffuse 
abdominal discomfort lasting 30 minutes to 1 hour, and approximately 2 to 3 hours of diffuse 
muscle tenderness. Because of these symptoms, the subject was pre-treated with 40 mg of 
prednisolone and 500 mg of Tylenol for subsequent injections without further difficulty until 
the 7th dose. Following his dose of prednisolone and olezarsen, he left the study site in stable 
condition, but returned within 5 minutes because of acute onset of profuse diaphoresis, facial 
edema, diffuse erythema, and severe low back pain. He was brought to the emergency 
department and administered IV Benadryl, solumedrol 125 mg, dexamethasone 10 mg, 
Reference ID: 5499859
(b) (6)
(b) (6)
(b) (6)

===== PAGE 145 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
famotidine 10 mg, and fluids. Vitals at the time were BP of 140 to 160 mmHg (note at 
screening for CS13 – the subject’s BP was 167/107 mmHg), HR 110 bpm, and oxygen 
saturation of 94%. ECG showed sinus tachycardia with non-specific ST-T abnormalities.  
Tryptase was 5.0 before dose, 4 hours later 10.7 (normal <11). On the same day, he 
recovered from the event and was discharged from the ER with a dexamethasone taper. The 
study drug was discontinued due to this event. His ADA titers remained positive. At the time 
of this event, his ADA titers were 6400 and, in the weeks, following increased to 12,800. 
Approximately 2 months after his last dose ADA titers were 200.
Reviewer Comment
This event was submitted to the IND as a 7-day suspected unexpected serious adverse reaction. 
At that time, an internal consult with the Division of Pulmonology, Allergy, and Critical Care 
was requested for review of this event. Following review of the information, the Division of 
Pulmonology, Allergy, and Critical Care review team felt this event was more consistent with an 
acute injection-related reaction versus anaphylaxis (Document Archiving, Reporting and 
Regulatory Tracking System [DARRTS] ID 5100318).
Conclusion
Hypersensitivity reactions, including serious events, occur in patients with FCS treated with 
olezarsen. The onset of symptoms usually occurs within minutes or same day of the injection. 
There is a variable length of treatment duration before the event (first couple of doses versus 
years of exposure). The numbers of hypersensitivity and FLR events overall are very small, so 
their dependence on ADA is unclear. Of the three serious cases, ADA values appear to increase 
in two cases before the event. There were only two cases of subjects receiving pre-treatment to 
avoid a hypersensitivity reaction, and both times this did not prevent events. Pre-treatment is not 
recommended given no systematic protocol for pre-treatment was provided or prospectively 
tested. It is anticipated that additional potentially serious hypersensitivity reactions will occur in 
individuals with FCS treated with olezarsen. Serious hypersensitivity reactions are a known 
adverse drug reaction for this drug class. Management of this risk through labeling with revisions 
to include the types of signs and symptoms observed is a reasonable mitigation.
7.7.2. Platelet Reductions and Bleeding Events
Issue
In the clinical trials, olezarsen-treated subjects experienced a higher frequency of platelet 
reductions compared to placebo-treated subjects, although none were severe (defined as <50 
x109/L). However, because of the exclusion of study subjects with baseline platelet counts below 
100 x 109/L, it is unknown whether serious thrombocytopenia events will occur in patients with 
FCS, a patient population with reported platelet variability who may have below normal platelet 
counts at the beginning of treatment in the postapproval setting.
Background
Anti-sense oligonucleotides are associated with thrombocytopenia and risk of serious bleeding, 
including fatal hemorrhage complications (Benson et al. 2018; Dominguez Senin et al. 2022). 
The primary safety concern for volanesorsen (which shares the same oligonucleotide sequence as 
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 146 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
129
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
olezarsen) was severe thrombocytopenia and risk of serious bleeding (EMDAC 2018). Although 
no deaths or serious bleeding events were noted in the volanesorsen clinical development 
program, there was a higher proportion of volanesorsen-treated subjects with clinical bleeding 
events (excluding bleeding at the injection site) than placebo-treated subjects (36% versus 6%, 
respectively). Reductions in platelet counts associated with volanesorsen were characterized by 
gradual declines over time of approximately 30% from baseline as well as large precipitous 
drops in platelet count below 50 x 109/L. The mechanism triggering these events is unknown but 
may be a result of drug-induced immune thrombocytopenia leading to increased destruction of 
platelets. 
Given the concern for thrombocytopenia, the pivotal FCS trial excluded study subjects that had a 
baseline platelet count below 100 x 109/L (>140 x 109/L was considered the lower bound of a 
normal platelet count) and instituted frequent platelet monitoring (every 2 weeks). Note, no 
subject was excluded during the screening process due to a platelet count <100 x109/L. In CS8, 
platelet levels were obtained Q4W; in CS7 initially every 2 weeks for first 13 weeks then Q4W, 
and in CS13 Q4W, 8 weeks, and 12 weeks in the 1st, 2nd, and 3rd years, respectively. All 
protocols had pre-specified actions for platelet counts below different platelet count thresholds 
below 100 x 109/L. An adverse event of special interest was defined as any case of a platelet 
count reduction below 50 x 109/L accompanied by a major or clinically relevant bleeding event, 
or platelet count below 25 x 109/L.
Assessment
Baseline Platelet Values
Given the reported platelet variability in individuals with FCS, the baseline platelet count was 
defined as the average of all assessments before the first dose of study drug. The average 
baseline level of platelets in CS3 was 200, 188, and 215 x 109/L in the olezarsen 50 mg, 80 mg, 
and placebo groups, respectively. The mean baseline level of platelets in CS8 was 244, 249, and 
252 x 109/L in the olezarsen 50 mg, 80 mg, and placebo groups, respectively. 
Platelet Values Over Time
As shown in the Figure 11 and Figure 12 below, platelets decreased over time for the subjects in 
the olezarsen groups compared to those in the placebo group during CS3. Average declines in 
platelet count of approximately 5 to 15% from baseline were noted in the olezarsen treatment 
groups. There were 11 (25.6%) olezarsen-treated subjects and 5 (21.7%) placebo-treated subjects 
that had platelet counts that went below 100 x 109/L. In CS3, there were 4 (18.2%) subjects 
treated with olezarsen 80 mg and 1 (4.3%) placebo-treated subject with dose interruptions due to 
low platelet count.
By visual inspection of Figure 11 and Figure 13 from CS3 and CS8, respectively, of mean 
platelet count over time, it appears that platelet counts gradually decline over the first 9 months 
and then appear to stabilize, although there is significant variability at some timepoints in the 
FCS population.

Categorical Changes in Platelet Values
A higher proportion of subjects treated with olezarsen exhibited categorical absolute or relative 
reductions in platelet count compared to subjects treated with placebo in both CS3 and CS8. In 
CS3, almost 50% of subjects treated with olezarsen versus 30% of placebo-treated subjects had a 
30% reduction in platelet counts from baseline. In CS8, roughly 20% of olezarsen versus 5% of 
placebo-treated subjects had a 30% reduction in platelet counts from baseline. However, the 
percentage of subjects with 50% reductions from baseline were similar across treatment groups 
in both trials. 
The empirical cumulative distribution functions for percent change from baseline to nadir 
platelet count are shown in Figure 15 and Figure 16 for CS3 and CS8, respectively, allowing one 
to note the proportion of patients in each arm of each trial that had a percent reduction in 
platelets of any amount of interest (or greater). For example, it can be easily discerned that ~50% 
of patients assigned to olezarsen in CS3 had ≥30% reduction in platelet count from baseline to 
on-study nadir.

Adverse Events Related to Platelet Count or Bleeding Events
Reduced Platelet Count Event Terms
In CS3 and CS8, a higher proportion of olezarsen-treated compared to placebo-treated subjects 
had AEs reported related to a reduced platelet count. Not all cases of thrombocytopenia or 
platelet count reductions were reported as AEs, and it is unclear whether investigators employed 
a systematic approach to determining whether a reduction in platelet count qualified as an 
“adverse event” and whether such an event should be reported as a decrease in platelet count or 
thrombocytopenia. Therefore, the number of events portrayed in Table 69 is lower than the 
actual number of cases that occurred in the trials, and a distinction between the event terms 
should not be overinterpreted. In CS3 and CS8, no subjects discontinued due to a 
thrombocytopenia-related AE.

Bleeding Event Terms
Bleeding events were tabulated below using the MedDRA Hemorrhage SMQ. SMQs are 
validated sets of MedDRA terms used to investigate specific safety topics. The Hemorrhage 
SMQ includes preferred terms that refer not only to clinical bleeding events but also to abnormal 
laboratory values (e.g., decreased hemoglobin), which may or may not be caused by bleeding. 
Additional analyses were done to exclude events at the injection site and/or related to a 
laboratory value, as well as evaluation of events in subjects with concomitant anticoagulant or 
antiplatelet medications. 
Using the Hemorrhage SMQ, the proportion of subjects with an event was similar across 
treatment groups in Trials CS3 and CS8. After excluding events that were localized to the 
injection site or represent a laboratory value, there was no meaningful differences were noted in 
“clinical” bleeding events across treatment groups. 
No olezarsen-treated subject in Trial CS3 paused, reduced, or discontinued olezarsen dosing due 
to a bleeding TEAE. Most of the subjects in Trial CS3 were not on concomitant anticoagulant or 
antiplatelet medications at the time of a clinical bleeding event. In contrast, with the exception of 
the clinical bleeding TEAE of epistaxis in a subject in the olezarsen 80 mg group, all “clinical” 
bleeding TEAEs in Trial CS8 were experienced by subjects concomitantly receiving 
anticoagulant or antiplatelet medications in Trial CS8. There were three olezarsen-treated 
subjects in Trial CS8 who discontinued, paused, or reduced study medication due to a bleeding 
AE at the injection site – ‘injection site bruising’ (n=1); ‘injection site hemorrhage’ (n=2) - none

Conclusion
Olezarsen treatment causes reductions in platelet count but there is no evidence of an increased 
clinical risk of bleeding. The decrease in platelet count occurs within the first weeks of treatment 
and is characterized by a gradual decline over time. Platelet counts tend to remain on average 5 
to 15% below baseline. Overall, the proportion of subjects experiencing a clinical bleeding AE, 
defined as those not occurring at the injection site or associated with a laboratory term, were 
similar across treatment groups. No olezarsen-treated subjects, to date, have reported a platelet 
count below 50 x109/L. There were two serious clinical bleeding events in olezarsen-treated 
subjects in the OLE studies, CS13 and CS7. Although temporally associated with olezarsen 
treatment, it is this reviewer’s assessment that concomitant medications and/or underlying 
comorbidities offer a plausible alternate etiology, especially given continued treatment with 
olezarsen in these two subjects without further bleeding events.  
7.7.3. Increases in Liver Enzymes
Issue
Serious hepatic AEs in subjects treated with olezarsen were observed. Mean increases in liver 
enzymes associated with olezarsen treatment and cases of categorical increases in liver enzymes 
greater than the ULN in olezarsen-treated subjects were also observed.
Background
Risk of hepatotoxicity with elevation of ALT and AST and hepatic steatosis has been indicated 
in mipomersen and inotersen, which are ASOs targeting hepatic gene expression. Inotersen 
labeling recommends baseline and monthly monitoring of liver enzymes (Severi et al. 2023; 
Akcea Therapeutics 2024a). Vupanorsen, an ASO targeting ANGPTL3, which is a key regulator 
in TG metabolism, caused a dose-dependent increase in hepatic fat fraction (Zimerman et al. 
2024). In contrast, volanesorsen, in clinical trials of different patient populations of HTG 
including FCS, reduced hepatic fat fraction by 24 to 53% placebo-corrected from baseline 
(Prohaska et al. 2023). Olezarsen includes the Gal-NAc receptor which targets it to the liver, its 
therapeutic site for its mechanism of action. The olezarsen clinical development program for the 
FCS treatment indication did not include assessments of hepatic fat. 
Assessment
Adverse Events
TEAEs related to abnormal hepatic function were evaluated by the following queries – Drug 
Related Hepatic Disorders Comprehensive Search SMQ (narrow and broad), Hepatic Injury 
FMQ (narrow) and Hepatic Failure FMQ (narrow), and review of the hepatobiliary and 
investigations SOC. There were no events related to Hepatic Failure FMQ (narrow) in any of the 
trials (including the open-label extension studies). The Hepatic Injury FMQ and Drug Related 
Hepatic Disorders SMQ, yielded similar results given the significant overlap between the terms 
comprising each query. Table 71 lists the adverse terms related to hepatic function in Trials CS3 
and CS8. 
Reference ID: 5499859

bands were placed to deflate the varices. The subject was discharged from the hospital the 
next day. The subject continued to take olezarsen and is currently enrolled in CS13. ALT and 
AST remain in normal range. Direct bilirubin levels have ranged mostly within the normal 
range – no values have been 2x above the ULN.  
Reviewer Comment
Underlying comorbidity of hepatic steatosis confounds an assessment of causality with 
olezarsen.
•
Subject #CS8-
 Trial CS8 ‘liver function test increased’ DAE: 73-year-old woman 
with HTG at risk for ASCVD, who was randomized to olezarsen 50 mg Q4W. Baseline ALT 
and AST were 23 U/L and 27 U/L, respectively (reference range ALT 10-53 U/L; AST 14-43 
U/L) On Study Day 89, the subject received her 4th dose of olezarsen. Laboratory values on 
that day were notable for ALT and AST >3x ULN (ALT 199 U/L, AST 134 U/L) leading to 
an AE of ‘liver function test increased’. On Study Day 94 she reported ‘fatigue’. As a result 
of these events, study drug administration was paused per stopping rules for elevated liver 
enzymes and symptoms, and ultimately led to the subject permanently discontinuing study 
drug. The subject’s ALT and AST peaked at 352 U/L and 240 U/L on Study Day 117, 
respectively. Bilirubin and alkaline phosphatase remained within normal limits. The ALT 
and AST returned toward normal range over the next 2 months while off study drug. The last 
laboratory values for ALT and AST were 53 U/L and 44 U/L on Study Day 145.
Reference ID: 5499859

drug-induced liver injury, noted with olezarsen treatment. However, to evaluate further this
