tests and hepatic imaging in patients with severe hypertriglyceridemia in dedicated clinical trials 
along with enhanced pharmacovigilance for hepatic toxicity events in patients with FCS.
7.7.4. Dysglycemia
Issue
Treatment with olezarsen did not have consistent salutary effects on glycemic control parameters 
in clinical trials.
Background
Volanesorsen showed a worsening in glycemic control in clinical studies. In a clinical trial of 
subjects with multifactorial chylomicronemia, in those with type 2 diabetes, the mean percent 
changes from baseline in HbA1c were 0.4% in the volanesorsen group and –0.2% in the placebo 
group; these were statistically significant (P=0.0301) (Gouni-Berthold et al. 2021). Per the 
authors of this trial, a mildly elevated baseline HbA1c in both groups and an imbalance in 
baseline antidiabetic therapy with 70% of subjects in the placebo group being on insulin 
compared to 27% of patients in the volanesorsen group, may have contributed to the observed 
differences. However, it is not reassuring that another ASO that also targets ApoC-III reported 
worsening glycemic control with drug therapy (Gaudet et al. 2024).
Assessment
In Trial CS3, average glucose and HbA1c values increased more in the olezarsen treatment 
groups than placebo. At baseline, mean glucose values were approximately 91 mg/dL, 96 mg/dL, 
and 103 mg/dL in the olezarsen 50 mg, olezarsen 80 mg, and placebo groups, respectively. At 
Week 53, there was an approximately 20% increase in the average glucose value in the olezarsen 
treatment groups (absolute mean change from baseline of 15 mg/dL in the olezarsen 50 mg 
group and 18 mg/dL in the olezarsen 80 mg group compared to 13% (4.5 mg/dL) increase in the 
placebo group (see Figure 24).
In Trial CS3, the mean HbA1c at baseline was 5.6%, 5.6%, and 5.8% in the olezarsen 50 mg, 
olezarsen 80 mg, and placebo treatment groups. Over time, the olezarsen treatment groups had 
higher average HbA1c values compared to the placebo group. At Week 53, the olezarsen 50 mg 
group was 0.19 percentage points higher, the olezarsen 80 mg group was 0.14 percentage points 
higher, and the placebo group was 0.08 percentage points higher (see Figure 25).

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
To further assess the effect of olezarsen on glucose metabolism, a hyperglycemia algorithmic 
FMQ was evaluated for CS3 and CS8 overall and by medical history of diabetes (see Table 74). 
In CS3, overall and by diabetic status more olezarsen-treated subjects fulfilled at least one of the 
hyperglycemia criteria compared to placebo-treated subjects. In subjects with no history of 
diabetes, the proportion of subjects fulfilling ≥1 FMQ criterion was greater in both the olezarsen 
50 mg (50%) and olezarsen 80 mg (53.3%) groups compared with the placebo group (35.3%). 
The most fulfilled criteria in this subgroup included “change from baseline fasting glucose ≥20 
mg/dL with postbaseline fasting glucose >100 mg/dL” (44.4%, 53.3%, and 23.5% in the 
olezarsen 50 mg, olezarsen 80 mg, and placebo groups, respectively) and “HbA1c Increase 
≥0.3% with Post-Baseline HbA1c ≥5.7%” (27.8%, 26.7%, and 11.8%, respectively).
In CS8, a less consistent pattern was observed. In subjects without a history of diabetes, 
proportionally fewer subjects fulfilled ≥1 FMQ criterion in the olezarsen 80 mg group (31.6%) 
compared with the olezarsen 50 mg (57.1%) and the placebo (55.6%) group. 
It is uncertain whether the differences in degree of HTG or proportion of subjects with type 2 
diabetes at baseline impacts the effect of olezarsen on glucose control and contributes to the 
differences observed between Trials CS3 and CS8.

Conclusion
Small increases in average values in fasting glucose and HbA1c over time were observed with 
olezarsen treatment in the FCS population. In an ad-hoc analysis using an algorithmic FMQ for 
hyperglycemia consisting of AEs, new antidiabetic medication, and laboratory values, a higher 
proportion of olezarsen-treated subjects without a medical history of diabetes at baseline versus 
placebo-treated subjects fulfilled at least one of the FMQ criteria, suggesting a safety signal for 
worsening glycemic control. However, it is uncertain whether this represents a true risk with 
olezarsen treatment. Furthermore, glucose metabolism is monitorable and treatable, and these 
factors should be considered when evaluating the benefits and risks associated with olezarsen.
7.7.5. Injection Site Reactions
Issue
Treatment with olezarsen is associated with ISRs.
Background
Subcutaneously administered oligonucleotides are associated with localized reactions around the 
injection site, such as erythema, itching, and pain, and less commonly severe ulceration and 
necrosis. The reported incidence of ISRs in various oligonucleotide clinical trials ranged from 
22% to 100% (van Meer et al. 2016). 
Assessment
An injection site reaction was defined as a TEAE with a preferred term or verbatim term 
containing ‘injection site’ that persisted for at least 2 days (i.e., event resolution date not on the 
day of the event onset date or the day after) or was ongoing. A separate definition was also used 
to describe a more severe reaction defined as local cutaneous reaction at the injection site 
(LCRIS), which was defined as: (A) moderate or severe AEs which included the preferred terms 
of injection site erythema, injection site swelling, injection site pruritus, or injection site pain that 
started on the day of injection, persisted for at least 2 days or were ongoing; or (B) any AE at the 
study drug injection site, regardless of severity, that led to discontinuation of study drug, where 
the AE at the study drug injection site was the principal reason for discontinuation or if the AE at 
the injection site led to discontinuation of study drug. The severity of AEs was graded based on 
the Toxicity Grading Scale for Healthy Adult and Adolescent Volunteers Enrolled in Preventive 
Vaccine Clinical Trials, which rates injection site reactions based on pain, tenderness, size of 
erythema/redness, and size of induration/swelling. 
Using the ISR definition, in Trial CS3, a higher proportion of olezarsen-treated subjects reported 
an ISR compared to placebo-treated subjects (see Table 81). None of the ISRs were serious and 
no subjects reduced study drug, withdrew from the trial, or required treatment of the ISR. The 
median number of injections received before the first ISR was 2 in the olezarsen 50 mg group, 3 
in the olezarsen 80 mg group, and 3 in the placebo group. The median duration of the ISR was 
42 days in the 50 mg olezarsen group, 6 days in the 80 mg olezarsen group, and 133 days in the 
placebo group. Two of the eight subjects’ injection site reactions (one placebo ‘IS discoloration’, 
and one olezarsen 50 mg ‘IS erythema’) had not recovered or resolved at the data cut-off.
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 178 =====
NDA 218614

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
There were no events in Trial CS3 that met the definition of an LCRIS.
In the OLE study, CS13, five subjects previously treated with placebo who started olezarsen 
reported 40 ISR events. One subject in the olezarsen-naïve treatment group reported 33 of these 
events. For this individual (#
), the events were ‘injection site discoloration’ that was 
reported as not resolved, ‘injection site erythema’, ‘injection site swelling’, and ‘injection site 
hematoma’. This study subject also experienced a TEAE of hepatic enzyme increased that led to 
permanent discontinuation of study drug. The study site noted that the ISR discoloration event 
had resolved 1 month after the last follow-up post-treatment visit.
Individuals who had been treated with olezarsen in the index trial and continued with olezarsen 
in the open-label extension, reported fewer events – five olezarsen-treated subjects in CS3 
reported seven events in CS13. No events were reported as serious, none met the LCRIS 
definition, and no subjects discontinued treatment due an ISR.

In Trial CS8, no placebo-treated subjects had an ISR compared with 17% in the olezarsen 50 mg

at the injection site reported the event had recovered/resolved.

In the open-label study, CS7, four (16.7%) subjects reported 56 ISR events, 32 of these were 
‘injection site erythema’ and 14 were ‘injection site discoloration’. Of these four subjects, one 
reported injection sites of moderate severity, thus qualifying as an LCRIS. This subject (#
) reported ISRs starting with the first dose and subsequent doses. The day of the 8th dose of 
study drug, the subject reported injection site erythema, swelling, and pain of moderate severity. 
No action was taken with regard to study drug, and 4 days later the events were 
recovered/resolved. The subject continued to report ISRs, for example on Study Days 226, 254, 
282, and 308 (last day before data cutoff) the subject reported ‘injection site discoloration’ while 
continuing olezarsen treatment. In CS7, the mean duration of injection site discoloration was 261 
days.
Conclusion
Injection site reactions occur with olezarsen treatment and are characterized primarily by 
erythema and discoloration. The median number of injections before the first ISR ranged from 1 
to 3. In the FCS controlled trial, CS3, the number of injection reaction site events was small 
(total events in olezarsen-treated [50 and 80 mg combined] group was 9 in 6 [14%] subjects). 
This may be an underestimation of the number of events given that in the open-label studies of 
FCS, 10 (19%) subjects in CS13 reported a total of 47 events, and in CS7, 4 (17%) subjects 
reported 56 events. None of the injection site reactions were serious, and no subjects in FCS 
trials reduced study drug, withdrew from the trial, or required treatment of the ISR. The majority 
of injection site reactions resolved, however the duration of ISRs of discoloration and erythema 
was variable (several days to months before resolution). The incidence of injection site reactions 
will be described in section 6.1 of the PI.
7.7.6. Increase in LDL-C
Issue
In the FCS trial, CS3, treatment with olezarsen was associated with increases in LDL-C and total 
ApoB. These directional shifts are conventionally considered adverse in assessments of 
Reference ID: 5499859
(b) (6)

===== PAGE 180 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
163
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
cardiovascular risk. The clinical impact of olezarsen’s effect on lipids in this population is 
uncertain.
Background
Several drug classes induce lipid abnormalities including anti-hypertensives (thiazide diuretics 
[increase cholesterol and TG], certain beta blockers [increase TG, decrease HDL-C]), 
immunosuppressants (cyclosporine and tacrolimus), protease inhibitors (increase LDL-C), 
antipsychotic drugs (increase TG), SGTL2 inhibitors (increase LDL-C), and fish oil derived 
prescription products (increase LDL-C). Volanesorsen in clinical trials increases LDL-C. This 
may be due to increased lipolysis of TG, although the mechanism has not been conclusively 
determined.
Assessment
In CS3, treatment with olezarsen resulted in elevations in LDL-C. At Month 12, the least square 
mean percent change in LDL-C from baseline increased in the olezarsen 50 mg and olezarsen 80 
mg groups by 53% and 47%, respectively versus -0.1% in the placebo group. However, because 
individuals with FCS have very low LDL-C at baseline, the mean LDL-C values at Month 12 
were 27 mg/dL, 29 mg/dL and 16 mg/dL in the olezarsen 50 mg, olezarsen 80 mg, and placebo 
groups, respectively (see Figure 28).

All subjects at baseline had an LDL-C <70 mg/dL. No placebo-treated subjects had a

categories in LDL-C; two subjects shifted from below 70 mg/dL to the LDL-C category of 100 
to <130 mg/dL and one subject shifted from below to 70 mg/dL to the LDL-C category of 130 to 
<160 mg/dL. These subjects were not on LDL-C lowering medications (e.g., statins) before the 
trial and they were not started on LDL-C lowering medications for these elevations. LDL-C 
values in these subjects trended down over time but remained above baseline values. 
ApoB also increased with olezarsen treatment. At Month 12, a 0.6% least squares (LS) mean 
change from baseline was noted in the placebo group, and a 1.6% and 7.8% increase from

of subjects in the olezarsen treatment groups exceeded the ApoB threshold of 130 mg/dL at any 
time postbaseline (10% olezarsen 50 mg; 38% olezarsen 80 mg; 4% placebo).

In Trial CS7, 22 out of 24 subjects at baseline had LDL-C below 70 mg/dL, and 4 subjects 
shifted to a higher LDL-C category. Three (12.5%) subjects had increases ≥2 categories from 
baseline, both of which were transient. One (4.2%) subject had a shift from <70 mg/dL at

mg during the study.
Conclusion
LS mean increases in LDL-C and ApoB of approximately 50% and 8% from baseline, 
respectively, were noted in the FCS population treated with olezarsen 80 mg compared to a <1% 
LS mean increase in those treated with placebo. In contrast, in Trial CS8 of subjects with 
moderate to high levels of TG, LDL-C increases were smaller and similar across treatment 
groups, notably ApoB had a moderate decrease. It is uncertain, if the difference in LDL-C 
changes between the two trials is related to the degree of TG elevation at baseline and resulting 
lipid metabolism following ApoC-III inhibition. The clinical impact of these changes in the FCS 
population is uncertain, in part because despite increases in LDL-C, the value remains low (i.e., 
<70 mg/dL) for most study subjects. LDL-C is monitorable and could be mitigated with lipid-
modifying therapy.

7.7.7. Imbalance in Cardiovascular Events
Issue
In the phase 2 trial, CS2, a higher number of serious cardiac disorders were observed in the 
olezarsen treatment group compared to the placebo group in a non-FCS population with 
moderately elevated TG at high risk for CVD. The effect of olezarsen on cardiovascular risk is 
unknown. 
Background
Genetic studies of loss of function variants in the apoC-III gene showed a decrease in TG levels 
and risk of atherosclerotic disease (Tg et al. 2014; Wulff et al. 2018). This observation has made 
ApoC-III a pharmaceutical target for reduction of cardiovascular risk. However, it is unknown 
whether a pharmacologic reduction in ApoC-III will translate into a clinical benefit of CV risk 
reduction. Recent trials that have focused on lipid endpoints other than LDL-C illustrate the 
limitations of lipid parameters as surrogates. A notable recent example is the PROMINENT trial, 
a cardiovascular outcome trial of pemafibrate, in statin-treated subjects with type 2 diabetes and 
moderate HTG which failed to show a cardiovascular benefit despite a placebo-adjusted 26% 
reduction in TG, and placebo-subtracted decreases in ApoC-III of 28%, but not ApoB (4.8%) 
(Das Pradhan et al. 2022). 
Assessment
In CS3, CS8, CS7, and CS13 all AEs and SAEs that were consistent with a MACE were 
adjudicated by a blinded, independent committee. Trial CS2, an early phase study of olezarsen 
did not have a MACE adjudication committee. 
In Trial CS2, a dose-finding study of olezarsen (10 mg to 50 mg) in subjects with mixed 
dyslipidemia there was a higher incidence of cardiac disorders in the pooled olezarsen group 
(13.3%) compared to the placebo group (4.2%), including a fatal cardiac arrest in the 15 mg 
every 2 weeks group. Serious cardiovascular events also occurred in a higher proportion of 
olezarsen-treated subjects (n=6 [6.7%]) compared to placebo-treated subjects (n=1 [4.2%]). A 
listing of the cardiovascular SAEs is listed below. Review of the narratives for these subjects was 
notable for a significant medical history of CVD which confounds a causality assessment.
Reference ID: 5499859

Conclusion
In the olezarsen clinical development program there was a higher number of deaths, serious 
cardiac events, and adjudicated MACE with olezarsen treatment compared to placebo treatment. 
There were no deaths or MACE in the placebo treatment groups. Most of the serious cardiac 
events and MACE occurred in a non-FCS population with underlying cardiovascular 
comorbidities. It is this reviewer’s opinion that the number of MACE and duration of exposure is 
too small to provide a definitive causality assessment for olezarsen. Ongoing larger studies in 
subjects without FCS with either sHTG or moderate HTG at risk for or with established ASCVD 
may provide further information to inform this safety review issue. At this time there is no clear 
signal of cardiovascular harm in the FCS population, and the small imbalance in serious cardiac 
events associated with olezarsen treatment in the non-FCS population is likely the play of 
chance.
7.7.8. Immunogenicity
Issue
Treatment with olezarsen results in the development of ADA, therefore an immunogenicity 
assessment to evaluate the potential impacts of olezarsen ADA on safety is a review issue.
Assessment
The immunogenicity of olezarsen was evaluated in all clinical trials. In CS3 and CS8, ADA were 
collected at baseline (Day 1 pre-dose), Weeks 2, 5, 9 (CS3 only), 13, 25, 37, 53, and during the 
follow-up period (Weeks 4, 8, 13). Subjects who experienced persistent or increasing 
constitutional symptoms were to have additional testing for ADA status.
Subjects were given ‘positive’ ADA status if they had at least one confirmed positive sample at 
any time during the treatment or post-treatment evaluation periods. Subjects were given 
‘negative’ ADA status if all evaluated immunogenicity sample results during the treatment and 
post-treatment evaluation periods were negative, and they had at least one evaluable 
immunogenicity sample postdose. Otherwise, study subjects were given ‘unknown’ ADA status.
In CS3, 24 (36.4%) of the 66 subjects at baseline were positive for anti-olezarsen ADA across 
treatment groups. The placebo group had the highest percentage of subjects positive for ADA at 
baseline (43.5%) compared to the olezarsen 50 mg (38.1%) and olezarsen 80 mg (27.3%) groups. 
Of note, 26 out of the 66 subjects had been previously treated with volanesorsen. Of those 
previously treated with volanesorsen, 14 (53.8%) were positive for ADA at baseline in Trial 
CS3. 
No placebo-treated subjects developed treatment-emergent ADA compared to 38.1% of subjects 
in the olezarsen 50 mg group and 45.5% of subjects in the olezarsen 80 mg group. All subjects 
with induced treatment-emergent ADA had a persistent response. Of the 42 subjects who were 
ADA negative at baseline, 29 (69%) remained negative throughout the study. The median time to 
onset of treatment-emergent ADAs were similar in treatment groups that received olezarsen 
(30.0 days and 29.5 days in the olezarsen 80 mg group and olezarsen 50 mg group, respectively). 
The median time to peak titer was greater in the olezarsen 80 mg group than in the olezarsen 
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 191 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
174
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
50 mg group (218.5 days vs. 29.5 days, respectively). The median (range) peak titer was 200 (50, 
25,600) in the olezarsen 80 mg group, and 75 (50, 25,600) in the olezarsen 50 mg group.
In the OLE study, CS13, treatment-emergent ADAs were detected in 30%, 52.9%, and 88.2% of 
subjects for the CS3-placebo, CS3-olezarsen 50 mg, and CS3-olezarsen 80 mg groups, 
respectively, suggesting the incidence of treatment-emergent ADA may increase with treatment 
duration. At least one ISR was experienced by 26.7% of patients with treatment-emergent ADAs 
in comparison with 6.3% and 12.5% of patients with negative ADA status and treatment-
unaffected ADA, respectively. Hypersensitivity events were only experienced by patients with 
treatment-emergent ADAs (23.3%). Two subjects (CS3 
 and CS3 
) 
experienced serious hypersensitivity events in CS13 and also had high ADA titers. See Section 
7.7.1 for narratives of these subjects.
In Trial CS8, more olezarsen-treated subjects developed treatment-emergent ADA compared to 
the placebo group (olezarsen 50 mg 41.4%; olezarsen 80 mg 40.4%; placebo 12.8%). Of the 125 
subjects that were negative/unknown for ADA at baseline, 84 (67%) remained negative. In 
subjects who developed treatment-emergent ADAs, the median time to onset was 83 days in the 
olezarsen 50 mg group, 29 days in the olezarsen 80 mg group, and 169 days in the placebo 
group. The median duration for treatment-emergent ADA was 298 days, 281, and 84 days, in the 
olezarsen 50 mg, olezarsen 80 mg, and placebo groups, respectively. 
Although there did not seem to be a clear association with ADA and AEs in either Trial CS3 or 
CS8, in the OLE CS13 study, there was a higher proportion of subjects with ADA that had ISRs 
and hypersensitivity events.
Conclusion
Treatment with olezarsen results in development of treatment-emergent ADA in approximately 
40% of treated subjects. The incidence of ADA may increase with longer drug exposure as 
demonstrated in subjects that enrolled in the OLE study, CS13. Review of immunogenicity data 
from events during CS3 and CS8 did not demonstrate a clear relationship between AEs, 
including hypersensitivity events, and ADA status. In CS13, however, ISRs and hypersensitivity 
events were more frequent in subjects with treatment-emergent ADAs compared to subjects with 
ADA negative or treatment-unaffected status, although the numbers were small and therefore 
limit definitive causality assessments. 
8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
Renal Impairment
No dedicated renal impairment study was conducted for olezarsen. The ultimate elimination of 
unconjugated olezarsen is likely a combination of slow nuclease metabolism within tissues and 
subsequent excretion of shortened metabolites (as well as any remaining full-length 
oligonucleotide) into urine.

Clinical studies conducted in this program enrolled participants with normal renal function as 
well as those with mild to moderate renal impairment. The range of post hoc estimates of steady-
state exposure, including Cmax, area under the concentration-time curve (AUC), and Ctrough, were 
overlapping between participants with normal renal function (N=221) and participants with mild 
(N=48) or moderate (N=3) renal impairment (Figure 32). Consistent with PK exposure metrics, 
the range of post hoc estimates of steady-state percent change from baseline for ApoC-III and 
TG were overlapping between participants with normal renal function (N=219) and participants 
with mild (N=48) or moderate (N=3) renal impairment (Figure 33 and Figure 34). 
The review team agrees with the Applicant’s labeling recommendation that no dose adjustment 
is needed in patients with mild or moderate renal impairment (≥30 mL/min).

Race
Japanese origin (from Study AKCEA-CS1) was identified as a statistically significant covariate 
on Vc/F in the population PK analysis. However, there were no clinically meaningful differences 
in olezarsen exposure metrics among categories of race (White, Black or African American, 
Asian, Japanese [from Study AKCEA-CS1], American Indian or Alaska Native, Native 
Hawaiian or Pacific Islander, Other or Unknown), given that exposures are still within the 
overall range of exposures seen in the population. The review team agrees that no dose 
adjustment is warranted on the basis of race.
Age and Sex
Age (adults aged <65 years [N=223], 65 to 74 years [N=41], and ≥75 years [N=8]) and sex were 
not identified as a statistically significant covariate in the population PK analysis. Slight 
differences in median Cmax,ss and AUCtau,ss, are attributable to differences in body weight between 
male (median body weight 86.2 kg) and female participants (median body weight of 70.6 kg) and 
are not considered clinically meaningful, given that exposures are still within the overall range of 
exposures seen in the population. Therefore, no dose adjustment is required on the basis of age 
and sex.
Body Weight
The population PK analysis identified body weight as a statistically significant covariate and as 
the most influential factor on olezarsen PK. Increased body weight was associated with increased 
apparent clearance (CL/F), Q/F, Vc/F, and Vp/F and ka. Compared with a reference subject with 
a body weight of 70 kg, the median olezarsen exposure (AUCtau,ss, Cmax,ss, Ctrough,ss) for a 
participant at the 10th percentile of the body weight distribution, i.e., one with a weight of 61 kg, 
is expected to have a 16%, 28%, and 9% increase, respectively. A participant at the 90th 
percentile of the body weight distribution, i.e., one with a weight of 105 kg, is expected to have a 
35% lower AUCtau,ss, 55% lower Cmax,ss, and 23% lower Ctrough,ss compared with a reference 
subject with weight of 70 kg. It is noted that the differences in Ctrough,ss between participants at 
the 10th and 90th percentiles of the body weight distribution were found to be smaller than the 
differences in Cmax,ss and AUCtau,ss for these participants.
A comparison of individual exposure metrics by body weight quartile was consistent with the 
magnitudes of differences found for the median exposure (Figure 38). While there was a trend of 
declining exposure with increasing body weight for Cmax,ss and AUCtau,ss, no such apparent trend 
was identified for Ctrough,ss. Consistent with the Ctrough,ss, steady-state ApoC-III and TG reductions 
(percent change-from-baseline) were similar across different body weight groups. 
The impact of body weight on olezarsen PK was, therefore, not considered clinically meaningful, 
and a fixed dose of 80 mg monthly is appropriate, regardless of body weight.
Reference ID: 5499859

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Interaction With Highly Plasma Protein-Bound Drugs
No drug-drug interaction was observed in vitro with regard to plasma protein-binding 
displacement between olezarsen and highly plasma protein-bound drugs, warfarin, and ibuprofen 
in vitro, and thus olezarsen is not expected to have drug-drug interactions with highly plasma-
bound drugs.
Metabolism and Transporter-Based Drug-Drug Interaction
Oligonucleotides like olezarsen are not substrates for the classic CYP metabolic pathways and 
thus their PK are unlikely to be impacted by other drugs that are CYP inhibitors or inducers. 
Olezarsen did not inhibit major CYP isoforms (CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, 
CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP2E1, or CYP3A4). Olezarsen up to 10 μM did not induce CYP1A2, 
CYP2B6, or CYP3A4. At 10 and 100 μM, olezarsen was not a substrate or inhibitor, 
respectively, of OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K, bile 
salt export pump (BSEP), breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP), or P-gp. It is unlikely that 
olezarsen would impact the PK of compounds that are substrates of CYPs or transporters.
8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
Olezarsen was granted orphan drug designation for the FCS indication. The Applicant was 
exempt from the Pediatric Research Equity Act requirement. Olezarsen has not been studied in 
pediatric patients. The Applicant has an agreed Pediatric Investigation Plan with the European 
Medicine Agency that is deferred 
 to determine pediatric dosing (EMA 2023).
8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of 
Reproductive Potential
The Applicant conducted a fertility and early embryonic development study in male and female 
mice with olezarsen. The Agency agreed that embryofetal development and pre-/postnatal 
development evaluations conducted with the unconjugated active ASO component of olezarsen 
in mice and/or rabbits were adequate to support a marketing application.
Table 86 summarizes the outcomes and conclusions of these studies. The identified multiples are 
based on a comparison of the BSA at the no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) dose 
converted to a monthly dose relative to the monthly clinical dose of olezarsen, even though for 
some of the studies it was the unconjugated ASO that was administered. Regardless, as the 
unconjugated ASO is the active substance for both test articles administered, the comparison is 
considered acceptable. Refer to Section 13 for further details of the reproductive toxicity studies 
conducted with the conjugated and unconjugated ASO. Specific labelling recommendations will 
be addressed separately.

8.1 Pregnancy
Combined fertility and embryo-fetal development study in mice with the 
unconjugated (lacking GalNAc) ASO.
• Unconjugated ASO administered to male and female mice by 
subcutaneous injection at doses of 3, 10, or 25 mg/kg every other 
day (10.5, 35, and 87.5 mg/kg/wk) prior to mating and through the 
completion of organogenesis on gestation day 15
• No adverse developmental outcomes were noted at doses up to 
87.5 mg/kg/wk (highest dose administered)
• Low concentrations of the unconjugated ASO were measurable in 
placental tissue (<10% of maternal plasma levels) while levels in 
fetal tissue were below the limit of detection
• The NOAEL was 87.5 mg/kg/wk (approximately 21x the MRHD 
based on BSA comparison of the unconjugated ASO)
Embryo-fetal development study in pregnant rabbits with the 
unconjugated ASO.
• Unconjugated ASO administered by subcutaneous injection at 
doses of 3, 6, or 15 mg/kg every other day (10.5, 21, and 52.5 
mg/kg/wk) during the period of organogenesis on gestation days 6 to 
18 
• A maternally toxic dose of 52.5 mg/kg/wk was associated with 
abortions and early delivery but was not associated with adverse 
developmental outcomes 
• Low concentrations of the unconjugated ASO were measurable in 
placental tissue (~7% of maternal plasma levels), whereas levels in 
fetal tissue were below the limit of detection
• The NOAEL was mid dose of 21 mg/kg/wk (approximately 20x the 
MRHD based on BSA comparison of the unconjugated ASO)
Pre- and postnatal study in mice with the unconjugated ASO.
• Unconjugated ASO administered to dams by subcutaneous injection 
at doses of 3, 10, or 25 mg/kg every other day (10.5, 35, and 87.5 
mg/kg/wk) from gestation day 6 through lactation day 21 (weaning) 
• Decreases in offspring body weights were associated with delays in 
the development of morphological landmarks at 87.5 mg/kg/wk
• The maternal NOAEL was the high dose of 87.5 mg/kg/wk 
(approximately 21x the MRHD based on comparison of the active 
substance dose). The NOAEL for effects on offspring was the mid 
dose of 105 mg/kg/wk, (approximately 9x the MRHD based on BSA 
comparison of the unconjugated ASO).
8.2 Lactation
• In milk from lactating mice from the pre- and postnatal toxicity study 
given subcutaneous doses of the unconjugated ASO at 87.5 
mg/kg/wk, very low levels (<0.01% of maternal liver levels) were 
detected.

9. Product Quality
Approval
The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality review team has assessed NDA 218614 with respect to 
chemistry, manufacturing, and controls and has determined that it meets all applicable standards 
to support the identity, strength, quality, and purity that it purports. As such, Office of 
Pharmaceutical Quality recommends approval of this NDA from a quality perspective. As part of 
this action, an expiration period of 36 months for the product, stored under refrigerated condition 
at 2°C to 8°C (36°F to 46°F) in the commercial packaging, is granted.

10. Human Subjects Protections/Clinical Site 
and Other Good Clinical Practice 
Inspections/Financial Disclosure Review
Human Subjects Protections
According to the Applicant, the clinical trials were conducted in substantial conformance with 
International Council for Harmonization good clinical practice requirements and with applicable 
country and/or local statutes and regulations regarding ethical committee review, informed 
consent, and protection of human subjects participating in biomedical research.
Clinical Site Inspections
Two clinical investigator inspections (CIs) were conducted and the Contract Research 
Organization (CRO) Medpace was inspected for the submitted trials. The inspection of one site 
for CS8 found two unreported AEs (redness at the injection site and nausea) in two subjects, both 
in olezarsen 80 mg group. This CI also enrolled three ineligible subjects in CS8, which were 
reported to the FDA as protocol deviations.
Based on the overall inspection results of these CIs, CRO, and the regulatory assessments, the 
data generated by the CIs, managed by the CRO, and submitted by the Applicant are verifiable. 
Trials ISIS 678354-CS3 and ISIS 678354-CS8 appear to have been conducted adequately and 
the clinical data submitted by the Applicant appear acceptable in support of the respective 
indication. See Section 22.

11. Advisory Committee Summary
An advisory committee meeting was not convened for this application. 
The application for the related drug volanesorsen for treatment of FCS was discussed at a 
meeting of the Endocrinologic and Metabolic Drugs Advisory Committee on May 10, 2018. The 
committee voted 12 to 8 in favor of approval. 
The primary safety risk for volanesorsen was severe thrombocytopenia.
Olezarsen has the same nucleotide sequence and backbone chemical composition as 
volanesorsen and differs from it only through its inclusion of triantennary N-acetyl 
galactosamine (GalNAc3), which allows for lower dosing and injection volume than with 
volanesorsen.
The safety and effectiveness concerns with volanesorsen have been largely addressed or resolved 
with the olezarsen product. Olezarsen has a mild signal for thrombocytopenia but not a signal for 
bleeding. Olezarsen 80 mg statistically significantly reduces TG to a clinically meaningful 
degree. In Study CS3, for the total olezarsen group (80 mg + 50 mg groups), a decrease in the 
adjudicated pancreatitis event rate was observed compared with placebo. Safety concerns with 
olezarsen are monitorable and can be addressed in labeling. These issues do not rise to the level 
of requiring advisory committee input.

13.3.1. Pharmacology
Pharmacology
The Applicant conducted both in vitro and in vivo studies to demonstrate pharmacological 
activity with olezarsen. Activity was characterized in vitro using primary hepatocytes (human 
and transgenic mouse) and in vivo activity was demonstrated in wild-type mice, transgenic mice 
expressing human apoC-III, and in NHPs (cynomolgus monkeys). Results from the in vitro 
pharmacology studies demonstrated that olezarsen reduces apoC-III mRNA up to 89% compared 
to untreated controls. Results from the in vivo studies demonstrated dose- and time-dependent 
reductions of liver apoC-III mRNA, plasma ApoC-III protein and plasma TG levels. These 
effects were consistent with the expected pharmacological properties of the compound. Results 
from these animal models showed reasonable proof-of-concept and included a clinically 
translatable endpoint (plasma TG). Key observations from studies demonstrating pharmacologic 
activity are summarized below.
In Vitro Pharmacological Activity of ISIS 678354 in Primary Human Hepatocytes 
In a study (Study No. 20-1d52) in primary human hepatocytes (HepatoPac®), olezarsen produced 
a concentration-dependent reduction of ApoC-III protein with a half-maximal inhibitory 
concentration (IC50) of 0.18 µM and maximal efficacy of 82% knockdown in apoC-III mRNA 
levels after 48 hours of incubation followed by 48 hours of recovery. See Figure 39 below.

Dose Response Evaluation of ISIS 838707 in Wild-Type Mice Fed a Normal Chow Diet 
An in vivo study (Study No. EX/3980_166) was conducted in wild-type CD-1 mice using a 
mouse-specific surrogate ApoC-III ASO (ISIS 838707, a murine GalNac3-conjugated version of 
olezarsen) because olezarsen is not pharmacologically active in mice due to the divergence in 
sequence between mouse and human ApoC-III protein. 
CD-1 mice were injected once weekly by subcutaneous (SC) injection with ISIS 838707 (5 and 
10 mg/kg/week or saline) for 6 weeks. Forty-eight hours following the last treatment, a dose-
related reduction in liver apoC-III mRNA expression and plasma TG levels were noted, with the 
reductions reaching 88% and 76%, respectively, compared to untreated control mice at the 
10 mg/kg/wk dose. See Table 87 below.

Dose Response Evaluation of ISIS 678354 in Human apoC-III Transgenic mice 
An in vivo study (study No. EX/3979_73, 23-144a) was conducted in transgenic mice that 
express human apoC-III gene and have extremely high levels of TG. Human apoC-III transgenic 
mice treated with a single dose of olezarsen at 0.3, 1, 3, and 10 mg/kg showed dose-dependent 
reductions in liver apoC-III mRNA by up to 77% and plasma ApoC-III protein and plasma TG 
with up to 87% reduction in liver apoC-III mRNA and plasma TG at the 10 mg/kg dose (see 
Table 88 below). The median effective dose (ED50) values for liver ApoC-III, plasma ApoC-III, 
and plasma TG reductions were identified as 1.3, 0.8, and 0.9 mg/kg, respectively.

Dose Response Evaluation of ISIS 678354 in 39-Week Study in Lean Cynomolgus Monkeys 
Non-human primates (cynomolgus monkeys) are considered the most relevant nonclinical 
species because the pharmacologically active ASO portion of olezarsen exhibits full 
complementarity to the human apoC-III mRNA transcript sequence. Cynomolgus monkeys were 
administered olezarsen SC at 2, 6, 12, and 30 mg/kg/wk, once weekly for up to 39 weeks in a 
toxicology study (study No. 678354 – AS02) that included recovery assessments following 13- 
or 39-weeks of treatment. Regarding pharmacologic endpoints, there were dose-dependent 
reductions in liver apoC-III mRNA up to 90% and plasma apoC-III mRNA up to 77% compared 
to pre-dose that were maintained for the duration of treatment. A reversal of these effects was 
seen following recovery periods (see Figure 41 and Table 89 below). Minimal effects on plasma 
TG levels were noted since chow-fed cynomolgus monkeys already have very low plasma TG 
levels (<50 mg/dL).

13.3.2. Absorption, Distribution, Metabolism, and 
Excretion/Pharmacokinetics 
The nonclinical pharmacokinetic and toxicokinetic (PK/TK) parameters of olezarsen, including 
absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion, were characterized in the mouse following 
single and repeat SC dosing in 6-, 13-, and 26-week studies, and in monkeys following repeat-
dosing in the 39-week study. 
Olezarsen was quantified in plasma using quantitative hybridization assay coupled with 
electrochemiluminescence (ECL) and in tissues using high-performance liquid chromatography 
with tandem mass spectrometry. In addition, liver and kidney concentrations were measured in 
the chronic mouse and monkey studies and in F0 mice as part of the fertility and early embryonic 
development study.
Absorption 
Across nonclinical species, the PK profiles were qualitatively comparable. Olezarsen rapidly 
enters plasma following SC administration and is then quickly distributed into tissues where it is 
metabolized by nucleases releasing the active ASO.   
In mice, olezarsen was rapidly absorbed (time to maximum concentration [Tmax] = 0.5 to 1 hour). 
After reaching peak levels, plasma concentrations declined rapidly over the first 8 to 12 hours. 
Clearance was multi-phasic with an initial quick decline from plasma followed by a more 
prolonged decline from tissues. The mean residence time ranged from 2 to 5 hours at the doses 
evaluated and was similar between doses at each time point. Mean peak concentration (Cmax) and 
total plasma exposure (AUC0-48h) increase with dose in a greater than proportional manner. 
Plasma drug levels following repeated dosing were higher than after the initial dose 
administration suggesting accumulation. There were no sex differences in exposure. Exposure 
profile curves are shown in Figure 42.
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 215 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
198
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Figure 42. Exposure Profile Curve of ISIS 678354 in Mice
Source: Figure 1 of Applicant’s Pharmacokinetic Written Summary, modified by reviewer to remove Applicant’s figure title
In the monkey, rapid absorption was also seen (Tmax = 1 to 4 hours), followed by a more 
prolonged clearance phase, with a half-life of approximately 30 days following repeated 
administration. Cmax and AUC0-48h increased greater than dose proportionally over the dose 
ranges evaluated, with levels generally 2- to 4-fold higher in antidrug antibody (ADA)-positive 
animals. In ADA-positive animals, plasma trough levels were also higher than in ADA-negative 
animals. There was no apparent accumulation in ADA-negative animals, but there was in ADA-
positive animals, likely resulting from olezarsen binding to ADA in plasma. No sex differences 
in exposure were identified. Exposure profiles are shown in Figure 43 below.

Table 92. Comparison of ISIS 678354 Plasma PK Parameters Between Monkey and Human 
Source: Table 6 of Sponsor’s pharmacokinetic written summary, modified by reviewer to remove Applicant’s table title.
Abbreviations: CL/F, apparent clearance; PK, pharmacokinetics; Tmax, time to maximum plasma concentration
Distribution 
The distribution of olezarsen was evaluated in mice and monkeys. Olezarsen is highly bound to 
plasma proteins (>98%) in the mouse and monkey and was similar to that in human plasma.  The 
mean blood to plasma ratios in human and monkey ranged from 0.488 to 0.98 at concentrations 
up to 300 μg/mL, whereas in mouse, mean blood to plasma ratios was ~1. Distribution to tissues 
in mouse and monkey was responsible for the initial rapid plasma clearance, with the highest 
concentrations present in liver and kidney. In both species, concentrations in the liver were 
higher compared to kidney cortex at the lower doses evaluated, most likely attributed to more 
efficient uptake of GalNAcs by the asyloglycoprotein receptor  in the liver. The concentration in 
the kidney cortex increased approximately dose-proportionally, whereas in the liver, it increased 
less than dose-proportionally, likely due to saturation of asyloglycoprotein receptor-mediated 
hepatocyte uptake at the higher doses evaluated in both species (12 and 20 mg/kg/wk in mouse 
and monkey, respectively) which limited further uptake into the liver. The saturation occurred at 
high multiples of the proposed clinical dose (80 mg/month, or 0.285 mg/kg/wk). Near steady-
state tissue exposure levels were achieved after 13 weeks of once-weekly dosing in both mouse 
and monkey. Minimal levels were reported in both tissues following recovery periods, consistent 
with terminal tissue half-lives of approximately 4 weeks in both species. Data are summarized in 
Table 93 (mice) and Table 94 (monkey) below.
Reference ID: 5499859

Table 94. Tissue Concentrations of Unconjugated ISIS 678354 in Monkeys Following 39 Weeks of

Source: Table 9 of Applicant’s pharmacokinetic written summary, modified by reviewer to remove Applicant’s table title
In monkeys, there were no notable differences in tissue concentrations between ADA-positive 
and ADA-negative animals.
Metabolism
Across species, the metabolism of olezarsen begins with an initial hydrolysis of the GalNAc3 to 
form the unconjugated ASO in tissues. The unconjugated ASO is metabolized in tissues by 
intracellular nucleases to produce chain-shortened oligonucleotide metabolites, consistent with 
endonuclease cleavage followed by sequential exonuclease cleavage of deoxyphosphorothioate 
linkages.
In mouse and monkey plasma, fully conjugated olezarsen was the major circulating species 
within the first 24 hours postdose, accounting for 69% to 93% of the detected analytes. N-1 to N-
15 chain-shortened metabolites and unconjugated drug accounted for the remaining analytes.  
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 219 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
202
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Prior to subsequent dosing, unconjugated drug accounted for the nearly total abundance of 
analyte detected.
In the liver and kidney of mouse and monkey, full-length oligonucleotide primarily associated 
with unconjugated olezarsen was the most abundant species (>80%), with multiple chain 
shortened oligonucleotide metabolites (N1-N15) accounting for anywhere from <1% to <7% of 
the total peak area in mice, and <1% to <2% in monkeys. In urine, unconjugated drug was the 
major component.
Eight linker-related metabolites (M5-M12) were detected in mouse plasma and 10 metabolites 
(M4-M12 and M14) in monkey plasma, with highest levels typically seen 2 hours after dosing. 
Concentrations were <6% of parent levels. Levels were decreased up to 10-fold further by 24 
hours and were inconsistently able to be detected prior to subsequent dose administration. In 
mouse urine, linker metabolites were predominantly excreted in the first 24 hours.  
Human plasma was collected and analyzed for fully conjugated drug and metabolites at pre-dose 
and/or 2 hours postdose at various study days in clinical Trial ISIS 678354-CS1 and reported in 
study 678354-IS13. Full length drug was the only analyte detected, accounting for 100% of the 
peak area in all samples. In human urine collected in clinical Trial ISIS 678354-CS1 and 
reported in study 678354-IS0999, unconjugated olezarsen (i.e., volanesorsen) is the most 
abundant component, while various chain-shortened metabolites accounted for the remainder. 
Overall, the metabolite profile in plasma and urine was similar between nonclinical species 
(mouse and monkeys) and humans and there were no unique metabolites identified in humans 
compared to animal species. 
Excretion
Excretion of the unconjugated olezarsen and its metabolites occur predominantly through urine, 
and to a lesser extent through feces.
In mouse urine, full-length olezarsen represented <1% of the administered dose during the initial 
48 hours following administration; the major component was unconjugated olezarsen. In human 
urine, unchanged fully conjugated olezarsen was the major oligonucleotide component, with 
several oligonucleotide metabolites associated with olezarsen 3′-deletion that still retained some 
of the linker component attached, as well as chain-shortened oligonucleotides from unconjugated 
olezarsen were detected.
Anti-Drug Antibodies
ADA levels were measured in the chronic monkey study. ADA were detected in animals from all 
treated groups (30-64%) and controls (22%). The plasma trough levels of olezarsen were 
increased approximately up to 6-fold in ADA+ animals as compared to that reported in ADA- 
animals following 3 months or more treatment, suggesting olezarsen binding to ADA in plasma. 
However, there were no notable differences in tissue levels of drug between ADA+ and ADA- 
animals. Furthermore, the presence of ADAs had no significant effects on the toxicity profile or 
pharmacologic endpoints (hepatic apoC-III mRNA levels).
Reference ID: 5499859

Toxicokinetics
Toxicokinetic data and/or tissue drug levels were collected as a component of various repeat-
dose toxicity studies. Descriptions of pertinent TK data are summarized with the results of 
individual toxicology studies (refer to Section 13.3.3).
13.3.3. Toxicology
The toxicology of olezarsen has been characterized in repeat-dose toxicity studies, a fertility and 
early embryonic developmental study, a battery of in vitro and in vivo genotoxicity studies, and 
an impurity qualification study. The toxicity of the active, unconjugated ASO (volanesorsen) on 
embryo-fetal development, pre-postnatal development, and its carcinogenic potential were 
previously assessed in support of the marketing application for that product. In agreement with 
the Agency, these studies with the unconjugated ASO were considered supportive of the current 
application.
General Toxicology
The general toxicity of olezarsen has been characterized in repeat-dose toxicity studies in CD-1 
mice and cynomolgus monkeys in studies of up to 26- and 39-weeks duration, respectively, with 
once-weekly SC injections. These studies included 13- and 26-week recovery periods in the 
mouse and monkey, respectively. The no observed adverse effect levels (NOAELs), basis for the 
NOAELs, and the safety margins based on body surface area (BSA) are captured in Table 95 
below. The summary table is followed by more detailed reviews of the pivotal toxicology studies 
conducted with olezarsen that support this application.
Drug-related effects consistent with the expected toxicities of an ASO with a phosphorothioate 
backbone were evident in the liver, kidney, and spleen. Tissue effects at the injection site were 
also observed. The most notable effect was thrombocytopenia, observed in both the mouse and 
NHP.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Key Study Findings 
•
Increases in aspartate aminotransferase (AST) (up to ~ 3-fold), alanine aminotransferase 
(ALT) (up to ~7x) at 20 mg/kg/wk dose in both sexes compared to controls. These effects 
were reversible.
•
Increase in alkaline phosphatase (up to ~2-fold compared to control) in males at 20 
mg/kg/wk that resolved during recovery.
•
Reversible increase in monocyte chemoattractant protein-1 (MCP-1) (~8-fold compared to 
control) in both sexes at 20 mg/kg/wk, which suggests an inflammatory response because 
MCP-1 is a potent inflammatory cytokine. 
•
Immunoglobulin M (IgM) decreased in males at ≥6 mg/kg/wk (up to 40% lower) and in 
females at ≥2 mg/kg/wk (up to 58%).
•
Dose-related increased absolute and relative weights for liver (1.2 to 1.6-fold) and spleen (1.6 
to 1.9-fold over control) at ≥6 mg/kg/wk in both sexes compared to controls, which 
correlated with microscopic findings in the liver (hepatocellular hypertrophy, cytoplasmic 
alteration, karyomegaly, increased mitotic figures and vacuolated/granular macrophages) and 
spleen (red pulp cellularity), respectively.
•
Decreased absolute and relative thymus weights (-41% to -45% lower) at 20 mg/kg/wk in 
both sexes, which correlated with increased generalized lymphoid depletion in all animals 
administered 20 mg/kg/wk.
•
The absolute and relative liver weight increase (1.1 to 1.2-fold); spleen weight increase (1.4 
to 1.5-fold) and thymus weight decrease (-30 to -59%) and microscopic findings persisted at 
20 mg/kg/wk during recovery.
•
Increased absolute and relative kidney weight (~1.2-fold), cytoplasmic basophilic granules in 
the tubular epithelial cells and minimal tubular vacuolation.
•
Decreases in hepatic apoC-III mRNA expression (~ -88%), plasma TG (~ -49% lower, 
relative to control), and mild increases in MCP-1 (~2-fold over control) with the ISIS 838707 
(the mouse specific inhibitor).
•
Tissue concentration (µg/g) of unconjugated olezarsen following multiple SC administration 
(48 hours after last dose) with recovery demonstrated dose-dependent increases in the liver 
and kidney, with more than dose-proportional increase in the kidney, and low concentrations 
in reproductive organs compared to kidney and liver in CD-1 mice.
The no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) for the 26-week mouse study was 2 mg/kg/wk, 
based on the dose-related pattern, incidence, and severity of the microscopic findings in the liver 
and spleen, which correlated with increased absolute and relative organ weights at ≥6 mg/kg/wk 
and minimal to marked increases in liver enzymes, resulting in a 0.5x safety margin based on 
BSA at the maximum recommended human dose of 80 mg/month. The pharmtox reviewer’s 
identified NOAEL differs from that determined by the Applicant. The Applicant considered only 
liver, spleen, and thymus effects (organ weight changes and microscopic findings) at 20 
mg/kg/wk to be adverse because they persisted through recovery and identified 6 mg/kg/wk as 
the NOAEL.

Dose Justification
The dosages used in the 26-week study were based on results from the 13-week study conducted 
in CD-1 mice administered 0, 2, 6, or 24 mg/kg once weekly by SC administration. The key 
finding from this study pertinent to dosage selection was a 25% reduction in platelet count 
(thrombocytopenia) in all 24 mg/kg/wk females which was reversible. Thrombocytopenia was 
also seen with the unconjugated ASO (volanesorsen).

The primary observations from the 26-week mouse study and tissue concentrations of the drug 
are summarized in Table 97 and Table 98.

39-Week Repeat Dose Toxicity Study of ISIS 678354 in Monkey With a 13-Week Interim 
Necropsy and a 26-Week Recovery Period (Study No. 678354- AS02)
SC dosages of 2, 6, and 12 mg/kg were administered for 39 weeks followed by a 26-week 
recovery period at 12 mg/kg. These groups also included animals terminated at 13 weeks. 
Additionally, a dosage of 30 mg/kg was administered for 13 weeks; this group also included a 
26-week recovery period.
Key Study Findings 
•
A single male administered 12 mg/kg/wk had very low platelet counts on both Day 65 and at 
the interim termination at Week 13.
•
Increases in glutamate dehydrogenase (up to 5-fold baseline), ALT (4-fold baseline), AST 
(1.6x baseline) were evident at >6 mg/kg/wk. Decreases in albumin (~13-30% lower) in both 
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 225 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
208
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
sexes at ≥6 mg/kg/wk and A:G ratio (~40% lower), and creatinine were also seen, primarily 
at dosages ≥12 mg/kg/wk. Liver enzyme increases correlated with microscopic findings in 
the liver. These effects on serum chemistry parameters were reversible in the recovery group.
•
At dosages ≥12 mg/kg, basophilic granules were found in the liver (in hepatocytes and 
Kupffer cells; associated with Kupffer cell hypertrophy), kidney, lymph nodes, and multiple 
other tissues. Additional findings were noted in the liver (glycogen vacuolation), kidney 
(tubular cell vacuolation), and injection site (mononuclear cell infiltration) was observed at 
the injection site. There were no microscopic correlates to the heavier spleen. Basophilic 
granules were still present in multiple tissues following recovery.
•
Dose-related reduction in hepatic apoC-III mRNA levels and plasma protein levels and 
plasma TG levels were evident. Both showed reversal following the recovery period.
•
Plasma exposure increased with dose in a greater than proportional manner, there were no 
sex differences, and there was minimal accumulation. A similar pattern was seen in liver and 
kidney tissues. The half-life in both plasma and tissues ranged from 20 to 30 days.
•
ADA-positive animals were identified in all groups. The overall incidence of animals testing 
positive for ADA’s ranged from approximately 30% to 64% in those administered olezarsen 
for 39 weeks while the incidence in controls was 22%. Following recovery, 50% of animals 
administered 12 mg/kg/wk and 75% of those administered 30 mg/kg/wk remained ADA-
positive.
•
Olezarsen was rapidly absorbed from plasma with Tmax occurring by 4 hours after dose 
administration, exposure increased with dose in a greater than proportional manner. The non-
linearity was attributed to higher clearance at the lower dose levels as compared to that at the 
higher dosages administered. There was no sex difference in exposure and minimal 
accumulation.
•
The plasma terminal elimination half-life (t1/2) was estimated to be approximately 30 days 
based on data generated at 12 and 30 mg/kg/wk supporting the once-monthly dosing 
regimen.
•
Following absorption from plasma, rapid distribution to tissues (liver and kidney), with less 
than 2-fold difference in levels between the tissues (liver had more than kidney). 
•
The calculated t1/2 following 13 and 39 weeks of treatment in kidney tissue was 
approximately 30 days and in liver was 21 to 29 days.
•
Hepatic apoC-III mRNA and ApoC-III protein expression in plasma were reduced in a dose-
dependent manner, up to 90% after up to 39 weeks of treatment at doses ≥2 mg/kg/wk.
The NOAEL was identified as 6 mg/kg/wk, based on the thrombocytopenia observed in a single 
animal at 12 mg/kg/wk and the increased incidence/severity of microscopic alterations in liver 
and kidney that were not completely resolved following the recovery period at dosages ≥12 
mg/kg/wk. This NOAEL of 6 mg/kg/wk gives a 6x safety margin based on BSA relative to 
maximum recommended human dose of 80 mg/month. The lowest observable effect level of 12 
mg/kg/wk at which thrombocytopenia was observed gives a 12x safety margin.

Additional Comments
Thrombocytopenia was a drug-related effect seen with olezarsen in a single male administered 
12 mg/kg/wk on Days 65 and Day 93. In comparison, thrombocytopenia occurred in multiple 
animals at comparable mg/kg dosage levels that correlated with clinical signs of severe bruising 
and petechia leading to early terminations and/or dosing holidays in studies with the active 
unconjugated ASO volanesorsen.

multiple tissues following recovery

Carcinogenicity
Carcinogenicity studies were not conducted for olezarsen. However, carcinogenicity studies were 
conducted with the active unconjugated ASO volanesorsen. 
Two-year carcinogenicity studies were conducted with volanesorsen in CD-1 mice (study no. 
304801-AS15) and Sprague-Dawley rats (study no. 304801-AS16) to assess its carcinogenic 
potential. Results from these studies are summarized below. 
In the 2-year mouse study with volanesorsen, dosages of 6, 25, and 40 mg/kg/wk were 
administered SC. There was reduced survival attributed to cardiomyocyte degeneration 
associated with inflammatory cell infiltrates at dosages >25 mg/kg/wk leading to early 
termination of these groups (Day 625, males at 25 mg/kg/wk; Day 617, females at 25 mg/kg/wk; 
Day 507, males at 40 mg/kg/wk and Day 487, females 40 mg/kg/wk). Drug-related neoplastic 
observations were noted at all dosages evaluated. In the liver of male mice, hepatocellular 
adenomas/carcinomas were increased at dosages >25 mg/kg/wk, and multicentric 
hemangiomas/hemangiosarcomas were increased at all doses. In female mice, multicentric 
histiocytic sarcomas were increased at all doses (including in the group dosed with the mouse- 
specific oligonucleotide), and pituitary gland adenomas were increased at 25 mg/kg/wk. The 
liver adenomas, histiocytic sarcomas, and hemangiosarcomas likely reflect the pro-inflammatory 
properties of the phosphorothioate ASO drug class and the greater sensitivity of rodents to these 
effects. Pituitary tumors are most likely a reflection of the high background rate of hyperplasia in 
this species, which is also a relatively common background lesion in aging mice. Thus, although 
the increased incidences of these tumors are drug-related, the relevance to the clinical population 
is likely to be limited. As a result of the reduction in survival at the mid dose/high dose (HD), 
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 230 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
213
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
dose-related increased incidences of cardiovascular effects, and neoplastic effects at all dosages, 
a NOAEL for the induction of neoplasia was not identified.
In the 2-year rat study with volanesorsen, dosages of 0.2, 1, and 5 mg/kg/wk were administered. 
Survival was reduced in males at 5 mg/kg/wk due to either masses at injection sites or chronic 
progressive nephropathy. Drug-related neoplasms observed were increases in the incidences of 
malignant fibrous histiocytomas (MFH) in the skin/subcutis of the injection sites at dosages >1 
mg/kg/wk in both sexes. The injection site MFH neoplasms with volanesorsen treatment in rats 
may be attributable to chronic inflammatory reactions associated with repeated SC injections into 
a limited number of sites and is not likely to be clinically relevant. The NOAEL for the induction 
of neoplasms in the 2-year rat study is considered the low dose (LD) of 0.2 mg/kg/wk (0.1x the 
maximum recommended human dose based on BSA comparison of administered dose) based on 
the increased incidence of MFH at ≥1 mg/kg/wk.
In summary, the carcinogenicity studies identified increased incidences of neoplasms in both 
mice and rats. The majority of these findings were related to the increased sensitivity of these 
species to the proinflammatory effects of phosphorothioate oligonucleotides and/or the method 
of administration. As such, the relevance of these findings to the clinical population is limited. 
Hepatocellular carcinomas, malignant fibrous histiocytomas, hemangiosarcomas, and histiocytic 
sarcomas have also been reported in carcinogenicity studies with other ASOs. Thus, the presence 
of these tumors with the unconjugated ASO provides further support that these neoplasms may 
be a class effect of the phosphorothioate oligonucleotides.
Reproductive Toxicology
A GLP-compliant fertility and early embryonic developmental study was conducted for 
olezarsen in CD-1 mice (study no. 678354-AS08) because of decreased prostate/seminal vesicle 
weight and decreased sperm count from the combined fertility and embryo-fetal development 
mouse study with volanesorsen. Studies evaluating embryofetal development and pre-/postnatal 
development were not conducted with olezarsen as there were no effects on parameters evaluated 
in these studies conducted with the unconjugated ASO volanesorsen. The NOAELs from the 
reproductive toxicology studies conducted with olezarsen and volanesorsen are summarized in 
Table 104 below.
In Table 104, safety multiples are estimated between the study NOAELs and the clinical dose, 
based on BSA comparisons. For the estimates, the weekly BSA dose of olezarsen or 
volanesorsen were multiplied by 2 and 4, respectively, to equate to the once monthly clinical 
dosing regimen of olezarsen compared to more frequent dosing in animal studies.

Summary: The potential effect of olezarsen on fertility and early embryonic development was 
evaluated in CD-1 mice. No adverse effects of olezarsen were observed for any parameter, 
including effects on mating, fertility, and early embryonic development. Consequently, the 
NOAEL for effects on fertility and early embryonic development was considered the HD of 20 
mg/kg.

Reproductive Toxicology Studies With the Active Unconjugated ASO (Volanesorsen) 
and/or ISIS 440670
A battery of GLP-compliant reproductive and developmental toxicity studies was conducted to 
assess the potential effects for the active unconjugated ASO (volanesorsen).    
Combination Fertility and Embryofetal Development Study in Mice
In a combined fertility and embryofetal development study in CD-1 mice (study number 304801-
AS07), volanesorsen was administered SC at dosages of 3, 10, or 25 mg/kg once every other day 
(equivalent to 10.5, 35, or 87.5 mg/kg/wk). Males were treated for 4 weeks prior to cohabitation 
with treated females and continued to be treated for a total of 10 weeks; females were treated for 
2 weeks prior to cohabitation and continued treatment until gestational day 15/16. An additional 
group was administered a murine specific version of this ASO (ISIS 440670) at a dose of 3 
mg/kg every other day. In males, treatment with volanesorsen did not result in any adverse 
effects on fertility following 4 weeks of treatment. However, following 10 weeks of treatment 
there was a reduction in sperm count (33% lower) and decreased prostate/seminal vesicle 
weights (↓ ~20%) at 87.5 mg/kg/wk. It is not known if there would have been an effect on 
fertility endpoints following a longer treatment period. Other effects observed included slight 
increases in monocytes (up to 4-fold), decreased platelets (reduced up to 32%), and increased 
ALT (up to 10-fold) and AST (up to 6-fold) in both sexes at 25 mg/kg/wk; the increase in liver 
enzymes was associated with increased liver weights. Increased spleen weights (up to 1.7-fold) 
were also evident at this dose.
Reference ID: 5499859

===== PAGE 234 =====
NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
217
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
In females, no effects on fertility endpoints were noted. Slight increases in the litter incidences of 
rib variations were noted at the HD, but the increases were not statistically significant. No effects 
were reported for ISIS 440670. The identified NOAEL for male and female fertility and embryo-
fetal development was 87.5 mg/kg/wk. 
A combined fertility and developmental study in CD-1 mice with ISIS 440670, an antisense 
inhibitor of the mouse apoC-III mRNA (study number 304801-AS08) administered SC every 
other day at dosages of 0, 10, or 25 mg/kg (equivalent to 0, 35, or 87.5 mg/kg/wk), effects were 
limited to decreases in platelet counts (reduced up to 22% at 25 mg/kg), decreased TG levels 
(reduced up to 67% at 25 mg/kg), and reductions in hepatic apoC-III mRNA (reduced up to 36% 
at 25 mg/kg) were observed in both sexes at both dosages. The latter two effects were related to 
the pharmacology of the compound. Postmortem examination revealed increases in spleen 
weights (up to 1.6-fold), increased kidney weights (up to 1.2-fold, and liver weights (up to 1.3-
fold) at the 25 mg/kg dose. As with volanesorsen, slight increases in the incidence of rib 
variations were seen at both dose levels that did not reach statistical significance. There were no 
effects on fertility endpoints. Overall, under the conditions of the experiment, ISIS 440670 was 
well-tolerated up to 25 mg/kg (87.5 mg/kg/wk) following 10 weeks of treatment in males and 4 
to 6 weeks of treatment in females. No adverse effects on male or female fertility or embryofetal 
development were observed at up to the top dose of 25 mg/kg (87.5 mg/kg/wk).
Embryofetal Development Study in the Rabbit
In an embryofetal development study, rabbits (study no. 304801-AS09) were administered 
volanesorsen SC at dosages of 3, 6, 15 mg/kg once every other day (equivalent to 10.5, 21, or 
52.5 mg/kg/wk). Reduced feed consumption (18% lower during treatment period) with 
associated clinical observations of reduced/absent feces and reduced weight gain during the 
treatment (47% lower) and post-treatment (33% lower) periods in maternal animals at 52.5 
mg/kg/wk. Postmortem examination revealed tan discoloration of the liver at 52.5 mg/kg/wk and 
increased weights of the kidney, liver, and spleen at all dosages (ranging from ~5% to 26%, 
although the effects were considered significant only at ≥21 mg/kg/wk. Abortions/delivery of 
non-viable fetuses were evident at 52.5 mg/kg/wk. At this dose, fetal weights were slightly lower 
(decreased up to 14%).  There were no effects on gross, visceral, or skeletal development. The 
identified maternal NOAEL was 21 mg/kg/wk based on the reduced feed consumption, reduced 
weight gain, and abortion/reduced viable fetuses at 52.5 mg/kg/wk.
Pre- and Postnatal Toxicity Study in Mice
In a pre-/postnatal study, female CD-1 mice (study no. 304801-AS17) were administered 
volanesorsen SC at dosages of 3, 10 or 25 mg/kg every other day (equivalent to 10.5, 35, or 87.5 
mg/kg/wk) during gestation followed by once weekly administration from parturition through 
postpartum day 21. An additional group was administered ISIS 440670 at 87.5 mg/kg/wk. 
Minimal effects were seen on maternal animals (body weights <6% lower; kidney, liver, and 
spleen weights increased <2-fold). The offspring at 87.5 mg/kg/wk weighed up to 15% less than 
concurrent controls and weights remained lower throughout the lifespan which contributed to 
delays in the age offspring reached various developmental landmarks. Delays of up to ~3 days 
occurred in the appearance of evidence of sexual maturity in females at all dosages of 
volanesorsen and with ISIS 440670. There were no effects on reproductive performance of F1 
animals. The weights of various organs were also reduced in the F1 generation animals, but these

effects are considered secondary to the lighter body weights. The HD of 87.5 mg/kg/wk was

function or the ability to raise a litter. For the F1 generation, 35 mg/kg/wk was considered the 
NOAEL based on the reduced weights observed throughout their lifespan at 87.5 mg/kg/wk.
13.3.4. Other Studies
Impurities/Degradants
Impurities were qualified in a 13-week impurity qualification study in mice and the 39-week 
repeat-dose toxicity study in monkeys.  
13-Week Repeat-Dose Toxicity and Impurity Qualification Study in ICR Mice (Study No. 
678354-AS06)
Olezarsen impurities were qualified in a 13-week repeat-dose toxicity study in Institute for 
Cancer Research mice in which the toxicity profile of two batches of olezarsen that had enriched 
impurity levels were compared to a representative batch of olezarsen that had not been enriched 
with impurities. The treatment groups (10/sex/group) received weekly SC doses of test article. 
Two dose levels (2.33 and 7 mg/kg/wk) of each formulation were administered. Animals in the 
olezarsen control group were dosed with test article prepared from drug substance CA678354-
002 (purity, 91.3%) which was also used in Clinical Trial 3 (CS3). A vehicle control group was 
administered saline. Animals were terminated on Day 87. Mortality, clinical signs, body weight, 
food consumption, ophthalmic examinations, clinical pathology, and microscopic findings were 
evaluated to assess potential impurity-related toxicity.
Key Study Findings
•
Target organs included liver, spleen, and injection site for all drug-treated groups. 
•
Microscopic findings were evident in the liver (single cell necrosis, mononuclear cell 
infiltration, nuclear degeneration/inclusions, cytomegaly / karyomegaly), spleen 
(extramedullary hematopoiesis), and injection site (mononuclear cell infiltration and 
basophilic granules). These effects were correlated with elevated liver enzymes (increases in 
ALT/AST of up to approximately 2-fold) and/or increase organ weights.  
•
In general, the incidences and severities were comparable between groups at the same dose, 
although the incidence and severity of single cell necrosis in the liver and extramedullary 
hematopoiesis in the spleen were slightly higher in the groups administered impurity-
enriched olezarsen relative to that seen with olezarsen. 
•
Liver drug levels were comparable between all three test articles.
The drug-related effects observed in groups administered olezarsen with enhanced levels of 
impurities was considered similar to that observed in the group administered olezarsen without 
enhanced levels of impurities at each dose. Overall, there were no meaningful differences in the 
severity or incidence of the various findings at the evaluated dose levels. In conclusion, enhanced 
levels of impurities did not affect the established toxicity profile of olezarsen.

14. Clinical Pharmacology
14.1. In Vitro Studies
Plasma Protein Binding
In Vitro Evaluation of the Extent of Plasma Protein Binding of ISIS 678354 in Human, 
Monkey, and Mouse Plasma by Ultrafiltration (Trial 678354-IS04)
The plasma protein binding of olezarsen was evaluated by an ultrafiltration method. Olezarsen at 
two nominal concentrations (5 and 150 μg/mL) in human, monkey, and mouse plasma was 
preincubated at 37°C for approximately 30 minutes without adjusting pH (pH in whole plasma 
ranged from 7.3 to 7.5) prior to ultra-centrifugation. The concentrations in plasma and 
ultrafiltrate were determined using a nuclease-dependent hybridization enzyme-linked 
immunosorbent assay (ELISA). Olezarsen is found to be highly bound to human plasma proteins 
(>99% bound) at concentrations of 5 and 150 μg/mL. The extent of plasma protein binding was 
similar across all species and between the two concentrations tested and determined to be ≥98%.
Distribution in Blood Cells
In Vitro Evaluation of Whole Blood and Plasma Concentration Ratio of ISIS 678354 
(Olezarsen) in Human, Monkey, and Mouse (Trial 678354-IS11)
The blood to plasma distribution ratio of olezarsen was evaluated neat and at six different 
nominal concentrations (0, 0.01, 0.03, 0.1, 0.3, 1, and 3 μg/mL) in human whole blood, neat and 
at eight different nominal concentrations (0, 0.1, 0.3, 1, 3, 10, 30, 100, and 300 μg/mL) in 
monkey whole blood, and at seven different nominal concentrations (0, 0.1, 0.3, 1.00, 3.00, 10.0, 
and 30.0 μg/mL) in mouse whole blood. The blood to plasma ratio for olezarsen ranged from 
0.73 to 0.98, 0.49 to 0.64, and 0.83 to 0.98 at concentrations tested in the human, monkey, and 
mice, respectively, suggesting that olezarsen distributes mostly in the plasma compartment.
14.1.1. Drug-Drug Interaction Studies
Study Related to Plasma Protein Binding Interactions
In Vitro Evaluation of Human Plasma Protein Binding of ISIS 678354 and its Interactions 
with Warfarin and Ibuprofen by Ultrafiltration (Trial 678354-IS06)
Warfarin and ibuprofen were selected for the evaluation of plasma protein-binding displacement 
with olezarsen because they are highly bound to albumin binding site I and site II, respectively. 
The substrate and displacer concentrations tested in this trial were 1 and 10 μg/mL for olezarsen, 
3 and 30 μg/mL for warfarin, and 30 and 300 μg/mL for ibuprofen. The concentrations of

olezarsen in plasma and ultrafiltrate were determined using a hybridization ECL assay, and 
concentrations of warfarin and ibuprofen were determined by high-performance liquid 
chromatography with tandem mass spectrometry.
The extent of plasma protein binding of olezarsen was similar with and without the presence of 
warfarin or ibuprofen, and the protein binding of warfarin and ibuprofen was similar with and 
without the presence of olezarsen. The percent displacement of olezarsen (as a substrate) by 
warfarin and ibuprofen (as displacers) or the percent displacement of warfarin or ibuprofen (as 
substrates) by olezarsen (as a displacer) was found to be negligible (<1%). These data suggest 
that no plasma protein-binding displacement is expected between olezarsen and other highly 
plasma protein-bound drugs.
Trials Related to Potential Involvement of Cytochrome P450 Isoforms
Cellular Uptake and In Vitro Evaluation of ISIS 678354 as Inhibitors of Cytochrome P450 
Expression in Human Hepatocytes (Trial 678354-IS10)
Cytochrome P450 (CYP) inhibition study was performed by preincubating olezarsen for 30 
minutes at concentrations ranging from 0.1 to 50 µM in suspensions of cryopreserved cultured 
primary human hepatocytes, followed by the addition of a probe substrate for each CYP enzyme 
to initiate the metabolic reaction for 45 minutes for 1A2, 2B6, 2C9, 2D6, 3A4, and 90 minutes 
for 2C8, 2C19, 2E1.
Evaluations of the cellular uptake of olezarsen in cryopreserved cultured human primary 
hepatocytes was conducted with olezarsen at concentrations of 1, 10, and 50 μM and an 
incubation time up to 120 minutes, where concentrations of olezarsen in cell pellets were 
determined using an LC-MS/MS method.
The results indicate that olezarsen up to 50 μM does not inhibit the activities of CYP1A2, 
CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP2E1, or CYP3A4. Under the same 
experimental conditions, known inhibitors of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, 
CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP2E1, and CYP3A4 showed expected inhibition of enzyme activities, 
demonstrating that the hepatocyte inhibition experiment was appropriately performed.
Hepatocyte uptake of olezarsen occurred throughout the 120-minute incubation period, with an 
average intracellular concentration of 78.1 µM after 60-minute incubation and 72.8 µM after 
120-minute incubation at 50 µM of olezarsen in the incubation medium. These intracellular 
concentrations associated with hepatocytes are approximately 10-fold higher than the estimated 
steady-state level in the liver at the clinical dose of 80 mg once Q4W.
Taken together, results from this study demonstrate the lack of potential for olezarsen to inhibit 
CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP2E1, and CYP3A4.
In Vitro Assessment of Cytochrome P450 Induction Potential of ISIS 678354 in Plated 
Cryopreserved Human Primary Hepatocytes (Trial 678354-IS07)
The potential for olezarsen to act as an inducer of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A4 was 
evaluated in cryopreserved primary human hepatocytes using both the enzyme activities and 
corresponding mRNA levels. Human cryopreserved primary hepatocytes were treated with 
olezarsen at a concentration range of 0.1 to 50 μM for 48 hours.

The results of the study demonstrated that olezarsen did not cause induction of CYP1A2,

Trial Related to Potential Involvement of Major Drug Transporters
Assessment of ISIS 678354 as an Inhibitor or Substrate of Human Organic Anion 
Transporter (OAT)1, OAT3, Organic Cation Transporter (OCT)1, OCT2, Organic Anion 
Transporting Polypeptide (OATP)1B1, OATP1B3, Multidrug and Toxin Extrusion Protein 
(MATE)1, MATE2-K, Breast Cancer Resistance Protein (BCRP), P-glycoprotein (P-gp), 
and Bile Salt Export Pump (BSEP)-Mediated Transport (Trial 678354-IS08)
The potential for olezarsen to act as a substrate or inhibitor of human OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, 
OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K, BSEP, BCRP, P-gp was evaluated at 10 μM 
and 100 μM of olezarsen, respectively. The transport of each probe substrate was determined by 
radiometric detection, while concentrations of olezarsen were determined using an LC-MS/MS 
method.
At a concentration of 100 μM of olezarsen, the mean percent inhibition ranged from −31.1% to 
45.3% for all the transporters evaluated. Therefore, olezarsen is not considered an inhibitor of 
OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K, BSEP, BCRP, or P-
gp. In the substrate assays at an olezarsen concentration of 10 μM, less than a 2-fold difference 
of uptake was observed in transporter-transfected cells compared with control cells for OAT1, 
OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K, and BSEP, suggesting that 
olezarsen is not a substrate for these transporters. For BCRP and P-gp, the permeability value 
and the efflux ratio were not determinable due to olezarsen concentrations in all receiver wells 
being below the lower limit of quantitation (LLOQ). However, given the extremely low B→A 
permeability (<0.0287 × 10-6 cm/s), olezarsen does not appear to be a substrate for BCRP or P-
gp.
Taken together, results from this study demonstrate that olezarsen is not a substrate or inhibitor 
of OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K, BCRP, P-gp, and 
BSEP.
